### [1] [Microwave Power-to-Frequency Transduction in a Magnetically Initiated Rydberg Dissipative Time Crystal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31824)
*Darmindra Arumugam*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: A room-temperature Rydberg dissipative time crystal in 87Rb vapor converts microwave amplitude into a continuous shift of its self-sustained ~21 kHz oscillation frequency, providing a nonlinear, high-responsivity RF-to-frequency transduction mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional RF sensing relies on externally engineered feedback oscillators, repeated optical spectral scans, or resolved Autler-Townes splitting, which add complexity and limit continuous frequency-domain operation. The authors seek an intrinsic many-body atomic route to amplitude-to-frequency conversion that avoids these requirements.

Method: A ~14 G magnetic field initiates a self-sustained dissipative time crystal in a room-temperature 87Rb vapor near 21 kHz. A near-resonant 8.325 GHz microwave field couples the 63D3/2 to 64P3/2 Rydberg manifold, continuously shifting the autonomous oscillation frequency. The measured field-to-frequency transfer function is compared with a self-consistent mean-field model in which microwave-driven Rydberg-population redistribution modifies the collective resonance condition; higher DTC harmonics are also characterized.

Result: The transfer function is strongly nonlinear, with a total fundamental-frequency excursion of ~6.7 kHz and peak responsivity approaching 1 kHz/(mV/cm), substantially steeper than a simple quadratic saturation response. Higher DTC harmonics preserve the same normalized transfer function while exhibiting approximately linear growth of absolute responsivity with harmonic order.

Conclusion: The work establishes an intrinsic many-body atomic route to RF amplitude-to-frequency conversion, enabling continuous frequency-domain sensing via an emergent atomic frequency without an externally engineered RF feedback oscillator, repeated optical spectral scans, or resolved Autler-Townes splitting.

Abstract: Microwave power-to-frequency transduction is demonstrated using the emergent oscillation frequency of a magnetically initiated Rydberg dissipative time crystal (DTC). In a room-temperature 87Rb vapor, a ~14 G magnetic field establishes a self-sustained DTC near 21 kHz, while a near-resonant 8.325 GHz field coupling the 63D3/2 to 64P3/2 manifold continuously shifts the autonomous oscillation frequency. The resulting field-to-frequency transfer function is strongly nonlinear, with a total fundamental-frequency excursion of ~6.7 kHz and peak responsivity approaching 1 kHz/(mV/cm). The nonlinear transition is substantially steeper than a simple quadratic saturation response and is captured by a self-consistent mean-field picture in which microwave-driven Rydberg-population redistribution modifies the collective resonance condition. Higher DTC harmonics preserve the same normalized transfer function while exhibiting approximately linear growth of absolute responsivity with harmonic order. These results establish an intrinsic many-body atomic route to RF amplitude-to-frequency conversion, enabling continuous frequency-domain sensing through an emergent atomic frequency without an externally engineered RF feedback oscillator, repeated optical spectral scans, or resolved Autler Townes splitting.

</details>


### [2] [Systematic errors from scalar slowing-down models in transient SERF magnetometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31865)
*M. Kopciuch,H. Raghavan,M. W. Mitchell*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper derives an effective Bloch model for SERF magnetometers using separate longitudinal and transverse slowing-down factors q∥(P) and q⊥(P), showing that the conventional single-scalar factor causes up to 4.2–10.1% magnetic-field estimation bias at high polarization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduced Bloch models of SERF magnetometers typically describe coupled electron–nuclear spin dynamics with a single nuclear slowing-down factor. This scalar description becomes inaccurate when the polarization magnitude changes appreciably, motivating a more accurate yet still simple model.

Method: The authors derive an effective Bloch equation with separate longitudinal and transverse slowing-down factors, q∥(P) and q⊥(P), that govern changes in polarization magnitude and direction, respectively. They validate it for ⁸⁷Rb under representative SERF conditions during free-induction decay (FID) by comparison with master-equation simulations.

Result: The dynamic scalar model yields a magnetic-field estimation bias up to 4.2% at high initial polarization, while fixing the slowing-down factor at a reference value raises the error to 10.1%. Lowering polarization suppresses the bias but reduces the magnetic-field information in the FID signal. The anisotropic response also modifies noncollinear optical pumping and adds a nonlinear contribution to the harmonic response under periodic driving.

Conclusion: The anisotropic slowing-down-factor model retains the simplicity of a three-component Bloch description while improving the quantitative accuracy of transient and driven SERF magnetometry.

Abstract: Reduced Bloch models of spin-exchange-relaxation-free (SERF) magnetometers commonly describe the coupled electron--nuclear spin dynamics using a single nuclear slowing-down factor. We show that this scalar description becomes inaccurate when the polarization magnitude changes appreciably. We derive an effective Bloch equation with separate longitudinal and transverse slowing-down factors, $q_\parallel(P)$ and $q_\perp(P)$, governing changes in polarization magnitude and direction. For $^{87}\mathrm{Rb}$ under representative SERF conditions and undergoing free-induction decay (FID), comparison with master-equation simulations shows that the dynamic scalar model produces a magnetic-field estimation bias of up to $4.2\%$ at high initial polarization, while fixing the slowing-down factor at a reference value increases the error to $10.1\%$. Lowering the polarization suppresses this bias but also reduces the magnetic-field information contained in the FID signal. We further show that the anisotropic response modifies noncollinear optical pumping and introduces an additional nonlinear contribution to the harmonic response under periodic driving. The resulting model retains the simplicity of a three-component Bloch description while improving the quantitative accuracy of transient and driven SERF magnetometry.

</details>


### [3] [Collinear Laser Spectroscopy of the $1s2s\,^3\!S_1\rightarrow 1s2p\,^3\!P_{0,2}$ Transitions in Helium-like $^{11}$B$^{3+}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33056)
*Axel Buß,Konstantin Mohr,Volker Hannen,Zoran Andelkovic,Max Horst,Phillip Imgram,Kristian König,Bernhard Maass,W. Nörtershäuser,Simon Rausch,Rodolfo Sánchez,Christian Weinheimer*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The authors measured hyperfine structure of fine-structure transitions in helium-like boron ions using an electron beam ion trap and collinear laser spectroscopy, confirming and improving previous measurements despite low statistics and technical challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To validate and improve prior measurements of fine-structure transitions in highly charged helium-like ions, which test atomic structure theory.

Method: Helium-like ions were created in an electron beam ion trap, ejected after a 15 ms production period, and probed via collinear laser spectroscopy, requiring careful treatment of the complex energy-time-profile of ion bunches and sensitivity to the EBIT starting potential.

Result: The previous measurement of the $1s2s\,{}^{3\!}S_1 \rightarrow 1s2p\,{}^{3\!}P_{0,2}$ transitions was confirmed and refined. Moderate tension was found in the hyperfine parameters of the $^{3\!}P_{0,2}$ levels, while fine-structure level energies agree reasonably well with experiments and theory.

Conclusion: Collinear laser spectroscopy on EBIT-produced ions is viable despite low statistics and technical complications, confirming and improving prior data; further work is needed on hyperfine parameters where tension remains.

Abstract: We report on hyperfine structure measurements of the $1s2s\,{}^{3\!}S_1 \rightarrow 1s2p\,{}^{3\!}P_{0,2}$ fine-structure transitions in the spectrum of $^{11}$B$^{3+}$ ions. The helium-like ions were created in an electron beam ion trap (EBIT) and ejected after a short production period of 15 ms. A challenge of the experiment was the treatment of the complex energy-time-profile of the created ion bunches and the sensitivity of the collinear laser spectroscopy to the starting potential inside the EBIT. Despite these complications and the low statistics of the experiment, we were able to confirm and improve the results of a previous measurement of these transitions. While we find moderate tension in the results for the hyperfine parameters of the $^{3\!}P_{0,2}$ levels, their fine-structure level energies are in reasonable agreement between experiments and with theory.

</details>


### [4] [Hyperfine structure splitting for the triplet $2^{3}S(L = 0)-$state in the two-muonic helium ${}^{3}$He $μ_{2}$ atom](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33059)
*Alexei M. Frolov*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: High-accuracy determination of the hyperfine structure splitting of the triplet 2³S state in the two-muonic helium ³He μ₂ atom, yielding Δ ≈ 5.1623403103010 × 10⁷ MHz with an uncertainty of ±10 Hz.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The hyperfine (spin-spin) interaction between the triplet two-muonic shell and the non-zero spin of the central nucleus produces a hyperfine structure splitting. Precise determination of this splitting is needed for fundamental tests of bound-state QED and for characterizing two-muonic atomic systems.

Method: The hyperfine structure splitting of the triplet 2³S (L = 0) state in the two-muonic helium ³He μ₂ atom is determined to high accuracy, accounting for the spin-spin interaction between the triplet two-muonic shell and the central nucleus spin.

Result: Δ ≈ 5.1623403103010 × 10⁷ MHz, with a standard uncertainty of ±10 Hz.

Conclusion: A highly accurate value for the hyperfine splitting of the triplet 2³S state in the ³He μ₂ atom is obtained, providing a precise benchmark for further theoretical and experimental study of two-muonic helium systems.

Abstract: The hyperfine structure splitting for the triplet $2^{3}S(L = 0)-$state in the two-muonic helium ${}^{3}$He $μ_{2}$ atom is determined to high accuracy. Such a structure arises as a result of hyperfine (or spin-spin) interaction between the triplet two-muonic shell and non-zero spin of the central helicon nucleus. Our result for the hyperfine structure splitting is $Δ\approx 5.1623403103010 \cdot 10^{7}$ $MHz$, where the standard uncertainty has been evaluated as $\pm 10$ $Hz$.

</details>


### [5] [Non-dipole effects in a multielectron atom](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33494)
*Alexey N. Hopersky,Alexey M. Nadolinsky,Rustam V. Koneev*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The Letter derives the analytical structure of the dipole radiation transition operator for a multielectron atom including non-dipole corrections, generalizing earlier "split" continuum-electron results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work (Komninos et al., PRA 86, 023420 (2012)) treated the dipole transition operator only within a "split" electron approximation for the continuous spectrum, leaving a general operator formulation incomplete.

Method: Analytical derivation of the dipole radiation transition operator, incorporating non-dipole effects for a multielectron atom; the complete multipole transition operator [formula (10)] is formulated using irreducible tensor operator methods and extended to higher multipoles.

Result: A closed analytical expression for the dipole radiation transition operator, which subsumes and extends the earlier "split" electron results; the complete multipole operator is specified through irreducible tensor operator techniques.

Conclusion: The formulation provides a general framework for multipole radiation transitions in multielectron atoms, valid beyond the dipole approximation and extendable to higher multipoles.

Abstract: This Letter establishes the analytical structure of the dipole radiation transition operator, taking into account non-dipole effects for a multielectron atom. The results summarize those of Komninos Y. et al., Phys. Rev. A 86, 023420 (2012), obtained in an approximation of a "split" electron of a continuous spectrum. The result for the complete operator of a multipole radiation transition [see formula (10)] can be specified within the framework of the methods of the theory of irreducible tensor operators and for higher multipoles.

</details>


### [6] [Breit interaction in elastic scattering of vortex electron on Hydrogen atomic target](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34578)
*Pengcheng Zhao*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies elastic scattering of a 300 keV vortex electron by hydrogen, showing that the Breit interaction dominates in certain kinematic regions for sufficiently large vortex angular momentum, contrary to the usual assumption that it is negligible for low-Z atoms, and that smaller targets favor observing this effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies on elastic scattering of electron vortex beams with atoms have considered only the Coulomb interaction, while the Breit interaction (the leading relativistic correction to the Coulomb potential between two electrons) has not been systematically examined, despite vortex beams being fundamental to applications.

Method: The authors compute elastic scattering of a 300 keV vortex electron by a hydrogen atomic target, comparing two situations: the pure Coulomb contribution versus the sum of Coulomb and Breit contributions. They also compare single-atom, mesoscopic, and macroscopic target cases.

Result: The Breit interaction dominates the scattering process in certain kinematic regions when the total angular momentum of the vortex electron is sufficiently large, breaking the conventional notion that the Breit interaction contributes little to electron scattering for low-Z atoms. This arises because a large-angular-momentum vortex electron possesses a substantial magnetic moment, and the Breit interaction describes magnetic-moment interactions. Target size significantly influences the observability of Breit-induced effects, with smaller targets favoring observation.

Conclusion: The Breit interaction can play a dominant role in elastic vortex-electron scattering for sufficiently large vortex angular momentum, even for low-Z targets, and the effect is most observable with small targets.

Abstract: The interaction of electron vortex beams with atoms is fundamental to their applications, yet existing studies on elastic scattering have considered only the Coulomb interaction. The role of the Breit interaction, which represents the leading relativistic correction to the Coulomb potential between two electrons, has not been systematically examined. In this paper, we study the elastic scattering of a \(300\) keV vortex electron by a hydrogen atomic target, with emphasis on the role of the Breit interaction. Comparisons are made between two situations: the pure Coulomb contribution and the sum of the Coulomb and Breit contributions. Numerous results show that the Breit interaction dominates the scattering process in certain kinematic regions when the total angular momentum of vortex electron is sufficiently large, which breaks with the conventional notion that the Breit interaction has little contribution to electron scattering for low-\(Z\) atoms. This phenomenon arises primarily because a vortex electron with large angular momentum possesses a substantial magnetic moment, and the Breit interaction precisely describes the interaction between magnetic moments. Moreover, we further compare the single-atom, mesoscopic, and macroscopic target cases, and show that the target size significantly influences the observability of the Breit-induced effects, with a smaller target size being favorable for observing this phenomenon.

</details>


### [7] [Interactions and dynamics of metastable triplet-state helium dimer with Ne and Ar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34713)
*Dibyendu Sardar,François Lique*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: First theoretical study of He$_2(a^3\Sigma_u^+)$+Ne van der Waals complex: ab initio PES, full close-coupling collisions, fine-structure propensity rules, and comparison with He$_2$+Ar.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: He$_2$ in its metastable triplet state $a^3\Sigma_u^+$ is a candidate for direct laser cooling and precision measurements, yet its interactions and collisions remain unexplored in the cold and ultracold regimes. There is a need for benchmark interaction potentials and scattering data.

Method: Spin-restricted coupled cluster with single, double, and noniterative triple excitations (RCCSD(T)) to compute the ground $X^3A'$ interaction potential; construction of a 3D vibrationally averaged potential energy surface (PES); full close-coupling scattering calculations including fine-structure levels of He$_2(a^3\Sigma_u^+)$, with analysis of quasi-bound-state resonances and a parallel study of He$_2$+Ar.

Result: The interaction potential has an extremely shallow well of depth 4.612 cm$^{-1}$. Low-energy scattering reveals resonant features tied to quasi-bound states; a propensity rule for fine-structure conserving transitions is established. He$_2$+Ar shows a richer resonance structure than He$_2$+Ne, demonstrating sensitivity of low-energy scattering to the PES and reduced mass.

Conclusion: The computed PES and scattering properties provide a reliable benchmark for the He$_2$+Ne van der Waals complex and should aid interpretation of future experimental spectra and collision measurements.

Abstract: Helium dimer in its metastable triplet electronic state, He$_2(a~^3Σ_u^+)$, is a promising candidate for direct laser cooling and precision measurements. Interactions and collisions involving He$_2(a~^3Σ_u^+)$ remain unexplored in ultracold and cold energy domains. Here, we present the first theoretical investigation of intermolecular interactions and collision dynamics of He$_2(a~^3Σ_u^+)$+Ne van der Waals complex. For the ground electronic state, $X~^3\mathrm{A}'$, we employ a spin-restricted coupled cluster method with single, double, and noniterative triple excitations. The resulting interaction potential exhibits an extremely shallow well with a depth of 4.612 cm$^{-1}$. We construct a three-dimensional vibrationally averaged potential energy surface. Using this surface, we perform full close-coupling calculations including fine-structure levels of He$_2(a~^3Σ_u^+)$ to investigate collisional excitation and quenching induced by Ne. We analyze the low-energy scattering dynamics and model resonant features associated with quasi-bound states supported by interaction potential. Propensity rule for fine-structure conserving transitions is established. We additionally consider He$_2$+Ar collisions and compare their energy dependence and resonance structure with those of He$_2$+Ne. The He$_2$+Ar system exhibits a richer resonance structure, highlighting the sensitivity of low-energy scattering to interaction potential and reduced mass. Calculated PES and scattering properties provide a reliable benchmark for description of He$_2$+Ne van der Waals complex and may be useful for interpretation of future experimental spectra and collision measurements.

</details>


### [8] [Potential-energy surfaces of water and its molecular ions up to \texorpdfstring{H$_2$O$^{3+}$}{H2O3+}](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35144)
*Giorgio Visentin,Agapi Emmanouilidou*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Potential-energy surfaces (PESs) of molecular ions up to H2O3+ with various hole configurations are computed using CASSCF-based ab initio techniques, benchmarked against literature, and validated as a function of two bond lengths and the bond angle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PESs of highly charged water ions (up to H2O3+) with arbitrary combinations of outer-valence, inner-valence, and core holes are needed for future studies of water interacting with free-electron laser pulses, but reliable ab initio methods for such states must first be identified.

Method: Several ab initio techniques based on the CASSCF method are considered and benchmarked against the literature to compute the potential-energy surfaces as a function of the two bond lengths and the angle between them.

Result: Techniques producing accurate potential-energy surfaces for molecular ions up to H2O3+ are identified.

Conclusion: The resulting PESs will be used in future studies of water interaction with free-electron laser pulses, and the techniques employed here for water are transferable to obtain PESs of other triatomic molecules.

Abstract: We compute the potential-energy surfaces for molecular ions up to H$_2$O$^{3+}$ with any combinations of outer-valence, inner-valence and core holes. To obtain the potential-energy surfaces, we consider several ab initio techniques based on the CASSCF method and benchmark them with respect to the literature. We thus identify the techniques which produce accurate potential-energy surfaces as a function of the two bond lengths and the angle between the two bond lengths. These potential-energy surfaces will be used in future studies of the interaction of water with free-electron laser pulses. Furthermore, the techniques employed in this work in the context of water can be used to obtain the potential-energy surfaces of other triatomic molecules.

</details>


### [9] [Reconstructing Molecular-Ion Quantum States with Two-Color Strong-Field Scattering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35283)
*Dina S. Eissa,Felipe Morales,Abraham Camacho Garibay,Vyacheslav Leshchenko,Cosmin I. Blaga,Misha Ivanov,Pierre Agostini,Maria Richter,Louis F. DiMauro*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Two-color strong-field scattering, combining a strong mid-infrared field with a weak second harmonic, enables retrieval of the amplitudes and relative phases of ionic superpositions by mapping them onto phase-dependent sub-angstrom bond-length changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strong-field ionization can create coherent superpositions of molecular ionic states, and their amplitudes and relative phases drive subsequent ultrafast dynamics. However, experimental access to this coherent information has remained challenging.

Method: A strong mid-infrared field ionizes the molecule and launches a continuum electron; a weak second harmonic field resonantly couples vibronic states of the parent ion during the electron's continuum excursion, inducing structural changes on the sub-laser-cycle timescale. Scanning the relative phase between the two colors controls the ionic evolution and maps it onto phase-dependent sub-angstrom bond-length changes retrieved from the rescattering signal.

Result: Applied to N2, the two-color scheme probes coherence between different ionization channels and reconstructs the initial ionic superposition.

Conclusion: Two-color strong-field scattering is established as a powerful structural approach for characterizing the complex amplitudes of ion quantum wavepackets.

Abstract: Strong-field ionization of molecules can prepare coherent superpositions of ionic states whose amplitudes and relative phases govern the ensuing ultrafast dynamics, but accessing this information experimentally remains a challenge. Here we introduce two-color strong-field scattering, in which a strong mid-infrared field ionizes the molecule and launches an electron, while a weak second harmonic field resonantly couples vibronic states of the parent ion during the electron's continuum excursion, thereby introducing structural changes on the sub-laser-cycle timescale. Scanning the relative phase between the two colors controls the ionic evolution and maps it onto phase-dependent sub-angstrom bond-length changes retrieved from the rescattering signal. Using the example of N$_2$, we apply the two-color setup to probe the coherence between different ionization channels and to reconstruct the initial ionic superposition. Our results establish two-color strong-field scattering as a powerful structural approach to characterize the complex amplitudes of ion quantum wavepackets.

</details>


### [10] [Raman Transitions in Collinear Laser Spectroscopy for High-Precision Frequency Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35284)
*Julien Spahn,Hendrik Bodnar,Kristian König,Wilfried Nörtershäuser,Robbe Van Duyse*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This work demonstrates stimulated Raman spectroscopy on dipole-forbidden fine-structure transitions in a fast ion beam, applied to the Sr-88 clock transition and the D3/2 to D5/2 fine-structure splitting, achieving a Doppler-free precision scheme that surpasses the linewidth limit of conventional electric-dipole spectroscopy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional electric-dipole (E1) fluorescence spectroscopy of narrow transitions is limited by the natural linewidth and lacks access to dipole-forbidden transitions. The authors seek a precision method that can address narrow, dipole-forbidden transitions in short-lived isotopes, enabling probes of fundamental atomic and nuclear physics.

Method: Stimulated Raman spectroscopy is applied to the dipole-forbidden S1/2 to D5/2 clock transition in a fast Sr-88 ion beam. Detection is enabled by combining it with a subsequent E1 transition, allowing standard fluorescence detection. The D3/2 to D5/2 fine-structure splitting is then measured directly using a Doppler-free scheme with two subsequent Raman transitions.

Result: The S1/2 to D5/2 clock transition is successfully excited and detected via the E1-assisted fluorescence channel, and the D3/2 to D5/2 fine-structure splitting is measured directly with the Doppler-free two-Raman-transition scheme.

Conclusion: The demonstrated approach provides a path toward precision collinear laser spectroscopy of narrow transitions in short-lived isotopes, overcoming the linewidth limit of conventional electric-dipole spectroscopy and offering a tool for probing fundamental aspects of atomic and nuclear physics.

Abstract: We demonstrate stimulated Raman spectroscopy on dipole-forbidden fine-structure transitions in a fast ion beam. It is applied to the $\mathrm{S}_{1/2}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{5/2}$ clock transition in $^{88}\mathrm{Sr}^{+}$ and combined with a subsequent E1 transition to allow for its detection by standard fluorescence detection. Building on this, the $\mathrm{D}_{3/2}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{5/2}$ fine-structure splitting is measured directly using a Doppler-free scheme employing two subsequent Raman transitions. These measurements demonstrate a path toward precision collinear laser spectroscopy of narrow transitions on short-lived isotopes beyond the linewidth limit of conventional electric-dipole spectroscopy, which can be used to probe fundamental aspects of atomic and nuclear physics.

</details>


### [11] [Qprop-ND: A Nondipole Extension of the Qprop Package for Strong-Field Laser Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35401)
*Haoyuan He,Zhao-Han Zhang,Karen Z. Hatsagortsyan,Christoph H. KeiteL*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Qprop 3.0 extends the Qprop package to include nondipole (magnetic-field and finite-wavelength) effects of laser propagation, enabling strong-field atomic simulations beyond the dipole approximation. It retains spherical-harmonic expansion, Crank-Nicolson propagation, and t-SURFF/i-SURFV photoelectron spectra, with validity demonstrated via examples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most strong-field atomic simulations rely on the dipole approximation, which neglects magnetic-field and propagation effects. These become non-negligible in the nondipole regime—where the laser magnetic field matters or the wavelength is comparable to the atomic size—so a tool capable of handling such phenomena is needed.

Method: The existing Qprop 3.0 time-dependent Schrödinger equation solver (electron in a laser field plus atomic potential) is extended to incorporate nondipole effects from laser wave propagation. As in the dipole version, the wave function is expanded in spherical harmonics, time propagation uses the Crank-Nicolson approach, and photoelectron spectra are computed with the t-SURFF or i-SURFV methods.

Result: The implementation's validity and accuracy are demonstrated through several illustrative examples of strong-field atomic phenomena in the nondipole regime, including above-threshold ionization, ionization stabilization in high-frequency laser fields, and high-order harmonic generation.

Conclusion: The extended Qprop 3.0 provides a usable, validated tool for simulating strong-field atomic processes beyond the dipole approximation, covering cases where magnetic-field effects are significant or the laser wavelength is comparable to the atomic size.

Abstract: The Qprop 3.0 package for the solution of the time-dependent Schrödinger equation for an electron in a laser field and atomic potential is extended to incorporate nondipole effects related to the laser wave propagation. The developed code can be used for the simulation of strong field atomic phenomena in the nondipole regime, such as above-threshold ionization, ionization stabilization in high-frequency laser fields, high-order harmonic generation, when the effects connected with the magnetic field are non-negligible or when the laser wavelength is comparable with the atomic size. Similar to the dipole Qprop the wave function is expanded in spherical harmonics, the time-propagation of the wave functions is performed using the Crank- Nicolson approach, and the photoelectron spectra are calculated employing the t-SURFF or i- SURFV methods. The validity and accuracy of the implementation are demonstrated through several illustrative examples.

</details>

### [12] [Knowledge-Driven XRD Phase Identification via Multi-View Retrieval and Explanation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31888)
*Doaa Mohamed,Markus Stricker*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A multi-decision framework integrates convolutional autoencoder representation learning, similarity-based retrieval, and explainable decision support to perform robust, interpretable XRD phase analysis, achieving 98.85% crystal system and 95.82% space group accuracy on synthetic data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interpreting XRD patterns is challenging, especially in high-throughput materials discovery where many novel materials require characterization and no reference patterns exist. Manual interpretation is error-prone, motivating automated, machine-learning-based analysis.

Method: The framework unifies representation learning, similarity-based retrieval, and explainable decision support within a single reference database. A convolutional autoencoder learns compact latent representations of XRD patterns that preserve structural similarity and resist experimental noise and measurement variations. Inference combines multiple decision pathways in a shared latent space: latent-space classification and retrieval, explanation-guided similarity via Integrated Gradients, and composition-based similarity search. These produce ranked candidate phase lists aggregated into a final prediction with a confidence score.

Result: On synthetic datasets, the framework achieved 98.85% accuracy for crystal system classification and 95.82% accuracy for space group prediction on the test set, while remaining robust under realistic perturbations.

Conclusion: The framework enables reliable, analyst-friendly identification of crystal phases and structures, supporting high-throughput and exploratory materials discovery. It moves beyond single-label prediction toward ranked, interpretable phase analysis that mirrors expert practice.

Abstract: X-ray diffraction (XRD) is a experimental technique for determining the phase composition and structure of crystalline materials. However, interpreting XRD patterns is challenging, particularly in high-throughput materials discovery, where many novel materials may need to be characterized and no reference patterns are available. Consequently, machine learning is increasingly used to accelerate and automate the analysis while reducing errors associated with human interpretation. We propose a multi-decision framework for XRD phase analysis that integrates representation learning, similarity-based retrieval, and explainable decision support within a unified reference database. A convolutional autoencoder learns compact latent representations of XRD patterns that preserve structural similarity while remaining robust to variations arising from experimental noise and measurement conditions. By integrating multiple decision pathways within a shared latent space, the framework moves beyond single-label prediction toward ranked and interpretable phase analysis that mirrors expert practice. During inference, complementary decision mechanisms are applied, including latent-space classification and retrieval, explanation-guided similarity using Integrated Gradients, and composition-based similarity search. These mechanisms generate ranked candidate phase lists that are aggregated into a final prediction with an associated confidence score. Experiments on synthetic datasets demonstrate strong predictive performance, achieving 98.85\,\% accuracy for crystal system classification and 95.82\,\% accuracy for space group prediction on the test set, while maintaining robustness under realistic perturbations. The framework supports reliable, analyst-friendly identification of crystal phases and structures in high-throughput and exploratory materials discovery settings.

</details>


### [13] [The Young-E(3) Tensor Product Decomposition for Rotation and Permutation Equivariant Cluster Expansions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31895)
*James M. Goff,Aidan P. Thompson*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Young--E(3) tensor product basis (YE3T), a generalization of cluster expansions (CE) and atomic cluster expansions (ACE) that encodes both rotation (SO(3)) and permutation (S_N) symmetry via Schur--Weyl decomposition and joint coupling, yielding a complete orthonormal equivariant basis that contains ACE as a special case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing rotation- and permutation-adapted expansions such as ACE restrict permutation symmetry to the fully symmetric carrier. Prior work (Barthelemy et al.) showed that avoiding an overcomplete invariant basis gives scaling benefits, motivating a direct, complete construction that also generalizes beyond permutation-symmetric features.

Method: Schur--Weyl decomposition is performed in Young subgroup-stabilized carriers, followed by joint coupling of rotation and permutation representations to generate arbitrary S_N x SO(3) tensor product carriers. This resolves complete orthonormal joint rotation- and permutation-adapted bases for arbitrary tensor product ranks and angular/radial content, with tunable permutation symmetry character.

Result: The ACE basis emerges as a subset corresponding to the fully symmetric permutation carrier. YE3T produces a complete orthonormal basis without an overcomplete step. The resulting joint irreducible rotation- and permutation-equivariant basis sets outperform existing rotation-adapted expansions in both speed and accuracy, establishing a new Pareto front for machine-learned interatomic potentials, and are useful for atomistic and electronic-structure simulations.

Conclusion: YE3T provides a rigorous, general framework unifying rotation and permutation symmetry adaption, eliminates the overcomplete-then-reduce step, extends to non-symmetric permutation characters, and delivers a Pareto-improving basis for machine-learned potentials across atomistic and electronic-structure applications.

Abstract: A generalization of fixed-lattice cluster expansions (CE) and atomic cluster expansions (ACE) is presented that expresses both rotation and permutation symmetries. By performing Schur--Weyl decomposition in Young subgroup-stabilized carriers, followed by joint coupling of rotation and permutation representations, arbitrary $S_N\times SO(3)$ tensor product carriers are generated. This allows us to resolve complete, orthonormal joint rotation and permutation-adapted bases for arbitrary tensor product ranks and arbitrary angular and radial tensor product content. We show that this very general Young--E(3) (YE3T) tensor product basis contains the ACE basis as a subset, corresponding to the special case where permutation symmetry character is restricted to the fully symmetric carrier. Rather than constructing an overcomplete rotation and permutation-invariant basis and reducing it \textit{a posteriori}, Barthelemy \textit{et al.} recently demonstrated scaling benefits by not constructing an overcomplete basis. YE3T directly produces a complete orthonormal basis without an overcomplete step and rigorously extends to permutation characters beyond permutation-symmetric features. The YE3T decomposition yields new joint irreducible rotation- and permutation-equivariant basis sets that surpass existing rotation-adapted expansions in both speed and accuracy, defining a new Pareto front for machine-learned interatomic potentials. We show that the tunability of the permutation symmetry character makes the basis useful for both atomistic and electronic-structure simulations.

</details>


### [14] [Jahn-Teller Distortion-Driven Lattice Parameter Tunability in Coherent High-Entropy Oxide Thin Films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31899)
*Dhiya Srikanth,Joseph Petruska,Matthew Furst,Kaylin Lamaute,Jon-Paul Maria,Saeed S. I. Almishal*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Cu concentration and Jahn-Teller distortions are shown to be co-equal structural control parameters governing the out-of-plane lattice parameter of MgCoNiCuZnO-derived high-entropy oxide films, with effects strongly dependent on growth temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work identifies Co valence change as the primary driver of the MgCoNiCuZnO lattice response with temperature, but the role of Cu and its Jahn-Teller distortions in controlling the lattice parameter remained isolated. This study aims to decouple and quantify how Cu concentration and Jahn-Teller distortions control the out-of-plane lattice parameter.

Method: Nine pseudomorphic MgCoNiCuZnO-derived high-entropy oxide compositions spanning 0-20% Cu were prepared on MgO. Target composition and phase were verified by X-ray fluorescence and diffraction. Films were grown by pulsed laser deposition across multiple substrate temperatures (notably 500 °C and 300 °C).

Result: At 500 °C, the lattice parameter increases nearly linearly with Cu content; at 300 °C, it decreases nonlinearly, yielding a 2.6% difference between temperature regimes for equimolar MgCoNiCuZnO under the growth conditions used.

Conclusion: The temperature-dependent lattice parameter change is attributed to coupling between Cu-driven Jahn-Teller distortions and Co valence change. While Co valence was previously considered the primary driver, the results establish Cu Jahn-Teller distortion as an equally important structural control parameter.

Abstract: Here, we isolate how Cu concentration and Jahn-Teller distortions control the out-of-plane lattice parameter of pseudomorphic MgCoNiCuZnO-derived high-entropy oxide films on MgO. We prepare nine compositions spanning 0-20% Cu, verify target composition and phase by X-ray fluorescence and diffraction, and grow films by pulsed laser deposition across multiple substrate temperatures. At 500 °C, the lattice parameter increases nearly linearly with Cu content; at 300°C, it decreases nonlinearly, producing a 2.6% difference between temperature regimes for equimolar MgCoNiCuZnO at our growth conditions. We attribute this change in lattice parameter to temperature-dependent coupling between Cu-driven Jahn-Teller distortions and Co valence change. While prior work identifies Co valence as the primary driver of the MgCoNiCuZnO lattice response with temperature; our results establish Cu Jahn-Teller distortion as an equally important structural control parameter.

</details>


### [15] [Composition-Driven Metal-to-Semiconductor Transition and Enhanced Phonon Transport in B-C substituted Clathrate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32894)
*Ghulam Hussain,Dario Massa,Rajibul Islam,Magdalena Birowska,Carmine Autieri,Xiaoguang Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A single B-to-C substitution in BaB3C3 simultaneously drives a metal-to-semiconductor transition and a ~2.5-fold enhancement of lattice thermal conductivity, revealing a unified chemical design rule coupling electronic structure and thermal transport.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Controlling electronic structure and thermal transport simultaneously via simple chemical design rules remains a long-sought goal for heat-management and energy materials. Conventional wisdom linking metallic behavior to high thermal conductivity and semiconducting behavior to low thermal conductivity motivates the search for a strategy that decouples these properties.

Method: DFT combined with machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs). Specifically, the Message Passing Atomic Cluster Expansion (MACE) model is trained to reproduce DFT energies and forces, then used to compute phonon dispersion and lattice thermal conductivity for cubic BaB3C3 and its B-to-C-substituted tetragonal BaB2C4.

Result: The B-to-C substitution donates one electron per formula unit to the B-C framework, inducing strong C-C bonds and a 0.33 eV bandgap. MACE reproduces DFT benchmarks for phonon dispersion and thermal conductivity. Cubic BaB3C3 shows isotropic lattice thermal conductivity of 7.6 W/mK at 300 K; substitution hardens phonons (upshift from ~800 to ~900 cm^-1) and increases group velocities and lifetimes, raising conductivity to 18.7 W/mK (~2.5-fold enhancement of the in-plane component) and switching from isotropic to anisotropic behavior (kappa_x = kappa_y > kappa_z).

Conclusion: Minimal B-to-C substitution is a viable strategy to modulate electronic structure, transforming the crystal from metal to semiconductor while concomitantly enhancing phonon thermal transport, establishing a coupled design rule for heat-management and energy materials.

Abstract: Establishing chemical design rules that simultaneously control the electronic structure and thermal transport is a long-sought goal for heat-management and energy materials. Here, we demonstrate that a single B-to-C substitution changes the electron count and simultaneously reconstructs the bonding network and crystal structure, driving a metal-to-semiconductor transition while concurrently enhancing the lattice thermal conductivity. Using density-functional theory (DFT) and machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs), we investigate the electronic structure, lattice dynamics, and phonon thermal transport in cubic BaB3C3 and its B-to-C-substituted tetragonal BaB2C4 structure. The substitution donates one electron per formula unit to the B-C framework, thereby triggering the formation of strong C-C bonds and opening a bandgap of 0.33 eV. The Message Passing Atomic Cluster Expansion (MACE) model reproduces DFT energies and forces, yielding phonon dispersion and lattice thermal conductivity in excellent agreement with DFT benchmarks. Thermodynamically stable cubic BaB3C3 possesses an isotropic lattice thermal conductivity of 7.6 W/mK at 300 K. Substituting B with C hardens the phonon dispersion (evidenced by a frequency upshift from ~800 to ~900 cm^-1) and simultaneously boosts both phonon group velocities and lifetimes. Consequently, the lattice thermal conductivity surges to 18.7 W/mK, representing a remarkable ~2.5-fold enhancement of the in-plane component and a transformation from isotropic to anisotropic behavior, characterized by kappa_x = kappa_y > kappa_z. Our results demonstrate that minimal B-to-C substitution provides a viable strategy to modulate the electronic structure, thereby transforming the crystal from a metal to a semiconductor and concomitantly enhancing phonon thermal transport.

</details>


### [16] [Let CSP Be Your ANCHOR: Adaptive Crystal Search over Frozen Structure Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33407)
*Emma Lei Hovmand,Jonas Elsborg,Melih Kandemir,Arghya Bhowmik*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper argues de novo crystal generation should be split into a frozen crystal structure prediction (CSP) prior and a separate composition search policy. It introduces ANCHOR (a GRPO composition policy with multi-objective rewards) and continuous adaptive novelty (CAN), a graded novelty metric against known and newly discovered structures. ANCHOR substantially raises metastable/unique-new (MSUN) and stable-unique-new (SUN) rates over DNG baselines and sets a new state of the art under MatterGen's evaluation pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: De novo crystal generation models conflate two distinct decisions—where to search in composition space and how to generate structures—within a single set of weights. The authors argue this coupling impairs discovery: DNG models optimized on fixed reward objectives tend to reweight chemistry already supported by the pretrained model, drifting toward known or less stable compositions rather than expanding genuine discovery. Separating the physical prior from the search policy should therefore better serve materials discovery.

Method: The framework separates a frozen CSP model (treated as a physical prior/fixed evaluator) from a composition-search policy. ANCHOR is a GRPO-trained composition policy optimized with multi-objective rewards operating around the frozen CSP backbone. Continuous adaptive novelty (CAN) provides a graded novelty score measured against known structures and a growing discovery history, enabling reward signals to account for the search's own trajectory. The authors also test alternatives: KL-regularized vs. unregularized fine-tuning of DNG models on the same rewards, routing a stability-only reward into the CSP backbone, and likelihood training on structures found during search.

Result: With the frozen CSP model as a fixed ruler under one evaluator, replacing DNG compositions with ANCHOR's policy on the same CSP backbone raises MSUN from 11.4% to 47.6% and SUN from 1.1% to 22.1% at 99.9% formula uniqueness. KL-regularized DNG fine-tuning can only reweight chemistry the pretrained model already supports by a bounded factor, while unregularized DNG fine-tunes move toward known or less stable chemistry; neither raises the on-hull fraction. A stability-only reward routed into ANCHOR's CSP backbone roughly halves SUN relative to the frozen backbone, whereas likelihood training on search-discovered structures can improve the CSP backbone. Under MatterGen's evaluation pipeline, ANCHOR raises state-of-the-art MSUN from 29.2% to 41.3%, transfers without retraining to two further CSP backbones, and reaches 47.1% after distillation into Crystalite-CSP.

Conclusion: Discovery is better served by decoupling composition search from structure generation: a frozen CSP model should serve as a physical prior improved via likelihood training, while rewards (including novelty relative to the search's own history) should drive a separate composition policy. ANCHOR demonstrates this separation yields large gains in stable, unique, novel crystal discovery and transfers across CSP backbones, though on-hull rates remain contingent on the quality of the underlying potential used for evaluation.

Abstract: De novo crystal generation (DNG) models decide where to search in composition space and how to generate structures with one set of weights. We argue that discovery is better served by separating the two. A crystal structure prediction (CSP) model is a physical prior that should be improved by likelihood training, while rewards, including novelty measured against the search's own history, should act on a search over compositions. We introduce ANCHOR, a GRPO composition policy trained with multi-objective rewards around a frozen CSP model, and continuous adaptive novelty (CAN), a graded novelty score against known structures and a growing discovery history. Using the frozen CSP model as a fixed ruler under one evaluator, we test where adaptation should act. Replacing DNG compositions with ANCHOR's policy on the same CSP backbone raises MSUN from 11.4% to 47.6% and SUN from 1.1% to 22.1% at 99.9% formula uniqueness. Fine-tuning DNG models directly on the same rewards instead moves their composition marginal without raising their on-hull fraction. We show that KL-regularized fine-tuning of a DNG model can only reweight chemistry the pretrained model already supports by a bounded factor, while unregularized DNG fine-tunes move toward known or less stable chemistry. Even a stability-only reward routed into ANCHOR's CSP backbone roughly halves SUN relative to the frozen backbone, whereas likelihood training on structures found during search can improve a CSP backbone. Under MatterGen's evaluation pipeline, ANCHOR raises state-of-the-art MSUN from 29.2% to 41.3%, transfers without retraining to two further CSP backbones, and reaches 47.1% after distillation into Crystalite-CSP. As with any model optimised against a potential, its on-hull rate depends on that potential.

</details>


### [17] [Temperature Dependent Evolution of the Electronic Structure in EuZn2As2 across the Neel Transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32121)
*Milo Sprague,Anup Pradhan Sakhya,Barun Ghosh,Mazharul Islam Mondal,Arun K. Kumay,Himanshu Sheokand,Kapil Gope,Tetiana Romanova,Dariusz Kaczorowski,Arun Bansil,Madhab Neupane*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper reports a pronounced resistivity anomaly in the antiferromagnetic compound EuZn2As2 near its Neel temperature (TN = 19 K), showing it is suppressed by applied magnetic fields and originates from spin scattering rather than a Fermi surface reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Antiferromagnetic EuX2Pn2 compounds have attracted intensive interest due to unusual anomalous Hall effect behavior and pronounced resistive anomalies near the Neel temperature. These magnetotransport phenomena have been variously interpreted via short-ranged ferromagnetic fluctuations, magnetic polaron formation, canted spin configurations, and temperature-dependent metal-insulator transitions. Clarifying the origin of such transport anomalies is important for understanding magnetoresistive materials relevant to magnetic memory storage and spintronic devices.

Method: The authors observed a pronounced resistivity anomaly in EuZn2As2 near TN = 19 K and demonstrated its suppression under both in-plane and out-of-plane applied magnetic fields. To interpret the origin of this transport behavior, they studied the temperature-dependent electronic structure using combined angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) and first-principles density functional theory (DFT) calculations.

Result: The resistivity anomaly near TN = 19 K was observed and suppressed by applied magnetic fields, both in-plane and out-of-plane. The combined ARPES and DFT study revealed only limited modifications to the bands across TN away from the Fermi energy.

Conclusion: The lack of significant electronic structure involvement across TN indicates a spin-scattering origin for the observed transport properties, rather than a reconstruction of the Fermi surface.

Abstract: Magnetoresistive materials have been tremendously important for the development of magnetic memory storage and spintronic devices. Recently, the antiferromagnetic EuX2Pn2 compounds, with X being a transition metal and Pn being a pnictogen, have seen intensive research interest due to their unusual anomalous Hall effect behavior and pronounced resistive anomaly near the Neel temperature (TN). These magnetotransport phenomena have been interpreted in the context of shortranged ferromagnetic Structuations, magnetic polaron formation, canted spin configurations, and temperature dependent metal insulator transitions in the electronic structure. Here, we report the observation of such a pronounced resistivity anomaly in EuZn2As2 near TN = 19 K. We demonstrate the suppression of this anomaly using applied magnetic fields, both in plane and out of plane. To further interpret the origin of the observed transport behavior, we studied the temperature dependent electronic structure using combined angle resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) and first principles density functional theory (DFT) calculations, which exhibits limited modifications to the bands across TN away from the Fermi energy. This lack of involvement of the electronic structure indicates a spin-scattering origin of the aforementioned transport properties, rather than a reconstruction of the Fermi surface.

</details>


### [18] [Unifying Magnetic, Electrical, and Thermoelectric Responses in a Non-Collinear Antiferromagnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33046)
*André José,Adrielson Dias,Carlos Eduardo,Giovanna Celly,Luiza Paffer,José Araújo,José Laurentino,Monalisa Cavalcante,José Holanda*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A robust, quantitatively consistent exchange-bias response of ~50 Oe is observed in an IrMn3/Py heterostructure via MOKE, AMR, and ANE measurements, establishing a coherent multifunctional imprint from a non-collinear antiferromagnet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-collinear antiferromagnets possess complex spin configurations and interfacial symmetries that could enable unconventional control of magnetic, electrical, and thermoelectric responses, yet such interfacial effects remain largely unexplored beyond conventional collinear antiferromagnetic systems. The authors aim to probe whether a non-collinear antiferromagnet can imprint a coherent exchange-bias response across multiple physical observables.

Method: The study employs an IrMn3 (10 nm)/permalloy (Py, 5 nm) heterostructure. Exchange bias is characterized through three independent measurement techniques: longitudinal magneto-optical Kerr effect (MOKE) for magnetization reversal, anisotropic magnetoresistance (AMR) for charge transport, and anomalous Nernst effect (ANE) for thermally driven voltage generation. Angular dependence measurements are also performed to assess the symmetry of the response.

Result: All three techniques independently reveal the same unidirectional field shift of approximately 50 Oe, along with a consistent cosine angular dependence. This establishes a direct correspondence between the interfacial magnetic symmetry imposed by the non-collinear antiferromagnet and the responses of the adjacent ferromagnet, manifested consistently in magnetization reversal, charge transport, and thermally driven voltage generation.

Conclusion: The exchange-bias imprint from the non-collinear antiferromagnet is not restricted to a single observable, revealing a coherent multifunctional response of IrMn3/Py. This extends the conventional understanding of exchange bias beyond collinear antiferromagnetic systems and highlights non-collinear antiferromagnets as promising platforms for coupling magnetic, electrical, and thermoelectric functionalities in spintronic and spin-caloritronic devices.

Abstract: Non-collinear antiferromagnets offer unconventional routes for controlling magnetic, electrical, and thermoelectric responses through their complex spin configurations and interfacial symmetry. Here, we detect exceptional interfacial properties arising from a non-collinear antiferromagnet by demonstrating a robust and quantitatively consistent exchange-bias response in an IrMn$_3$/Py heterostructure. The system consists of a 10 nm IrMn$_3$ layer coupled to a 5 nm permalloy (Py) film. Longitudinal magneto-optical Kerr effect (MOKE), anisotropic magnetoresistance (AMR), and anomalous Nernst effect (ANE) measurements independently reveal the same unidirectional field shift of approximately 50 Oe, together with a consistent cosine angular dependence. The simultaneous observation of these signatures establishes a direct correspondence between the interfacial magnetic symmetry imposed by the non-collinear antiferromagnet and the responses of the adjacent ferromagnet. This symmetry is manifested consistently in magnetization reversal, charge transport, and thermally driven voltage generation, demonstrating that the exchange-bias imprint is not restricted to a single experimental observable. Our results reveal a coherent multifunctional response of IrMn$_3$/Py and extend the conventional understanding of exchange bias beyond collinear antiferromagnetic systems. More broadly, they demonstrate the potential of non-collinear antiferromagnets as platforms for coupling magnetic, electrical, and thermoelectric functionalities in spintronic and spin-caloritronic devices.

</details>


### [19] [Native implementation of the machine-learned Skala exchange-correlation functional in CP2K: Unified one-centre reconstruction for molecular and condensed-phase calculations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34055)
*Johann Pototschnig,Franz Pöschel,Jürg Hutter,Thomas D. Kühne*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The machine-learned Skala exchange-correlation functional is implemented natively in CP2K using GPW/GAPW, validated on molecular, condensed-phase, and band-gap benchmarks with competitive accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Skala, a machine-learned XC functional, was previously limited to molecular quantum chemistry (GauXC). Extending it to periodic condensed-phase electronic structure requires an efficient plane-wave-based implementation that preserves the functional's nonlocal couplings and mixed gradient terms.

Method: Native CP2K implementation of Skala via GPW and GAPW. A joint one-centre reconstruction of density, density gradients, and kinetic-energy density before functional evaluation preserves mixed gradient terms and nonlocal smooth/atom-local couplings in all-electron and pseudopotential calculations. An XC-specific GAPW representation resolves rapidly varying local contributions on atom-centred grids, lowering plane-wave requirements. Includes Brillouin-zone sampling and point-group symmetry reduction.

Result: Retains molecular benchmark accuracy of GauXC with flexible orbital bases. All-electron Skala-D3(BJ) gives MAE of 1.54 kJ/mol vs diffusion Monte Carlo for crystalline CO2, NH3, and urea. For all thirteen DMC-ICE13 phases, MAEs are 1.16 kJ/mol (absolute lattice energies) and 0.82 kJ/mol (twelve relative energies to ice Ih). Band-gap MAE of 0.43 eV on 15 materials, competitive with hybrids. However, LC10 shows systematically underestimated equilibrium lattice constants, indicating structural overbinding.

Conclusion: The framework bridges molecular Skala to condensed-phase electronic structure, delivering competitive accuracy across molecular, lattice-energy, and band-gap benchmarks, while revealing structural overbinding and enabling future development with periodic many-body reference data.

Abstract: We implement the machine-learned Skala exchange-correlation (XC) functional natively in CP2K using its Gaussian and plane-wave (GPW) and Gaussian and augmented-plane-wave (GAPW) methods. A joint one-centre reconstruction of density, density gradients, and kinetic-energy density before functional evaluation preserves mixed gradient terms and nonlocal couplings between smooth and atom-local contributions in all-electron and pseudopotential calculations. An XC-specific GAPW representation resolves rapidly varying local contributions on atom-centred grids, reducing plane-wave requirements for pseudopotential calculations. The implementation includes Brillouin-zone sampling and point-group symmetry reduction. With sufficiently flexible orbital bases, it retains the molecular benchmark accuracy of our earlier GauXC formulation. All-electron Skala-D3(BJ) calculations yield a mean absolute error (MAE) of 1.54 kJ mol$^{-1}$ against diffusion Monte Carlo for crystalline CO$_2$, NH$_3$, and urea, spanning dispersion, quadrupolar electrostatics, and hydrogen bonding. For all thirteen DMC-ICE13 phases, the MAEs are 1.16 kJ mol$^{-1}$ for absolute lattice energies and 0.82 kJ mol$^{-1}$ for the twelve relative energies to ice Ih. At fixed experimental geometries, all-electron/mixed-core calculations give a band-gap MAE of 0.43 eV on a 15-material set, substantially below commonly used semilocal functionals and comparable to widely used hybrid functionals. The LC10 benchmark nevertheless reveals systematically underestimated equilibrium lattice constants, indicating structural overbinding. This framework connects molecular Skala to condensed-phase electronic structure and enables future development using periodic many-body reference data.

</details>


### [20] [Dopant-modulated lattice softening drives drastic thermal conductivity reduction in \b{eta}-FeSi2 thermoelectrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32261)
*Cuiping Zhang,Qingyong Ren,Yangfan Cui,Chen Chen,Songbai Hu,Shengnan Dai,Chin-Wei Wang,Wanju Luo,Dexiang Gao,Bao Yuan,Junying Shen,Fan Chen,Wei Xu,Yuting Li,Mingfang Shu,Xiaoli Huang,Pengfei Qiu,Jie Ma*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Dopant-induced lattice softening, not mass/size mismatch, drives large lattice thermal conductivity suppression in β-FeSi2; Co doping cuts κlat by ~71% at 300 K via reduced Debye temperature and weakened interatomic force constants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Suppressing lattice thermal conductivity (κlat) is pivotal for thermoelectric efficiency, but traditional strategies rely heavily on phonon scattering from mass- and size-mismatches, which limits design freedom. The authors seek an alternative, robust suppression mechanism based on dopant-induced lattice stiffness modulation.

Method: Comparative analysis of p-type (Mn) versus n-type (Co, Ir) doping in the β-FeSi2 model system. Transport data were correlated with neutron powder diffraction, heat capacity measurements, and Raman spectroscopy to probe lattice expansion, Debye temperature, and vibrational behavior.

Result: Co and Ir doping significantly reduce κlat, with Co achieving a ~71% reduction at 300 K even without significant mass and size contrast. The mechanism is evidenced by anomalous lattice expansion, substantial reduction in Debye temperature, and marked vibrational redshift and broadening, indicating atomic-scale lattice softening and weakened interatomic force constants that lower phonon group velocities and amplify anharmonic scattering.

Conclusion: Lattice stiffness manipulation is established as a powerful strategy for thermal management, offering a distinct design pathway beyond traditional mass- and strain-fluctuation models.

Abstract: Suppressing lattice thermal conductivity (\k{appa}lat) is pivotal for thermoelectric efficiency. While traditional strategies rely heavily on phonon scattering from mass- and size-mismatches, we demonstrate a robust \k{appa}lat suppression mechanism driven by dopant-induced lattice stiffness modulation. Through a comparative analysis of p-type (Mn) and n-type (Co, Ir) doping in the \b{eta}-FeSi2 model system, we show that Co and Ir doping significantly reduce \k{appa}lat. Notably, Co doping achieves a ~71% reduction at 300 K even without significant mass and size contrast. By correlating transport data with neutron powder diffraction, heat capacity, and Raman spectroscopy, we reveal anomalous lattice expansion, a substantial reduction in Debye temperature, and marked vibrational redshift and broadening. These systematic changes provide strong evidence for atomic-scale lattice softening and a fundamental weakening of interatomic force constants, which synergistically lower phonon group velocities and amplify anharmonic scattering. Our findings establish lattice stiffness manipulation as a powerful strategy for thermal management, offering a distinct design pathway beyond traditional mass- and strain-fluctuation models.

</details>


### [21] [Electro-Thermal Origins of Enhanced Performance and Scalability in Disc-Type Phase-Change Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32654)
*Ghazi Sarwat Syed,Loris Coccia,Siddharth Gautam,Vara Prasad Jonnalagadda,Abu Sebastian*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study builds a comparative analytical framework using compact electro-thermal models to analyze state-dependent readout and programming behavior of disc-type phase-change memory devices for analog in-memory computing, revealing underlying physical mechanisms and optimization directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disc-type phase-change memory is a promising architecture for analog in-memory computing, owing to ultra-thin phase-change materials and device geometry. A clearer physical understanding of its state-dependent behavior is needed to guide further optimization.

Method: A comparative analytical framework based on compact electro-thermal models is developed to examine the state-dependent readout and programming behavior of disc-type phase-change memory devices.

Result: The framework provides insight into the fundamental physical mechanisms responsible for the devices' enhanced functionality and identifies promising directions for device optimization and performance improvement.

Conclusion: Compact electro-thermal modeling offers a useful comparative tool for understanding and improving disc-type phase-change memory toward analog in-memory computing applications.

Abstract: Disc-type phase-change memory is a promising device architecture for analog in-memory computing, enabled by the unique physical properties of ultra-thin phase-change materials and the device geometry. In this study, we develop a comparative analytical framework using compact electro-thermal models to examine the state dependent readout and programming behavior of these devices. The framework provides insight into the fundamental physical mechanisms responsible for their enhanced functionality and highlights promising directions for further device optimization and performance improvement.

</details>


### [22] [Chemical site bases and average-atom potentials for the atomic cluster expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34869)
*Lorenzo Piersante,Anirudh Raju Natarajan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper revisits multicomponent ACE with arbitrary chemical site bases, derives an exact mapping between fitted linear ACE and average-atom potentials, and benchmarks occupational, Chebyshev, and conventional bases, showing the occupational basis is most data-efficient for scarce-data alloy modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atomic cluster expansion (ACE) conventionally encodes chemical degrees of freedom with a one-hot representation, producing chemically stratified interatomic potentials. Alternative chemical representations used in on-lattice configurational cluster expansions have not been evaluated for interatomic potentials, leaving the choice of chemical basis—and its impact on data efficiency and disordered-alloy description—unexamined.

Method: The authors formulate multicomponent ACE for an arbitrary chemical site basis and establish an exact analytical mapping between a fitted linear ACE and the average-atom potential describing a perfectly random alloy. They then benchmark potentials built on three bases—occupational, Chebyshev, and conventional ACE—against solute binding and vacancy formation energies in Mg-Nd, and mixing enthalpies of Mo-Nb and Cr-W solid solutions, across scarce- and large-data regimes.

Result: When training data are scarce, the occupational basis converges fastest and provides the best control over targeted material properties, whereas the conventional and Chebyshev bases struggle to reproduce these properties. The occupational basis also yields the most reliable average-atom description of disordered alloy thermodynamics. In the large-data limit, all three bases perform identically.

Conclusion: The chemical basis is a design choice governing data efficiency rather than an inherent modeling constraint. Its explicit treatment enables average-atom potentials for the thermodynamic, mechanical, and kinetic properties of concentrated alloys.

Abstract: Interatomic potentials are central tools in the atomistic modeling of materials. The atomic cluster expansion (ACE) parameterizes such potentials from ab initio data, conventionally encoding the chemical degrees of freedom with a one-hot representation that yields chemically stratified models. The alternative chemical representations used in on-lattice configurational cluster expansions have not been assessed for interatomic potentials. Here we revisit the multicomponent ACE for an arbitrary chemical site basis. We then establish an exact analytical mapping between a fitted linear ACE and the average-atom potential that describes a perfectly random alloy. We benchmark potentials built on the occupational, Chebyshev, and conventional ACE bases against solute binding and vacancy formation energies in Mg-Nd, and against the mixing enthalpies of the Mo-Nb and Cr-W solid solutions. When training data are scarce, the occupational basis converges fastest and offers the best control over targeted material properties, while the conventional and Chebyshev bases face challenges in reproducing these properties. The occupational basis likewise yields the most reliable average-atom description of disordered alloy thermodynamics. In the large-data limit the three bases perform identically. The chemical basis is therefore a design choice that governs data efficiency. Its explicit treatment opens a route to average-atom potentials for the thermodynamic, mechanical, and kinetic properties of concentrated alloys.

</details>


### [23] [Site-Selective Yttrium Substitution in Ti3AlC2 Enables Interlayer Engineering and Li Transport in Ti3C2Tx cathodes for High-Power Energy Storage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32707)
*Tetiana Boichuk,Andrii Boichuk,Mahesh Eledath Changarath,Joao Fonseca,Said Agouram,Marie Finas,Alejandro Molina-Sanchez,Juan F. Sanchez-Royo*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Precursor-level Y doping of Ti3AlC2 MAX yields Y-doped Ti3C2Tx MXene with expanded interlayer spacing and enhanced Li-ion transport, delivering high-power cathode performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MXenes derived from MAX phases often suffer from limited interlayer spacing and sluggish ion transport. A strategy is needed to tune the interlayer structure at a fundamental level, rather than modifying the MXene after synthesis.

Method: Substitutional doping of the Ti3AlC2 MAX precursor with a small amount of Y during top-down MAX preparation, producing permanent structural changes retained in the resulting Ti3C2Tx MXene. DFT calculations were used to identify the preferred Y incorporation site, complemented by XRD, XPS, and TEM characterization.

Result: DFT identified the outer Ti(4f) site (facing the Al layer) as the preferred Y incorporation site under Al-excess conditions, consistent with lattice expansion and carbide-like Y bonding. Y incorporation increased hydroxyl surface terminations, expanding interlayer spacing. The Y-doped Ti3C2Tx cathode showed diffusion-controlled storage with Li diffusion coefficient of 10-9-10-11 cm2/s, ~130 mAh/g at 0.2 C, 74 mAh/g after 1000 cycles at 2 C with nearly 100% Coulombic efficiency, and power density up to 3970 W/kg.

Conclusion: Substitutional doping of the MAX precursor is a promising strategy for controlling MXene interlayer structure and improving ion transport, offering a new route to high-power energy-storage electrodes.

Abstract: In this work, we introduce a precursor-level strategy by substitutionally doping the Ti3AlC2 MAX precursor with a small amount of Yttrium during top-down MAX preparation. This allows permanent structural changes that are retained in the resulting Ti3C2Tx MXene. Density functional theory (DFT) calculations identify the outer Ti(4f) site, facing the Al layer, as the preferred incorporation site for Y under the Al-excess synthesis conditions used here, rather than the inner Ti(2a) or Al(2b) sites. This site-specific substitution is consistent with the experimentally observed lattice expansion and carbide-like Y bonding and provides a microscopic basis for the structural changes retained in the resulting Ti3C2Tx MXene. Y incorporation expands the MXene interlayer spacing due to an increase in the amount of hydroxyl surface terminations, creating more favorable pathways for lithium-ion transport. Comprehensive structural characterization (including XRD, XPS and TEM) confirms these modifications. As a result, the Y-doped Ti3C2Tx cathode shows diffusion-controlled charge storage with a Li diffusion coefficient of 10-9-10-11 cm2/s. It delivers a reversible capacity of about 130 mAh/g at 0.2 C and retains 74 mAh/g after 1000 cycles at 2 C with nearly 100% Coulombic efficiency. The electrode also achieves a high power density of up to 3970 W/kg, outperforming the undoped MXene and previously reported multilayered MXene cathodes. These results show that substitutional doping of the MAX precursor is a promising strategy for controlling the interlayer structure of MXenes and improving ion transport, providing a new route for the development of high-power energy-storage electrodes.

</details>


### [24] [Density functional perturbation theory of meta-generalized gradient approximations using algorithmic differentiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35572)
*Bruno Ploumhans,Niklas Frederik Schmitz,Michael F. Herbst*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The authors implement density functional perturbation theory (DFPT) for exchange-correlation functionals depending on both density ρ and kinetic energy density τ (meta-GGAs and beyond), replacing cumbersome analytic second-derivative expressions with a Jacobian-vector product evaluated via algorithmic differentiation (AD), yielding a general "AD-DFPT" framework for arbitrary derivatives at the meta-GGA level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DFPT is a standard tool for computing derivatives in plane-wave DFT, but its extension to functionals with explicit τ dependence (meta-GGAs) is hindered by the need to derive and implement complicated XC second energy derivatives. A general, derivation-free route is needed to obtain response properties and, more unusually, the functional derivatives required for gradient-based training of new XC functionals.

Method: DFPT for XC functionals E_xc(ρ,τ) with explicit ρ and τ dependence is implemented. The XC second derivatives are recast as a Jacobian-vector product of the XC potentials, which is evaluated with algorithmic differentiation (AD) rather than hand-derived expressions. This is integrated into the previously developed AD-DFPT framework, giving access to derivatives of arbitrary ground-state quantities with respect to arbitrary perturbations.

Result: AD-DFPT is used to compute a range of response properties for ZnO and BaTiO3; the recent r2SCAN01 meta-GGA generally outperforms LDA and PBE. As a further demonstration, a neural-network meta-GGA is optimized to self-consistently reproduce hybrid-DFT reference densities of bulk silicon using AD-DFPT gradients.

Conclusion: AD-DFPT provides a versatile, general route for computing DFT derivatives at the meta-GGA level, applicable both to standard response properties and to the unusual derivatives needed for gradient-based training of novel XC functionals.

Abstract: Density functional perturbation theory (DFPT) is an established framework for the computation of derivatives in plane-wave density functional theory. We present an implementation of DFPT for exchange-correlation (XC) functionals $E_\mathrm{xc}(ρ,τ)$ that incorporate an explicit dependence on both the density $ρ$ and the kinetic energy density $τ$. This covers the popular class of semilocal meta-generalized gradient approximations (meta-GGAs) as well as broader nonlocal parametrizations. We sidestep the derivation of cumbersome XC second energy derivative expressions by recasting these derivatives as a Jacobian-vector product of the XC potentials, which we evaluate with algorithmic differentiation (AD) techniques. Integration with our previously developed AD-DFPT framework [N. F. Schmitz et al., npj Comput. Mater. 12, 6 (2026)] provides access to derivatives of arbitrary ground state quantities with respect to arbitrary perturbations. We employ AD-DFPT to compute a range of response properties for ZnO and BaTiO3, and find that the recent r2SCAN01 meta-GGA functional generally outperforms LDA and PBE. Finally, we showcase the optimization of a neural-network meta-GGA to self-consistently reproduce hybrid-DFT reference densities of bulk silicon, using AD-DFPT gradients. Overall, these results establish AD-DFPT as a versatile route for computing DFT derivatives at the meta-GGA level, be they common response properties or the unusual derivatives required for the gradient-based training of novel XC functionals.

</details>


### [25] [Phase-selective growth of water-soluble strontium aluminate via sputtering deposition for freestanding oxide membranes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32760)
*Evgenios Stylianidis,Marie Dallocchio,Bernat Mundet,Lucia Varbaro,Clementine Thibault,Christo Guguschev,Mario Brützam,Javier Herrero,Marta Gibert*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Water-soluble epitaxial Sr3Al2O6 and Sr4Al2O7 films are grown by sputtering via atmosphere control, enabling ferromagnetic freestanding Sm2NiMnO6 membranes with epi-film-like quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Freestanding complex-oxide membranes typically rely on water-soluble sacrificial layers; a sputtering-based route with phase selectivity would broaden accessible sacrificial materials and membrane fabrication.

Method: Sputter deposition of strontium aluminate films; systematic study of structural quality versus growth conditions; selective stabilization of cubic Sr3Al2O6 and tetragonal Sr4Al2O7 from a single target by varying growth atmosphere; use as water-soluble sacrificial layers to release ferromagnetic Sm2NiMnO6 membranes; characterization by X-ray diffraction, synchrotron spectroscopic techniques, and SQUID magnetometry.

Result: High-quality epitaxial Sr3Al2O6 and Sr4Al2O7 films were obtained, with phase selection controlled by growth atmosphere using one target. The resulting freestanding ferromagnetic Sm2NiMnO6 membranes exhibit properties comparable to epitaxial films.

Conclusion: Sputtering provides a versatile, phase-selective route to water-soluble strontium aluminate sacrificial layers, enabling high-quality freestanding ferromagnetic oxide membranes.

Abstract: We report the epitaxial growth of high-quality water-soluble strontium aluminate films via sputtering deposition.
