We systematically investigate the dependence of the films structural quality on the growth conditions and we show that we can selectively grow, using the same target, both the cubic Sr3Al2O6 and the tetragonal Sr4Al2O7 phases by varying the atmosphere during growth. Using these water-soluble sacrificial layers we then synthesize ferromagnetic freestanding Sm2NiMnO6 membranes. By means of X-ray diffraction, synchrotron spectroscopic techniques and SQUID magnetometry, we demonstrate that our freestanding films exhibit properties comparable to epitaxial films.

</details>


### [26] [Digital Etching of Single-Crystalline Silicon Fin Barriers Down to Sub-10 nm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32826)
*Yu Wu,Teun A. J. van Schijndel,Anthony P. McFadden,Wilson J. Yánez-Parreño,Raymond W. Simmonds,Christopher J. Palmstrøm*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A fabrication process combining anisotropic wet etching with digital (RTA-oxidation/VHF-etch) thinning yields sub-10 nm-thick, high-aspect-ratio Si fins (down to 6 nm) with reduced tapering, enabling single-crystal Si dielectric barriers for superconducting qubits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable scalable, reproducible superconducting qubit fabrication using single-crystal Si dielectric barriers for Josephson junctions, a method is needed to produce ultra-thin (sub-10 nm), high-aspect-ratio Si fins with controlled profile from readily available Si processing technology.

Method: High-resistivity float-zone single-crystal Si wafers were patterned and subjected to anisotropic wet etching to form fins, followed by a digital etching process of repeated rapid thermal annealing (RTA) oxidation and vapor-phase hydrofluoric acid (VHF) etching of the resulting oxide to thin the fins. The RTA oxidation ambient and ramp rate were tuned (N2/O2 5:1 with reduced ramp rate) to reduce fin tapering.

Result: Cross-sectional scanning transmission electron microscopy confirmed fin thicknesses as thin as 6 nm, though with a tapered profile from the initial wet etch. Using an N2/O2 (5:1) ambient and reduced ramp rate during RTA oxidation reduced tapering, achieving 3 nm thickness variation along a 1.2 um tall fin (12 nm near the top, 15 nm near the base).

Conclusion: The developed process provides a pathway for fabricating Josephson junctions and lays a foundation toward reproducible and scalable superconducting qubit fabrication with single-crystal Si dielectric barriers using readily available Si processing technology.

Abstract: This work presents a fabrication process to realize sub-10 nm-thick, high-aspect-ratio Si fins using high-resistivity float-zone single-crystal Si wafers. Following anisotropic wet etching, a digital etching process based on repeated rapid thermal annealing (RTA) oxidation and vapor-phase hydrofluoric acid (VHF) etching of the resulting oxide was developed, enabling thinning of Si fins into the sub-10 nm regime. Cross-sectional scanning transmission electron microscopy confirms fin thicknesses as thin as 6 nm, although with a tapered profile resulting from the initial anisotropic wet etch. Tapering of the fin was reduced by using an N{_2}/O{_2} (5:1) ambient together with a reduced ramp rate during the temperature ramping for the RTA oxidation process. A thickness variation of 3 nm along a 1.2 μm tall fin was achieved on a fin with 12 nm near the top and 15 nm near the base. The developed process provides a pathway for fabricating Josephson junctions, setting a foundation toward reproducible and scalable superconducting qubit fabrication with single-crystal Si dielectric barriers using readily available Si processing technology.

</details>


### [27] [Parity-selected Bethe-Salpeter equation: symmetry-adapted regularization of the exciton-phonon self-energy in polar semiconductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32909)
*Michael O. Atambo*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The intra-1s exciton-phonon scattering vertex in polar materials suffers a Fröhlich divergence, which the authors show is analytically cancelled by electron-hole interference: the vertex factors exactly as q·G(q) with G(0) ∝ (m_h − m_e)/(m_h + m_e), vanishing for equal masses. A multipole interference factor selects which inter-state channels retain the singularity. This enables a parity-selected truncation of the exciton-phonon self-energy, converging with 10²–10³ fewer q-points and revealing that the regularized intra-1s channel governs the temperature-dependent exciton shift in mass-asymmetric polar semiconductors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite-temperature Bethe-Salpeter calculations of exciton-phonon self-energies in polar materials are bottlenecked by the Fröhlich divergence of the intra-1s scattering vertex, which forces prohibitive numerical sampling and obscures the underlying physics.

Method: The authors derive an exact analytic factorization of the intra-1s scattering vertex, V_el(q) = q·G(q), with G(0) ∝ (m_h − m_e)/(m_h + m_e), showing the divergence is removed by electron-hole interference and vanishes identically under parity protection for equal masses. They introduce a multipole interference factor I_n = β_e^n − (−β_h)^n that identifies where the singularity persists, i.e., only in dipole-allowed inter-state channels. Based on this, they construct a parity-selected truncation of the exciton-phonon self-energy in which the intra-1s channel is treated exactly at negligible cost.

Result: The parity-selected truncation converges with 10²–10³ fewer q-points than conventional approaches. The regularized intra-1s channel—previously hidden by numerical cutoffs—is found to control the temperature-dependent exciton shift in mass-asymmetric polar semiconductors.

Conclusion: Electron-hole interference analytically eliminates the Fröhlich divergence in the intra-1s exciton-phonon vertex, yielding an efficient parity-selected scheme and identifying the regularized intra-1s channel as the key driver of temperature-dependent exciton shifts in mass-asymmetric polar semiconductors.

Abstract: Finite-temperature Bethe-Salpeter calculations of exciton-phonon self-energies in polar materials are bottlenecked by the Fröhlich divergence of the intra-1s scattering vertex. We show that for a bound electron-hole pair this divergence is analytically eliminated by electron-hole interference: the intra-1s vertex admits the exact factorization $V_{\mathrm{el}}(q)=q\,\mathcal G(q)$ with $\mathcal G(0)\propto(m_h-m_e)/(m_h+m_e)$, vanishing identically for equal masses (parity protection). A multipole interference factor $\mathcal I_n=β_e^{\,n}-(-β_h)^{n}$ determines where the singularity survives-only in dipole-allowed inter-state channels. We formulate a parity-selected truncation of the exciton-phonon self-energy in which the intra-1s channel is evaluated exactly at negligible cost, converging with $10^2$-$10^3$ fewer $q$-points. We find that the regularized intra-1s channel, previously obscured by numerical cutoffs, controls the temperature-dependent exciton shift in mass-asymmetric polar semiconductors.

</details>


### [28] [Pressure-induced s,p-d electron redistribution accompanies structural transformation in amorphous Zr-Cu alloy under compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35380)
*P. Dziegielewski,J. Antonowicz,K. Georgarakis,O. Lord,M. Amboage,D. Daisenberger,L. Clough,T. Irifune,T. Shinmei*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Extreme compression can drive electrons from extended s,p orbitals into compact d orbitals. Combining X-ray absorption measurements up to 69 GPa with MD and DFT, this study shows pressure-induced s,p-d electronic redistribution in amorphous Zr67Cu33 metallic glass, dominated by Zr and linked to its anomalous structural evolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extreme compression can reorganize atomic valence states, in some regimes driving charge from spatially extended orbitals into more compact ones. The authors aim to test whether such pressure-induced electronic redistribution occurs in amorphous Zr67Cu33 metallic glass and whether it underlies the alloy's previously reported anomalous structural evolution.

Method: High-pressure X-ray absorption fine structure (XAFS) measurements, including Zr K-edge XANES and EXAFS, were performed up to 69 GPa. These were combined with molecular dynamics simulations and density functional theory calculations. MD simulations reproduced the measured EXAFS spectra and provided reliable structural input for electronic-structure calculations, which used Mulliken populations and the density of states.

Result: The Zr K-edge XANES spectra show a pronounced increase in pre-edge intensity and a change in its pressure dependence, whereas the Cu response is markedly weaker. Calculated Mulliken populations reveal pressure-induced depletion of Zr s and p states with a simultaneous increase in d-state population, while the density of states indicates enhanced p-d hybridization under compression.

Conclusion: The results provide experimental and computational evidence for pressure-induced s,p-d electronic redistribution in zirconium, and link this effect to the previously reported anomalous structural evolution of the compressed amorphous alloy.

Abstract: Extreme compression can reorganize atomic valence states, in some regimes driving charge from spatially extended orbitals into more compact ones. We investigate this effect in amorphous Zr67Cu33 metallic glass by combining high-pressure X-ray absorption fine structure measurements up to 69 GPa with molecular dynamics simulations and density functional theory calculations. The Zr K-edge XANES spectra show a pronounced increase in the pre-edge intensity and a change in its pressure dependence, whereas the Cu response is markedly weaker. Molecular-dynamics simulations reproduce the measured EXAFS spectra and provide reliable input for the electronic-structure calculations. The calculated Mulliken populations reveal pressure-induced depletion of Zr s and p-states, accompanied by an increase of the d-state population, while the density of states indicates enhanced p-d hybridization under compression. These results provide experimental and computational evidence for pressure-induced s,p-d electronic redistribution in zirconium and link this effect to the previously reported anomalous structural evolution of the compressed amorphous alloy.

</details>


### [29] [Impact of Phonon-Magnon Scattering on Thermal Transport across Antiferromagnetic and Ferromagnetic Oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32954)
*Zeyu Xiang,Bolin Liao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Spin-lattice dynamics combined with first-principles calculations reveal that phonon-magnon coupling in antiferromagnetic NiO and MnO depends on the match between phonon and magnon energy scales, with antiferromagnets supporting larger scattering phase space than ferromagnets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phonon-magnon coupling is well studied in ferromagnets, but its microscopic mechanisms in antiferromagnets remain poorly understood. The authors aim to clarify how spin dynamics affect phonon thermal transport in antiferromagnetic systems and how magnetic order influences this coupling.

Method: First-principles calculations are combined with spin-lattice dynamics to study phonon-magnon interactions in antiferromagnetic NiO and MnO, which are compared with ferromagnetic EuO having the same crystal structure. Green-Kubo and spectral energy density analyses are used to quantify phonon thermal conductivity and lifetimes. A simplified phase-space model is also employed.

Result: Spin dynamics reduce phonon thermal conductivity and lifetimes, with the strongest effects in MnO. Spin-induced phonon scattering is highly sensitive to the relative phonon and magnon energy scales. The contrasting behavior between MnO and EuO shows that magnetic order matters, and for comparable phonon and magnon energy scales, the antiferromagnetic case supports a larger phonon-magnon scattering phase space, indicating more kinematically allowed scattering channels than in the ferromagnetic case.

Conclusion: Excitation energy scales and magnetic order are identified as key factors governing phonon-magnon scattering in magnetic materials, providing microscopic insight into spin-lattice coupling in antiferromagnets.

Abstract: Phonon--magnon coupling has been extensively studied in ferromagnets, whereas its microscopic mechanisms in antiferromagnets remain less understood. Here, we combine first-principles calculations with spin--lattice dynamics to investigate phonon--magnon interactions and their impact on thermal transport in antiferromagnetic NiO and MnO, which are put in contrast to the ferromagnetic EuO with an identical crystal structure. Green--Kubo and spectral energy density analyses show that spin dynamics reduce phonon thermal conductivity and lifetimes, with the strongest effects in MnO. Comparison of the excitation spectra indicates that spin-induced phonon scattering depends sensitively on the relative phonon and magnon energy scales, while the contrasting behavior of MnO and EuO further highlights the role of magnetic order. A simplified phase-space model further illustrates that, for comparable phonon and magnon energy scales, the antiferromagnetic case can support a larger phonon--magnon scattering phase space than the ferromagnetic case, suggesting a greater number of kinematically allowed scattering channels. These results identify excitation energy scales and magnetic order as key factors governing phonon--magnon scattering in magnetic materials.

</details>


### [30] [Piezo-Hall effect in $\mathrm{RbCr_2Se_2O}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33026)
*San-Dong Guo,Shi-Hao Zhang,Feng-Ren Fan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper proposes the "piezo-Hall effect," where strain transforms a magnetic material's magnetic space group from one that forbids a net magnetic moment (and thus suppresses the anomalous Hall effect) to one that permits it, thereby enabling the AHE. Using first-principles calculations on the d-wave altermagnetic metal RbCr₂Se₂O, it shows that uniaxial strain along x and y yields anomalous Hall conductivities of opposite sign but equal magnitude. The concept is extended to piezo-magneto-optical, electro-Hall, electro-magneto-optical, and temperature Hall effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The anomalous Hall effect (AHE) is fundamentally important for probing spin-orbit coupling and magnetic transport, and strain is known to strongly modify material properties (e.g., the piezoelectric effect). The authors aim to unify these two ingredients by exploring how strain-induced changes in the magnetic space group can switch the AHE on, thereby advancing understanding of the coupling between strain and physical properties.

Method: The authors define the piezo-Hall effect in terms of magnetic space groups (MSGs): a pristine MSG forbids a net magnetic moment and suppresses the AHE, while strain drives a transition to a new MSG that permits a net moment, enabling the AHE. The proposed mechanisms are strain-induced magnetic electronic state transition and strain tuning of magnetization direction. They verify the scenario with first-principles calculations on the d-wave altermagnetic metal RbCr₂Se₂O.

Result: First-principles calculations on RbCr₂Se₂O confirm the piezo-Hall scenario. Notably, under identical uniaxial strain applied along the x and y directions, the anomalous Hall conductivity exhibits opposite signs but equal magnitudes. The framework is further generalized: replacing strain tuning with electric-field modulation yields the electro-Hall and electro-magneto-optical effects, and temperature can act as the external field to give a temperature Hall effect.

Conclusion: The work establishes the piezo-Hall effect as a strain-driven, magnetic-space-group-based mechanism for turning the AHE on in materials whose pristine symmetry forbids it, validated in the altermagnet RbCr₂Se₂O. It generalizes this idea to piezo-magneto-optical, electro-Hall, electro-magneto-optical, and temperature Hall effects, advancing the understanding of coupling between strain and physical properties.

Abstract: The anomalous Hall effect (AHE) is of fundamental importance for understanding spin-orbit coupling and magnetic transport in magnetic materials. Meanwhile, strain can significantly modify material properties, and the piezoelectric effect stands as a well-known representative. The piezo-Hall effect combines the two physical ingredients of AHE and strain. Specifically, the piezo-Hall effect describes a physical process in which the magnetic space group (MSG) of a magnetic material prohibits a net magnetic moment and suppresses the AHE in the pristine state but transforms into a new MSG upon strain application that permits a net magnetic moment, thereby enabling the emergence of the AHE. The underlying mechanisms cover strain-induced magnetic electronic state transition and strain tuning of magnetization direction. We take the $d$-wave altermagnetic metal as an example to analyze the piezo-Hall effect in detail, with first-principles calculations carried out on the specific material $\mathrm{RbCr_2Se_2O}$ to verify this scenario. A notable result is that under identical uniaxial strain applied along the $x$ and $y$ directions, the anomalous Hall conductivity exhibits opposite signs but equal magnitudes. Similarly, the piezo-magneto-optical effect can also be proposed, and if strain tuning is replaced by electric-field modulation, the electro-Hall effect and electro-magneto-optical effect can be also put forward accordingly. Even temperature can serve as an external field to introduce the temperature Hall effect. Our work advances the understanding of the coupling between strain and physical properties.

</details>


### [31] [Unveiling Lattice Dynamics and a Hidden Structural Transition in the 2D Ferromagnet AgVP$_2$Se$_6$ via Raman Spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33116)
*Thi Hai Yen Pham,Po-hao Chang,Aidan C. Malloy,Seng Huat Lee,Zhiqiang Mao,Maria F. Munoz,Riccardo Torsi,Angela R. Hight Walker,Igor Mazin,Patrick M. Vora*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Polarization- and temperature-dependent Raman plus magneto-Raman spectroscopy of AgVP2Se6 single crystals, supported by DFT, reveals weak spin-phonon coupling across the ferromagnetic transition, a possible structural transition near 100 K, and reconciles the transport gap with the optical absorption edge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AgVP2Se6 is a rare quaternary transition-metal chalcophosphate ferromagnet with a triangular V sublattice, contrasting with the antiferromagnetic zigzag chains of AgVP2S6. Prior Raman studies were performed far above the Curie temperature, so spin-phonon coupling and low-temperature lattice dynamics remained unexplored.

Method: Single crystals were grown by chemical vapor transport (CVT) and flux methods. The authors performed polarization-resolved, temperature-dependent Raman and magneto-Raman spectroscopy across the ferromagnetic transition, with angle-resolved measurements for symmetry assignment, complemented by DFT calculations of phonon, optical, and magnetic properties.

Result: Cryogenic Raman spectra resolve up to thirty peaks with symmetry assignments. Spectral differences between CVT- and flux-grown crystals are attributed to different interlayer stacking domains. Temperature- and field-dependent spectra remain largely unchanged across TC, indicating weak spin-phonon coupling, while pronounced spectral alterations near 100 K suggest a potential structural phase transition. DFT reconciles the transport activation gap (~0.325 eV) with the optical absorption edge (~2.14 eV), attributing the former to a transition to the V-d upper Hubbard band that is optically dark but thermally accessible, and offers a theoretical explanation for the differing magnetic properties of the sulfide and selenide.

Conclusion: The findings provide key information on the lattice dynamics of AgVP2Se6 and will guide future applications in spin-based electronics.

Abstract: Transition-metal chalcophosphates (TMCs) are two-dimensional (2D) van der Waals materials supporting a broad range of electronic and magnetic properties. Among quaternary TMCs, AgVP$_2$Se$_6$ is a rare ferromagnet (FM) with a triangular V sublattice, in contrast to the antiferromagnetic (AFM) zigzag chains of the sulfide AgVP$_2$S$_6$. Prior Raman studies of AgVP$_2$Se$_6$ have been performed far above the Curie temperature ($T_C$), preventing studies of spin-phonon coupling. Here, we report polarization-resolved, temperature-dependent Raman and magneto-Raman spectroscopy of AgVP$_2$Se$_6$ single crystals grown by chemical vapor transport (CVT) and flux methods, across the FM transition, complemented by density functional theory (DFT) calculations of phonon, optical and magnetic properties. Cryogenic Raman spectra resolve up to thirty peaks, while angle-resolved measurements enable their symmetry assignment. Spectral differences between CVT- and flux-grown crystals are traced back to the presence of different interlayer stacking domains in these samples. Temperature- and field-dependent Raman spectra remain largely unchanged across $T_C$, suggesting weak spin-phonon coupling. However, pronounced spectral alterations near 100 K suggest a potential structural phase transition. Our DFT calculations further reconcile the large discrepancy between the transport activation gap ($\approx$ 0.325 eV) and optical absorption edge ($\approx$ 2.14 eV), attributing the former to a transition to the V-d upper Hubbard band that is optically dark but thermally accessible. We also suggest a theoretical explanation of the qualitative difference (reproduced by DFT calculations) between the magnetic properties of the sulfide and selenide. These results provide key information regarding the lattice dynamics of AgVP$_2$Se$_6$ and will guide future applications in spin-based electronics.

</details>


### [32] [Symmetry-guided Design Principles for Spin Splitting and Hall Transport in Orthorhombic Altermagnetic Perovskites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33130)
*Rasmita Kumari,Bishal Das,Aftab Alam*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A symmetry framework combining spin/magnetic group theory with first-principles calculations links the magnetic order's irreducible representation to altermagnetic spin splitting, SOC-induced canting, and anomalous Hall conductivity in orthorhombic Pbnm perovskites, validated in eight oxides.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnets generate momentum-dependent spin-split bands even without SOC, but a predictive, symmetry-based understanding connecting magnetic order to spin splitting and transport in a broad material family is lacking.

Method: The authors combine spin and magnetic group theory with first-principles calculations to build a unified symmetry framework for collinear antiferromagnets in orthorhombic Pbnm perovskites, then validate predictions on eight experimentally realized perovskite oxides in insulating and metallic regimes.

Result: The magnetic order's irreducible representation uniquely determines the momentum-space planes of altermagnetic spin splitting, the effective low-energy Hamiltonian, SOC-induced weak ferromagnetic canting orientation, and allowed anomalous Hall conductivity tensor components. LaTiO3 and CaCrO3 show sizable AHCs of ~38 and 205 S/cm despite nearly vanishing net magnetization; SOC gaps symmetry-protected band crossings producing large Berry curvature, while the Hall response is rooted in non-relativistic spin splitting.

Conclusion: The work provides a predictive symmetry-based framework for discovering and engineering altermagnetic materials with tunable spin-dependent electronic and transport properties.

Abstract: Magnetic symmetry can generate momentum-dependent spin-split electronic bands even in the absence of spin-orbit coupling (SOC), giving rise to the recently discovered class of altermagnets. Here, we establish a unified symmetry framework for collinear antiferromagnets in orthorhombic Pbnm perovskites by combining spin and magnetic group theory with first-principles calculations. We show that the irreducible representation of the magnetic order uniquely determines the momentum-space planes supporting altermagnetic spin splitting, the form of the effective low-energy Hamiltonian, the orientation of SOC-induced weak ferromagnetic canting, and the allowed anomalous Hall conductivity (AHC) tensor components. These predictions are validated in eight experimentally realized orthorhombic perovskite oxides spanning both insulating and metallic regimes. In particular, LaTiO3 and CaCrO3 exhibit sizable anomalous Hall conductivities of approximately 38 and 205 S/cm, respectively, despite nearly vanishing net magnetization. We further show that SOC gaps symmetry-protected altermagnetic band crossings, generating large Berry curvature, while the Hall response is fundamentally rooted in the underlying non-relativistic spin splitting. Our work establishes a predictive symmetry-based framework for discovering and engineering altermagnetic materials with tunable spin-dependent electronic and transport properties.

</details>


### [33] [Low-temperature thermal expansion of the $α$ and $ω$ phases of titanium](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33302)
*Norimasa Nishiyama,Atsushi Togo,Yoshitaka Matsushita*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Low-temperature thermal expansion of α-Ti and high-pressure ω-Ti was measured (5-300 K) and computed, revealing similar volumetric but markedly different axial behavior; the α-Ti c-axis negative expansion is smaller than previously reported.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To elucidate the low-temperature thermal expansion behavior and anisotropy of α-Ti and its high-pressure polymorph ω-Ti, and to resolve discrepancies with prior single-crystal length measurements.

Method: Single-phase bulk polycrystalline ω-Ti synthesized at 7.7 GPa and 500 °C, recovered to ambient pressure; X-ray diffraction from ~5 to 300 K; first-principles calculations; Debye-Grüneisen analysis.

Result: Both phases show similar volumetric thermal expansion but very different axial responses. α-Ti c-axis expansion coefficient turns negative below ~50 K, minimum -0.71×10⁻⁶ K⁻¹ at 20 K, much smaller than prior single-crystal results. ω-Ti expands nearly isotropically with c/a nearly temperature-independent, near ideal bcc-derived value; α-Ti c/a decreases with temperature. Debye temperatures: 427±5 K (α-Ti) and 409±12 K (ω-Ti); effective Grüneisen parameters: 1.33±0.04 and 1.23±0.04. Calculations reproduce volumetric expansion and axial trends.

Conclusion: α-Ti and ω-Ti share similar volumetric thermal expansion but differ strongly in axial anisotropy; the α-Ti c-axis negative thermal expansion is substantially smaller than previously reported, and first-principles calculations capture the overall trends.

Abstract: We investigated the low-temperature thermal expansion of the $α$ phase and the $ω$ phase, a high-pressure polymorph of titanium, by X-ray diffraction measurements from approximately 5 to 300 K, together with first-principles calculations. Single-phase bulk polycrystalline $ω$-Ti was synthesized at 7.7 GPa and 500 $^\circ$C and recovered to ambient pressure. Although the two phases exhibit very similar volumetric thermal expansion, their axial responses are markedly different. In $α$-Ti, the $c$-axis thermal expansion coefficient becomes negative below approximately 50 K and reaches a minimum of $-0.71 \times 10^{-6}$ K$^{-1}$ at 20 K. Both experiment and theory indicate substantially smaller $c$-axis negative thermal expansion than previously reported from single-crystal length measurements. In contrast, $ω$-Ti expands nearly isotropically along the $a$ and $c$ axes, and its $c/a$ ratio remains nearly temperature independent and close to the ideal bcc-derived value. The $c$/$a$ ratio of $α$-Ti decreases with increasing temperature. Debye-Grüneisen analysis yielded Debye temperatures of $427 \pm 5$ K and $409 \pm 12$ K and effective Grüneisen parameters of $1.33 \pm 0.04$ and $1.23 \pm 0.04$ for $α$-Ti and $ω$-Ti, respectively. The first-principles calculations reproduce the volumetric thermal expansion of both phases and capture the overall trends of their axial responses.

</details>


### [34] [Temperature Periodic Modulation Doubles the Power Output of Thermoelectric Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33425)
*Dario Narducci,Antonio Mazzacua,Federico Giulio*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Periodic (non-sinusoidal) modulation of the heat-source temperature can double thermoelectric power output versus the stationary case, and the enhancement holds for real materials with full temperature-dependent properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Thermoelectric conversion efficiency remains low despite two decades of materials optimization. A largely unexplored route is dynamic heat-flux conversion; prior work showed sinusoidal source-temperature modulation raises maximum power by up to 50%. This paper aims to push that enhancement further and test its robustness beyond idealized assumptions.

Method: The authors analyze thermoelectric heat harvesting under periodic modulation of the heat-source temperature, comparing sinusoidal modulation against alternative periodic waveforms. They extend the analysis beyond the constant-property approximation by accounting for the temperature dependence of all phenomenological coefficients, i.e., for real materials.

Result: Non-sinusoidal periodic modulation outperforms sinusoidal modulation, increasing power output by up to 100% relative to the stationary case. The power enhancement is robust and persists when the temperature dependence of all phenomenological coefficients is included.

Conclusion: Dynamic modulation of the heat source offers an immediately applicable pathway to overcome the efficiency limits of thermoelectric devices, with up to 100% power gain achievable in real materials.

Abstract: Thermoelectric heat harvesting provides in principle a flexible, reliable way to convert heat into electric energy, thus recovering waste heat and powering off-net devices. However, thermoelectricity conversion efficiency is still low, despite the major efforts deployed over the last two decades to improve materials performances. A largely unexplored opportunity to increase harvesting efficiency is offered by converting heat flux dynamically. It was shown [D. Narducci et al., Mater. Today Phys. 54, 101713 (2025)] that when the temperature of the heat source is sinusoidally modulated, this leads to an increase of the maximum power by up to 50 %. In this paper we show how this approach can be further and significantly enhanced. We found that sinusoidal modulation can be outperformed by a different periodic modulation, leading to an increase of the power output by up to 100 % compared to the stationary case. Furthermore, we extended our analyses beyond the constant-property approximation, showing that the power output enhancement is robust, applying to any real material where the temperature dependence of all phenomenological coefficients is accounted for. This major power output enhancement offers a novel, immediately applicable pathway to overcome the efficiency limits of thermoelectric devices.

</details>


### [35] [Toward catalyst-mediated growth of diamond and cubic boron nitride wires](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33478)
*Rodney S. Ruoff*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A proposal to achieve reproducible catalyst-mediated growth of diamond and cubic boron nitride (cBN) wires via vapor-liquid-solid (VLS) and vapor-solid-solid (VSS) routes that change conditions between initial segment formation and continued growth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Catalyst-assisted growth has yielded carbon and boron nitride nanotubes and many nanowire types, but no reproducible method exists for sustained catalyst-mediated growth of diamond and cBN wires. Such wires are attractive because they could combine high specific stiffness and strength with heat conduction and electrical insulation.

Method: Proposes VLS and VSS routes in which temperature, feed, catalyst, or starting contact is changed between formation of the first diamond or cBN segment and its continued growth. Specific approaches include switching from carbide or boron-rich starter wires, depositing catalyst on selected facets of existing crystals, and using local solid feedstock to supply catalyst during initial growth. Historical diamond-whisker reports motivate renewed tests of diamond-metal contacts, asking whether these contacts can favor tetrahedral bonding while maintaining an atom supply to the growing wire.

Result: No experimental results are reported; the work is a proposal. It targets both single-crystal and polycrystalline products, from nanometer to micrometer and larger diameters.

Conclusion: Reproducible catalyst-mediated diamond and cBN wire growth may be achievable by decoupling nucleation from sustained growth through staged changes in temperature, feed, catalyst, or contact, and by re-examining diamond-metal contacts for their ability to sustain tetrahedral bonding and continuous atom supply.

Abstract: A reproducible method for sustained catalyst-mediated growth of diamond and cubic boron nitride (cBN) wires has yet to be established. Catalyst-assisted growth has produced carbon and boron nitride nanotubes and a wide range of elemental, compound and alloy nanowires. Diamond and cBN wires could combine high specific stiffness and strength with heat conduction and electrical insulation. I propose vapor-liquid-solid (VLS) and vapor-solid-solid (VSS) routes in which temperature, feed, catalyst or starting contact changes between formation of the first diamond or cBN segment and its continued growth. The approaches include switching from carbide or boron-rich starter wires, depositing catalyst on selected facets of existing crystals, and using local solid feedstock to supply the catalyst during initial growth. Historical diamond-whisker reports motivate renewed tests of diamond-metal contacts. The proposed experiments ask whether these contacts can favor tetrahedral bonding while maintaining a supply of atoms to the growing wire. Both single-crystal and polycrystalline products, from nanometer to micrometer and larger diameters, are of interest.

</details>


### [36] [Computational symmetry hierarchy of time-reversal-even and -odd spin Hall conductivity tensors in altermagnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33631)
*Dameul Jeong,Seoung-Hun Kang,Young-Kyun Kwon*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work separates the spin Hall conductivity (SHC) of altermagnets into time-reversal-even and time-reversal-odd channels, combining symmetry analysis with first-principles calculations to map the full 27-component SHC tensor and provide a prescreening framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting the full SHC tensor of magnetic crystals is difficult because crystallographic and magnetic symmetries constrain different parts of the response differently, and no unified scheme links these symmetries to the complete tensor.

Method: Decompose the SHC into time-reversal-even and time-reversal-odd channels, combine symmetry analysis with first-principles calculations for six representative altermagnetic compounds across distinct crystallographic classes, perform momentum-resolved analysis, and convert crystallographic and magnetic symmetry operations into linear constraints on the 27-component SHC tensor. Extend the analysis to 62 spin-split collinear antiferromagnets to build a magnetic-point-group tensor atlas.

Result: The even channel obeys crystallographic selection rules consistent with its Berry-curvature Fermi-sea origin; the odd channel is set by magnetic symmetry and can be reshaped by antiunitary operations with spatial parts from screw or glide symmetries. Momentum-resolved analysis links the even response to spin-orbit-driven avoided crossings and the odd response to anisotropic near-Fermi-level spin-current textures. The atlas reveals design limits such as the complete 13/14 tensor partition in CuF2 and the one-parameter time-odd tensor compression in MnSe.

Conclusion: A unified symmetry-based framework provides a computational prescreening route to identify symmetry-allowed spin Hall channels before dense first-principles transport calculations.

Abstract: Predicting the full spin Hall conductivity tensor of a magnetic crystal is difficult because crystallographic and magnetic symmetries do not constrain all parts of the response in the same way. Here, we separate the spin Hall conductivity of altermagnets into time-reversal-even and time-reversal-odd channels and combine symmetry analysis with first-principles calculations for six representative compounds spanning distinct crystallographic classes. The even channel follows crystallographic selection rules consistent with its Berry-curvature Fermi-sea origin. The odd channel is set by magnetic symmetry and can be reshaped by antiunitary operations whose spatial parts originate from screw or glide symmetries. Momentum-resolved analysis further links the even response to spin-orbit-driven avoided crossings and the odd response to anisotropic near-Fermi-level spin-current response textures. By converting crystallographic and magnetic symmetry operations into linear constraints on the full 27-component SHC tensor, this framework provides a computational prescreening route for identifying symmetry-allowed spin Hall channels before dense first-principles transport calculations. Extending the same analysis to 62 spin-split collinear antiferromagnets yields a magnetic-point-group tensor atlas, identifying design limits such as the complete 13/14tensor partition in CuF2 and the one-parameter time-odd tensor compression in MnSe.

</details>


### [37] [Placement-driven reversal of preferred spin alignment in monolayer hBN vacancy pairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33714)
*Daniel Hashemi*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: DFT (q = -2) shows that atomic-scale placement of two boron vacancies in monolayer hBN reverses the preferred collinear spin alignment: the closest 4.3 Å pair is ferromagnetic by 27.6 meV, while a single lattice-vector shift or a 7.5 Å pair gives antiferromagnetic alignment (50.7 meV), revealing a placement-sensitive regime of interacting defects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The negatively charged boron vacancy is an optically addressable spin centre in hBN, but existing understanding treats vacancies as isolated defects. Bringing two vacancies together introduces electronic interactions absent from the isolated-defect picture, so the coupling behavior of proximal spin centres remains unclear.

Method: Density-functional theory at total charge q = -2 for seven two-vacancy configurations in monolayer hBN, comparing pairs at different separations (4.3 Å and 7.5 Å, plus a single-lattice-vector displacement). Numerical controls test the robustness of the reference splittings, hybrid-functional calculations check the sign and magnitude of the magnetic splittings, and local distortions are analyzed to link the antiparallel preference to vacancy-state hybridization along an intervening atomic chain.

Result: The closest pair (4.3 Å) favors parallel alignment by 27.6 meV within the semilocal approximation. Moving one vacancy by one lattice vector reverses the preference, and the 7.5 Å pair favors antiparallel alignment by 50.7 meV. Numerical controls change these splittings by less than 1 meV; hybrid functionals retain the signs but substantially change the magnitudes. The closest pair shows contact-localized spin-density contrast and constrained angular energies departing from the bilinear Heisenberg form.

Conclusion: Atomic-scale placement controls the sign of inter-vacancy spin alignment in monolayer hBN, defining a placement-sensitive regime of interacting defects and providing a microscopic foundation for investigating coupled spin centres in atomically thin materials.

Abstract: The negatively charged boron vacancy is an optically addressable spin centre in hexagonal boron nitride (hBN), but bringing two vacancies together introduces electronic interactions absent from the isolated-defect picture. Here we use density-functional theory at total charge q = -2 to show that atomic-scale placement reverses the preferred collinear spin alignment in monolayer hBN. Among seven configurations, the closest pair, separated by 4.3 angstrom, favours parallel alignment by 27.6 meV within the semilocal approximation. Moving one vacancy by a single lattice vector reverses the preference, and a 7.5 angstrom pair favours antiparallel alignment by 50.7 meV. Numerical controls change these two reference splittings by less than 1 meV; hybrid-functional calculations retain their signs while substantially changing their magnitudes. Local distortions connect the antiparallel preference at 7.5 angstrom to changes in vacancy-state hybridization along an intervening atomic chain. The closest pair instead exhibits a contact-localized spin-density contrast and constrained angular energies that depart from the bilinear Heisenberg form. These results identify a placement-sensitive regime of interacting defects and provide a microscopic foundation for investigating coupled spin centres in atomically thin materials.

</details>


### [38] [Benchmark of First-Principles Titanium K-Edge X-Ray Absorption Spectral Simulations on Titanium-containing Oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33776)
*Chuntian Cao,Joshua J. Kas,Karol Dyro,Bruce Ravel,John Vinson,Deyu Lu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work benchmarks first-principles Ti K-edge XANES simulations against experiments for nine titanium compounds, showing that quadrupole excitations and thermal disorder are essential for pre-edge features and many-body shake-up shapes the main/post-edge regions, yielding high similarity with experiment except for a missing BaTiO3 shoulder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: XAS is a powerful element-specific probe of local structure and electronic properties, but quantitative interpretation is difficult and requires accurate first-principles spectral simulations. A systematic assessment of which physical effects must be included for reliable Ti K-edge XANES is needed.

Method: First-principles simulations of Ti K-edge XANES for nine common titanium compounds are benchmarked against experimental data. Key physical effects are systematically examined: quadrupole excitations, thermal disorder, and many-body shake-up, combined with a band-theory treatment of the core-hole final state.

Result: Quadrupole excitations and thermal disorder are necessary to capture accurate pre-edge features, while many-body shake-up strongly affects the main- and post-edge spectral shape. With all effects included and band-theory core-hole treatment, simulations agree excellently with experiment for most systems by high similarity scores. The ~4980 eV shoulder in BaTiO3 is largely absent in simulation; analysis attributes this to correlation effects of Ba 4f orbitals requiring theory beyond semi-local DFT, with possible contributions from defects such as oxygen vacancies.

Conclusion: The study provides a robust workflow for building high-fidelity Ti K-edge XANES databases for titanium compounds and offers a framework generalizable to a broad range of materials.

Abstract: X-ray absorption spectroscopy (XAS) is a powerful, element-specific probe for investigating the local structural and electronic properties of materials. However, quantitative analysis remains challenging, necessitating accurate first-principles spectral simulations. In this study, we benchmark first-principles simulations of Ti K-edge X-ray absorption near-edge structure (XANES) against experimental data for nine common titanium compounds. We systematically investigate key physical effects, including quadrupole excitations, thermal disorder, and many-body shake-up. Our results demonstrate that quadrupole excitations and thermal disorder are essential for capturing accurate pre-edge features, while many-body shake-up effects significantly influence the spectral shape of the main- and post-edge regions. By incorporating these effects alongside a band-theory treatment of the core-hole final state, our simulated spectra achieve excellent agreement with experimental data for most of the systems, as evidenced by high similarity scores. The shoulder peak in BaTiO$_3$ at 4980 eV in the experiment is largely missing in simulation. Further analysis shows that more accurate electronic structure theory than semi-local density functional theory is required to capture the correlation effects of the Ba $4f$ orbitals and that defects, such as oxygen vacancies, may also contribute to the shoulder. Beyond tackling specific titanium material systems, this work establishes a robust workflow for generating high-fidelity Ti K-edge XANES databases for titanium compounds, providing a framework that can be generalized to a broad range of materials.

</details>


### [39] [Democratizing Atomistic Simulation Workflows for the AI Era with the Quantum Accelerator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33823)
*Andrew S. Rosen,Naisargi Goyal,Brad Ayers,Vineet Bansal,Julia H. Baratta,Samuel M. Blau,Yuan Chiang,Sihoon Choi,Orion Archer Cohen,Blake Dallmann,Tom Demeyere,Will Engler,Yue-Wen Fang,Isabella Furrick,Eliu Huerta,Honghui Kim,Hironori Kondo,Anup Kumar,Jaehong Kwon,Osman Mamun,Charles B. Musgrave,Hananeh Oliaei,Aryan Saha,Davide Sarpa,Benjamin X. Shi,Yuliang Shi,Xing Wang,Robert B. Wexler*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: QuAcc is an open-source workflow library that decouples scientific logic from workflow engines, enabling portable atomistic simulation workflows across multiple engines and unifying foundation MLIP benchmarking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atomistic simulation workflows are now central to machine learning and AI-driven research, yet existing tools tightly couple domain-specific scientific logic to a particular workflow engine and often lack consistent protocols for evaluating foundation MLIPs. This creates barriers to developing and sharing new workflows, and causes methodological drift when benchmarking models.

Method: QuAcc supplies predefined workflow recipes covering first-principles electronic structure, semiempirical and tight-binding methods, classical potentials, and foundation MLIPs. Its core design separates domain-specific scientific logic from the orchestration layer: workflows are ordinary Python functions executable by multiple supported workflow engines without source-code modification. It further offers a unified platform for generating ab initio reference calculations consistent with a given model of interest.

Result: The abstraction lets a single workflow run unchanged across several supported engines, lowering the barrier to workflow development and contribution. The unified reference-generation platform enables consistent, self-consistent assessment of foundation MLIPs.

Conclusion: QuAcc provides a flexible, accessible framework for atomistic simulation workflows, well suited to the current machine learning and AI era, by promoting engine-agnostic portability and reproducible MLIP evaluation.

Abstract: We present the Quantum Accelerator (QuAcc), an open-source workflow library for atomistic simulations with an emphasis on quantum-mechanical calculations. QuAcc provides predefined workflow recipes spanning first-principles electronic-structure methods, semiempirical and tight-binding approaches, classical potentials, and foundation machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs). A central design feature of QuAcc is its separation of domain-specific scientific logic from the workflow engine used to orchestrate and execute calculations. Workflows are written as ordinary Python functions and can be executed with multiple supported workflow engines without modifying the underlying source code, lowering the barrier to developing and contributing new workflows. QuAcc also streamlines the evaluation of foundation MLIPs by providing a unified platform for generating ab initio reference calculations consistent with the model of interest, mitigating methodological drift when assessing model performance. Together, these features make QuAcc a flexible and accessible framework for atomistic simulation workflows that have become central to the current era of machine learning and artificial intelligence.

</details>


### [40] [Correlation Between Dopant Atom Evaporation Field and Measured Site Preference in Atom Probe Tomography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34008)
*Kasper A. Hunnestad,Constantinos Hatzoglou,Frida H. Danmo,Zewu Yan,Edith Bourret,Francois Vurpillot,Antonius T. J. van Helvoort,Sverre Selbach,Dennis Meier*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses Zr-doped ErMnO₃ as a model system to reveal how preferential retention of matrix and dopant atoms during atom probe tomography distorts the measured atomic positions of dopants, and explains these artefacts via field-evaporation simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex oxides have tunable electric, magnetic, and optical properties controlled by chemical doping, but atomic-scale analysis of dopants becomes increasingly difficult at low doping levels. APT can image individual dopant atoms down to a few ppm, yet reliable extraction of this information requires detailed knowledge of atom-specific field evaporation processes, which is currently lacking.

Method: The authors perform APT measurements on Zr-doped ErMnO₃ as a model complex oxide system, analyzing the measured atomic positions of dopant atoms and field evaporation conditions. They complement the experiments with field-evaporation simulations to explain the observed retention behavior.

Result: A substantial preferential retention of both matrix and dopant atoms is observed, which strongly affects dopant site determination and can lead to incorrect interpretation. The retention effect is governed by intrinsic parameters (the dopant's evaporation field and concentration) and extrinsic parameters (the analysis temperature), as confirmed by field-evaporation simulations.

Conclusion: The findings are important for APT-based analysis of individual dopant atoms in solid systems and for understanding field evaporation dynamics at the atomic level in general.

Abstract: Complex oxides possess a wide range of electric, magnetic, and optical properties that can be precisely tuned by chemical doping. The atomic-scale analysis of the property-controlling dopants, however, becomes increasingly difficult towards low doping levels. Atom probe tomography (APT) offers chemical sensitivity and spatial resolution to image individual dopant atoms down to a few parts per million. To reliably extract such information, detailed knowledge about the atom-specific field evaporation processes is required. Here we demonstrate a first insight into the APT-measured atomic position of dopant atoms and the field evaporation conditions, using Zr-doped ErMnO3 as a model system. Our analysis reveals a substantial preferential retention of both matrix and dopant atoms which strongly affects the dopant site determination and can lead to an incorrect interpretation. The retention effect is determined by intrinsic and extrinsic parameters, such as the dopant's evaporation field and concentration and the analysis temperature, respectively, as we explain based on field-evaporation simulations. Our results are important for the APT-based analysis of individual dopant atoms in solid systems and the understanding of field evaporation dynamics at the atomic level in general.

</details>


### [41] [Nanometer-scale operando electric field mapping in oxide junctions by correlative STEM-EBIC and 4D-STEM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34087)
*Yining Xie,Eoin Moynihan,Xingyao Li,Richard Beanland,Aditya Singh,Marin Alexe,Mingmin Yang,Ana Sanchez*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper correlates operando STEM-EBIC with 4D-STEM to quantitatively reconstruct bias-dependent nanoscale electric field distributions across an oxide Schottky junction (LSMO/NSTO), revealing non-classical interfacial electrostatics that deviate from the ideal Schottky depletion model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local electric fields at oxide interfaces govern device functionality, but conventional electrical measurements only infer these fields indirectly. Quantitative 4D-STEM field mapping for heterojunctions suffers from key limitations that hinder direct, nanoscale field reconstruction under operating conditions.

Method: The authors combine operando scanning transmission electron microscope electron-beam-induced current (STEM-EBIC) with four-dimensional-STEM (4D-STEM). Using a La0.67Sr0.33MnO3/Nb:SrTiO3 (LSMO/NSTO) oxide Schottky junction as a model system, they fabricate an electron-transparent junction on a MEMS biasing platform and verify preservation of rectifying transport behavior. STEM-EBIC provides complementary information that addresses limitations of quantitative 4D-STEM field mapping. The two techniques jointly reconstruct the bias-dependent electric field spatial distribution across the junction at nanometer scale.

Result: The reconstructed electric field profiles exhibit a pronounced nonlinear decay within the depletion region and an extended penetration into the LSMO layer. These features directly reveal deviations of the oxide Schottky junction from the ideal conventional Schottky depletion model, providing experimental signatures of non-classical interfacial electrostatics.

Conclusion: The correlative STEM-EBIC/4D-STEM approach enables quantitative nanoscale electric field mapping in operating oxide heterojunctions. It links interfacial electrostatics to macroscopic transport and offers a basis for guiding oxide device design.

Abstract: Understanding local electric fields at oxide interfaces is essential for linking interfacial electrostatics to device functionality, yet conventional electrical measurements infer these fields only indirectly. Here, we combine operando scanning transmission electron microscope electron-beam-induced current (STEM-EBIC) with four-dimensional-STEM (4D-STEM) to quantitatively reconstruct the bias-dependent electric field spatial distribution across an oxide Schottky junction at nanometer scale. Using La0.67Sr0.33MnO3/Nb:SrTiO3 (LSMO/NSTO) as a model system, we fabricate an electron-transparent junction on a MEMS biasing platform and verify that it retains the rectifying transport behavior. STEM-EBIC provides complementary information addressing several key limitations in quantitative 4D-STEM field mapping for heterojunctions. The reconstructed electric field profiles show a pronounced nonlinear decay within the depletion region and an extended penetration into the LSMO. These results directly reveal the deviations of oxides Schottky junction from the ideal conventional Schottky depletion model, providing experimental signatures of non-classical interfacial electrostatics. Our correlative approach enables quantitative nanoscale electric field mapping in operating oxide heterojunctions, providing a basis for linking interfacial electrostatics to macroscopic transport and guiding oxide devices design.

</details>


### [42] [Finite-temperature grain boundary phases, transformations, and diagrams in alloys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34131)
*Flynn Walsh,Timofey Frolov*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Monte Carlo method that simultaneously samples atomic position, configuration, and atom number to predict finite-temperature alloy grain boundary structures, phase transformations, and phase diagrams, demonstrated on W-V alloys.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current understanding of alloy grain boundaries is limited because atomistic simulation techniques are incomplete: they either neglect entropy or artificially constrain the atomic density, preventing rigorous finite-temperature predictions.

Method: Monte Carlo simulations that simultaneously sample all microscopic degrees of freedom in complex interfaces—position, configuration, and atom number—allowing rigorous prediction of finite-temperature grain boundary structures, phase transformations, and diagrams in multicomponent materials.

Result: Simulations of W-V alloys uncover a range of previously inaccessible phase transitions, including condensation of interstitial solutes and transformation of the dislocation structure, demonstrating the necessity of the approach.

Conclusion: Simultaneously sampling position, configuration, and atom number enables rigorous finite-temperature grain boundary phase behavior in multicomponent alloys, revealing phase transitions missed by conventional atomistic methods.

Abstract: Current understanding of alloy grain boundaries is limited by the incompleteness of atomistic simulation techniques, which either neglect entropy or artificially constrain the atomic density. This study introduces Monte Carlo simulations that simultaneously sample all the microscopic degrees of freedom in complex interfaces: position, configuration, and atom number. The method enables rigorous predictions of finite-temperature grain boundary structures, phase transformations, and diagrams in multicomponent materials. Simulations of W-V alloys demonstrate the necessity of the approach by uncovering a range of previously inaccessible phase transitions including condensation of interstitial solutes and transformation of dislocation structure.

</details>


### [43] [Magneto-optical Kerr spectroscopy of exciton Rydberg states in a magnetic van der Waals heterostructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34536)
*Astha Khandelwal,Benran Zhang,Madhusmita Jena,Zhenchao Wen,Kenji Watanabe,Takashi Taniguchi,Saroj P. Dash,Zhenglu Li,Ryo Kitaura,Bhagwati Prasad,Daichi Kozawa*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Wavelength-resolved magneto-optical Kerr spectroscopy of a proximity-coupled MoSe2/Fe3GaTe2 heterostructure resolves magnetic symmetry breaking across the A- and B-exciton manifolds, assigning strongly enhanced Kerr resonances to the ground and 2s states and explaining opposite A/B Kerr polarities via proximity-induced exchange coupling to opposite valence-band spins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Excitonic states in two-dimensional semiconductors are sensitive to time-reversal symmetry breaking, yet how interfacial exchange acts on higher-lying exciton Rydberg states remains largely unexplored.

Method: Wavelength-resolved magneto-optical Kerr spectroscopy combined with reflection, photoluminescence, and photoluminescence excitation spectroscopy, alongside first-principles many-body calculations that include screening by the metallic Fe3GaTe2 layer, applied to a proximity-coupled MoSe2/Fe3GaTe2 van der Waals heterostructure.

Result: Strongly enhanced Kerr resonances are assigned to the ground and 2s states of both the A- and B-exciton manifolds. Reversing the magnetic field inverts the polarity of every resonance, and a field-odd/field-even decomposition confirms a magnetic origin. The opposite Kerr polarities of the A- and B-exciton manifolds are explained by proximity-induced exchange coupling to the opposite valence-band spins of the two exciton series.

Conclusion: Resonant Kerr spectroscopy is established as a sensitive probe of magnetically induced symmetry breaking across the excitonic manifold.

Abstract: Excitonic states in two-dimensional semiconductors are sensitive to time-reversal symmetry breaking, yet how interfacial exchange acts on higher-lying exciton Rydberg states remains largely unexplored. Here we show that wavelength-resolved magneto-optical Kerr spectroscopy of a proximity-coupled MoSe$_2$/Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$ van der Waals heterostructure resolves magnetic symmetry breaking across the A- and B-exciton manifolds. Combining reflection, photoluminescence, and photoluminescence excitation spectroscopy with first-principles many-body calculations including screening by the metallic Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$ layer, we assign the strongly enhanced Kerr resonances to the ground and 2s states of both manifolds. Reversing the magnetic field inverts the polarity of every resonance. A field-odd/field-even decomposition confirms a magnetic origin. The opposite Kerr polarities of the A- and B-exciton manifolds can be explained by proximity-induced exchange coupling to the opposite valence-band spins of the two exciton series. Our results establish resonant Kerr spectroscopy as a sensitive probe of magnetically induced symmetry breaking across the excitonic manifold.

</details>


### [44] [Phase compositions of Si oxynitride: A comprehensive thermodynamic modeling study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34796)
*Andrey Sarikov,Mariia Babiichuk,Mariia Voitovych,Sergii V. Mamykin1*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A thermodynamic study of amorphous SiOxNy films establishes their phase behavior as a function of composition and fabrication temperature, yielding a comprehensive phase diagram distinguishing single-phase oxynitride from two-phase oxide/nitride mixtures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To fundamentally understand how the phase state of amorphous SiOxNy films depends on chemical composition and fabrication temperature, since such knowledge is needed to tailor film characteristics for practical applications.

Method: Thermodynamic analysis based on minimizing the Gibbs free energy, comparing two candidate phase states: a homogeneous single-phase Si oxynitride versus a two-phase mixture of Si oxide and Si nitride. The approach examines phase composition changes with stoichiometry indices and temperature, identifies boundaries between phase states, and determines critical stoichiometry values.

Result: The phase composition and its modification with SiOxNy stoichiometry and temperature are characterized; features of the chemical composition of the Si oxide and Si nitride phases in two-phase oxynitrides are revealed; critical stoichiometry values separating phase states are determined; and a comprehensive phase diagram of single- and two-phase regions as a function of fabrication temperature is constructed.

Conclusion: The results clarify the thermodynamic origin of phase-state boundaries and transitions in SiOxNy films, extending understanding of their fundamental properties and their dependence on fabrication conditions, with potential use for tailoring SiOxNy characteristics in applications.

Abstract: In this work, a comprehensive thermodynamic description of the formation of phases in amorphous SiOxNy (0 <= x <= 2, 0 <= y <= 4/3) films as a function of the film chemical composition and fabrication temperature is presented. Two possible phase states, namely homogeneous single-phase Si oxynitride and two-phase mixture of Si oxide and Si nitride phases, are analyzed in terms of the lowest values of the Gibbs free energy. The characters of the modification of the phase composition upon changing the SiOxNy stoichiometry indices and fabrication temperature are determined. Moreover, characteristic features of the chemical composition of the Si oxide and Si nitride phases in two-phase Si oxynitrides are revealed. Particular attention is given to the thermodynamic origin of the boundaries between different phase states of the SiOxNy films and to determining the critical stoichiometry values separating them. A comprehensive phase diagram indicating single- and two-phase SiOxNy regions as functions of the fabrication temperature is constructed. The peculiarities of the transitions between different SiOxNy phase states, along with underlying mechanisms, are discussed with reference to this phase diagram. The obtained results extend our understanding of the fundamental properties of Si oxynitride films and their dependence on the film fabrication conditions, and may be used for tailoring SiOxNy characteristics for practical applications.

</details>


### [45] [Optimization of InAs/InAlGaAs/InP quantum dots for O-band laser applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34872)
*Anna Penkała,Paweł Podemski,Vinayakrishna Joshi,Vitalii Sichkovskyi,Johann P. Reithmaier,Grzegorz Sęk*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Optimization of InAs quantum dot emitters on InP by MBE for O-band laser active regions, improving carrier capture efficiency and confinement to suppress thermal carrier escape.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: O-band telecom range offers minimal chromatic dispersion and low transmission loss, making it ideal for optical communications. Low-dimensional active regions like quantum dots provide low threshold power and high thermal stability, but long-wavelength QD emitters suffer thermal carrier escape through excited states when the ground-to-excited-state energy separation is insufficient.

Method: InAs quantum dot emitters were grown on InP by molecular-beam epitaxy (MBE) for O-band laser active regions. The design was optimized for better carrier capture efficiency, and the emission wavelength was tuned to the O-band to improve carrier confinement. Absorption spectroscopy was performed on the finalized structure.

Result: Absorption spectroscopy confirms substantial energy separation between the quantum dot ground and excited states, effectively suppressing thermal carrier escape even at elevated temperatures.

Conclusion: These results highlight critical design principles for improving carrier capture and retention in long-wavelength quantum dot lasers.

Abstract: The O-band telecommunication spectral range combines minimal chromatic dispersion with low transmission loss, making it highly suitable for optical communications. Semiconductor telecom lasers utilizing low-dimensional active regions, such as quantum dots, offer low threshold power and high thermal stability. However, long-wavelength quantum dot emitters often suffer from thermal carrier escape through excited states when the energy separation between the ground and excited states is insufficient. Here we demonstrate the optimization of InAs quantum dot emitters grown on InP by molecular-beam epitaxy for O-band laser active regions. By optimizing the design for better carrier capture efficiency and tuning the emission wavelength to the O-band, we show ways of improving the carrier confinement. Absorption spectroscopy on the finalized structure confirms substantial energy separation between the quantum dot ground and excited states, effectively suppressing thermal carrier escape even at elevated temperatures. These results highlight critical design for improving carrier capture and retention in long-wavelength quantum dot lasers.

</details>


### [46] [AI-Assisted Identification of Magnetic Orders and Skyrmions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35566)
*Haowen Yang,Sophia Huerta,Yingying Wu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A machine-learning framework classifies FM vs. AFM order (93.6% balanced accuracy) and predicts magnetization (0.686 μB/f.u. MAE) for 2D materials, then uses simulated magnetic imaging and topological-charge analysis to screen candidates for antiferromagnetic and skyrmion-based spintronics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust high-temperature van der Waals antiferromagnets and topological magnetic states in 2D materials are highly desirable for energy-efficient spintronics, but their discovery remains difficult, motivating an efficient computational screening approach.

Method: Descriptors (structural, compositional, electronic) are derived from the Materials Project and encoded as fixed-length vectors for two tasks. FM/AFM classification uses LightGBM on structure-derived descriptors only, with five-fold cross-validation grouped by chemical system to limit chemical leakage, plus hyperparameter and threshold optimization. Magnetization prediction uses a residual MLP with compositional, structural, electronic, and task-specific initial-state descriptors, a logarithmic target transform, robust weighted loss, and a three-model ensemble. Selected candidates undergo simulated magnetic imaging and phase-reconstruction analysis of normalized magnetization profiles and topological charge.

Result: Balanced accuracy of 93.6% on an isolated test set for FM/AFM classification; mean absolute error of 0.686 μB/formula unit for magnetization prediction; skyrmion-like features characterized via magnetization profiles and topological charge in selected candidates.

Conclusion: The framework offers an efficient route to screen 2D magnetic materials and prioritize candidates for antiferromagnetic and topological spintronic applications.

Abstract: Exotic magnetic orders in two-dimensional (2D) materials are attracting huge interest for energy-efficient spintronic applications, yet realizing robust high-temperature van der Waals antiferromagnets and topological magnetic states remains challenging. In this work, we develop a machine-learning framework for identifying magnetic orders and predicting magnetization using structural, compositional, and electronic information derived from the Materials Project. Fixed-length descriptors are constructed for two complementary tasks: ferromagnetic (FM) versus antiferromagnetic (AFM) classification and quantitative magnetization prediction. Magnetic order is classified using a LightGBM model trained on structure-derived descriptors without explicitly including magnetic descriptors. Five-fold cross-validation grouped by chemical system is used to reduce chemical leakage, together with hyperparameter and classification-threshold optimization. On an isolated test set, the classifier achieves a balanced accuracy of 93.6% in the testing set. Magnetization is predicted using a residual multilayer perceptron with compositional, structural, electronic, and task-specific initial-state descriptors. A logarithmic target transformation and robust weighted loss are used to account for the broad magnetization distribution, while three independently trained models are combined into a final ensemble. The model achieves a mean absolute error of 0.686 μB/formula unit. For selected magnetic candidates, simulated magnetic imaging and phase-reconstruction analysis are further used to investigate magnetization textures and skyrmion-like features through normalized magnetization profiles and topological charge. This framework provides an efficient approach for screening 2D magnetic materials and prioritizing candidates for antiferromagnetic and topological spintronic applications.

</details>


### [47] [First Principles Magnetic Initialisation Made Simple](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35081)
*Kit D. Brown,Robert A. Lawrence*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A cheap, first-principles method predicts magnetic structures from symmetry analysis plus a non-magnetic band structure, reproducing known ground states across ferromagnetic, antiferromagnetic, and frustrated materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: First-principles electronic structure calculations typically require magnetic initialisations that come from experimental results, limiting predictive capability.

Method: Symmetry analysis combined with a non-magnetic band structure is used to generate magnetic initialisations, requiring no experimental input.

Result: The method reproduces the known magnetic ground state for a wide class of materials: ferromagnetic BCC Fe, antiferromagnetic L1_0 PtMn, and frustrated antiferromagnetic L1_2 IrMn_3, while correctly predicting non-magnetic materials to remain non-magnetic.

Conclusion: Symmetry-based initialisation from non-magnetic band structures offers a computationally inexpensive, experimentally independent route to predicting magnetic ground states.

Abstract: A fundamental issue with first principles electronic structure calculation is the requirement for magnetic initialisations, which are usually derived from experimental results. In this paper, we present a new and computationally inexpensive method to predict magnetic structures from first principles, based upon symmetry analysis and a non-magnetic band structure only. We demonstrate that this method of creating initialisations successfully reproduces the known magnetic ground state for a wide class of magnetic materials, including ferromagnetic BCC Fe, antiferromagnetic L1$_0$ PtMn, and frustrated antiferromagnetic L1$_2$ IrMn$_3$, while also predicting non-magnetic materials to remain non-magnetic.

</details>


### [48] [Machine-learning-guided exploration of domain walls in the hybrid improper ferroelectric Ca3Ti2O7](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35346)
*Ida C. Skogvoll,Erik Fransson,Leo Ö. Westin,Benjamin A. D. Williamson,Nicholas C. Bristowe,Sverre M. Selbach,Paul Erhart*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using group theory and machine-learned interatomic potentials, this work maps domain walls in the {100}, {110}, and {001} planes of hybrid improper ferroelectric Ca3Ti2O7, achieving DFT-level accuracy and identifying a low-energy antipolar pathway for polarization switching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ruddlesden-Popper phases host hybrid improper ferroelectricity with complex domain walls where octahedral rotations, polar distortions, and strain interact. Understanding these multi-order-parameter domain walls requires accurate atomic-scale modeling, which is challenging with purely DFT-based approaches at the scale needed.

Method: The authors combine group-theoretical analysis with machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs) to systematically map domain walls in the {100}, {110}, and {001} pseudo-tetragonal planes of Ca3Ti2O7. They employ a charge-aware training framework incorporating reference Born effective charges to capture long-range electrostatics.

Result: The trained potential reproduces DFT order parameter and polarization profiles across all wall types and orientations. The charge-aware training with Born effective charges reduces prediction errors for domain wall formation energies by 70%, demonstrating the importance of long-range electrostatics. The MLIP identifies minimum energy pathways at near-DFT precision, revealing a low-energy antipolar configuration for polarization switching.

Conclusion: Charge-aware MLIPs enable accurate, DFT-level modeling of complex domain walls in hybrid improper ferroelectrics, and reveal a low-energy antipolar polarization-switching pathway in Ca3Ti2O7.

Abstract: Ruddlesden-Popper phases are highly tunable and naturally layered structures, in which polarization can arise via a hybrid improper ferroelectric mechanism. This enables a complex domain wall (DW) structure where multiple order parameters, like octahedral rotations, polar distortions and strain, interact. In this work, we explore the rich set of DW structures in prototypical Ca3Ti2O7, mapping out the DWs in the {100}, {110} and {001} pseudo-tetragonal planes using group theory and machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs). The trained potential reproduces the density functional theory (DFT) order parameter and polarization profiles for all wall types and orientations considered. A charge-aware training framework combined with reference Born effective charges reduces the prediction errors for the DW formation energies by 70%, revealing the importance of including long-range electrostatics to model symmetry-broken interfaces. Finally, the MLIP is used to identify minimum energy pathways at the atomic scale with nearly the precision of DFT calculations, revealing a low-energy antipolar configuration for polarization switching.

</details>


### [49] [High-Throughput Imaging of Degradation-Inducing Microscopic Impurities in Perovskite Solar Cells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35510)
*Sofiia Kosar,Anil R. Pininti,Vladyslav Hnapovskyi,José P. Jurado,Subhashri Mannar,Lorenzo Mardegan,Anand S. Subbiah,Frédéric Laquai,Stefaan De Wolf*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work identifies residual compositional impurities in wide-bandgap perovskite films via nano-FTIR and high-resolution microscopy, shows they act as degradation nucleation sites, and develops a machine-learning-based rapid imaging framework to detect and quantify impurities as an early-warning stability metric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scalable fabrication of high-quality, large-area perovskite thin films is limited by microscopic inhomogeneities, especially residual compositional impurities formed during processing. Identifying these impurities, understanding their impact on device operation, and enabling their rapid detection are essential for upscaling perovskite solar cells (PSCs), particularly for tandem applications.

Method: Nano-Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy combined with high-resolution optical and scanning probe techniques was used to identify detrimental impurities in wide-bandgap perovskite films. Photo-stress experiments probed degradation initiation. A rapid, non-invasive, high-throughput framework based on high-resolution reflected light microscopy and machine-learning-supported image analysis was then developed for impurity detection and quantification in as-prepared films.

Result: Impurities were identified as detrimental in wide-bandgap films, and degradation was found to initiate at impurity-perovskite interfaces, establishing impurities as failure nucleation sites. The machine-learning imaging framework detected and quantified harmful impurities within seconds. Films with higher impurity density exhibited accelerated early degradation, validating impurity density as an early-warning stability metric. Degradation tracking further revealed coupled photo- and thermo-chemical contributions to impurity-mediated instability.

Conclusion: The study establishes a practical, chemically-validated diagnostic imaging framework for rapid pre-screening of perovskite films, supporting the development of stable and scalable perovskite solar cells.

Abstract: The scalable fabrication of high-quality, large-area perovskite thin films is hindered by microscopic inhomogeneities, particularly residual compositional impurities formed during processing. Identifying these impurities, understanding their impact on device operation, and enabling their rapid detection are essential for upscaling perovskite solar cells (PSCs). Here, nano-Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy combined with high-resolution optical and scanning probe techniques was used to identify detrimental impurities in wide-bandgap perovskite films relevant to tandem solar cells. Photo-stress experiments revealed that degradation of the perovskite layer initiates at impurity-perovskite interfaces, demonstrating that impurities act as failure nucleation sites. Leveraging these insights, a rapid, non-invasive, and high-throughput framework based on high-resolution reflected light microscopy and machine learning-supported image analysis was developed, enabling detection and quantification of harmful impurities in as-prepared films within seconds.
