### [71] [Uncertainty Quantification for the Fission Matrix Method: A Rigorous Mathematical Framework and Computationally Efficient Alternatives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33161)
*Valerio Mascolino*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This work develops and benchmarks uncertainty propagation methods for fission matrix (FM) eigenvalues and fission sources, deriving a full-covariance Delta-method framework and comparing it against a diagonal-covariance approximation and direct resampling, validated on an OpenMC model of the JSI TRIGA Mark-II reactor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior FM-based neutron transport calculations propagate Monte Carlo statistical uncertainty to keff and the fission source by assuming independent FM coefficients, without validating this assumption against the coefficients' actual covariance. A rigorous uncertainty propagation framework is needed.

Method: A full-covariance Delta-method framework is derived for propagating uncertainty to keff and the fission source. It is evaluated against two cheaper alternatives—a diagonal-covariance approximation and direct resampling of FM coefficients—using an OpenMC model of the JSI TRIGA Mark-II reactor, with an empirical benchmark of 100 independent Monte Carlo runs.

Result: For the all-rods-out case, the three estimators and the empirical benchmark agree within 5.7% for keff and 1% for the fission source, though the diagonal approximation's bias sign flips between statistics levels. With a global fixed-source FM generation strategy, the diagonal, resampling, and independent-run estimators agree within about 2% and lie roughly 10% below the full-covariance estimate, a gap attributed to covariance sampling noise. The large off-diagonal covariance is nearly symmetric and largely cancels when propagated, contrary to prior assumptions that it is small or negative. For the FM-CRd strategy, keff matches an exact perturbed reference within 60 pcm (about two standard deviations) and the fission source within 0.7%, with diagonal and resampling estimators agreeing closely and being conservative by about a factor of two.

Conclusion: Given its close agreement with the full covariance and the prohibitive memory cost of the full covariance, the diagonal Delta method is recommended as the practical default, with the full covariance reserved for one-time system characterization.

Abstract: The fission matrix (FM) method recasts neutron transport in matrix form, enabling fast, interpolation-based reactor calculations from a pre-computed database of Monte Carlo-derived coefficients. Propagation of the underlying Monte Carlo statistical uncertainty to the FM eigenvalue and eigenvector is rarely rigorous: prior approaches assume independent coefficients without testing this against their actual covariance. This work derives a full-covariance Delta-method framework for propagating uncertainty to keff and the fission source, and evaluates it with two cheaper alternatives, a diagonal-covariance approximation and direct resampling of the FM coefficients, using an OpenMC model of the JSI TRIGA Mark-II reactor. For the all-rods-out case, these three estimators and an empirical benchmark of 100 independent Monte Carlo runs agree within 5.7% for keff and 1% for the fission source. The sign of the diagonal approximation's bias flips between the two statistics levels, so it must be checked case by case. With a global fixed-source FM generation strategy, the diagonal, resampling, and independent-run estimators agree within about 2% and lie roughly 10% below the full-covariance estimate, a gap attributed to covariance sampling noise. The large off-diagonal covariance is nearly symmetric and mostly cancels when propagated, rather than being small or negative as previously assumed. For the FM control-rod interpolation and combination strategy (FM-CRd), keff matches an exact perturbed reference within 60 pcm (about two standard deviations) and the fission source within 0.7%, while the diagonal and resampling estimators agree closely and are conservative by about a factor of two. Given this and the prohibitive memory cost of the full covariance, the diagonal Delta method is recommended as the practical default, with the full covariance reserved for one-time characterization of a system.

</details>


### [72] [When does the moment basis matter in lattice Boltzmann methods?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33328)
*Alessandro De Rosis*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: For velocity sets with velocities in {0,±1}^d and symmetric weights, orthogonalising the moment basis affects a central-moment LBM only via the fourth-order (and diagonal) moments: shear rates are free (unchanged) on D2Q9 and D3Q15, while D3Q19 and D3Q27 change whenever fourth-order moments relax at a different rate than shear. On rectangular lattices, symmetry breaking makes orthogonalisation alter transport coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify when (and how) choosing an orthogonal moment basis actually changes the physics of a central-moment lattice Boltzmann scheme, since this choice is often made for numerical convenience and its physical effect is ambiguous.

Method: Algebraic analysis of the collision operator: the basis enters only through the conjugate T^{-1}ΛT, so two bases connected by a constant matrix A give identical schemes iff the commutator [Λ,A] vanishes on physical states; this is applied to velocity sets {0,±1}^d with symmetric weights, plus rectangular lattices, numerical stability tests, Taylor-Green DNS comparisons, and cost measurement on D3Q27.

Result: Orthogonalisation reaches shear moments only through face/body diagonals: shear rate free on D2Q9 and D3Q15; scheme changes on D3Q19/D3Q27 when fourth-order moments do not relax at the shear rate. On rectangular lattices an independent bulk rate produces an orthogonal counterpart with different transport coefficients. The rest-equilibrium-orthogonal basis is never less robust by more than a few per cent (often much more robust) and is more accurate against Taylor-Green DNS, costing at most 2% more on D3Q27.

Conclusion: Orthogonalisation of the moment basis is physically neutral only in restricted cases (D2Q9, D3Q15, or when fourth-order moments relax at the shear rate); generally it can change transport coefficients and stability. A basis orthogonal in the rest-equilibrium inner product is recommended, as it guarantees linear stability at rest and is more robust and accurate at negligible extra cost.

Abstract: Whether orthogonalising the moment basis changes a central-moment lattice Boltzmann scheme is decided by the geometry of the velocity set. The basis enters the collision only through the conjugate $\mathit{T}^{-1} Λ\mathit{T}$, so two bases related by a constant matrix $\mathit{A}$ define exactly the same scheme if and only if $[Λ,\mathit{A}]_{ij}=\mathit{A}_{ij}(λ_i-λ_j)$ vanishes, and on physical states only its columns for the non-conserved moments matter. We prove that on lattices with velocities in $\{0,\pm1\}^d$ and symmetric weights orthogonalisation reaches the shear moments only through the face and body diagonals: whatever the orthogonalisation, the shear rate is free on D2Q9 and D3Q15, whereas on D3Q19 and D3Q27 the scheme changes whenever the fourth-order moments do not relax at the shear rate. On rectangular lattices, where the symmetry is broken, an independent bulk rate yields an orthogonal counterpart that does not share the transport coefficients of the published scheme, which is itself orthogonal in the inner product of the rest equilibrium up to the one relaxation entry that consistency requires. Where the formulations differ but share their hydrodynamics, a basis orthogonal in the inner product of the rest equilibrium, which guarantees linear stability at rest, was never less robust in the least stable flow direction by more than a few per cent, often much more robust, and the more accurate against a Taylor-Green DNS. Computed through the non-orthogonal transforms with a conjugated relaxation matrix, it costs at most $2\%$ more on D3Q27.

</details>


### [73] [Stochastic Physics-Constrained Operator Inference for Complex Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33380)
*Changhong Mou*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: SPOpInf is a non-intrusive method that jointly learns a quadratic-linear drift and diffusion amplitude of stochastic systems from data, enforcing energy-preserving structure exactly and estimating noise via EM, outperforming OpInf and remaining stable for stochastic ROMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadratically nonlinear systems with additive noise appear widely in science and engineering, serving both as stochastic dynamical systems and as reduced-order models (ROMs) of turbulent flows. Existing operator inference (OpInf) fits only a deterministic drift by least squares, treating the stochastic part inadequately and failing to preserve the energy-preserving quadratic structure, which leads to instability in long-time simulations.

Method: SPOpInf maximizes an Euler--Maruyama increment likelihood where the residual covariance is also unknown, calibrating deterministic and stochastic components jointly. It enforces the energy-preserving quadratic structure exactly via linear equality constraints inside the optimization, and uses an upper-triangular parametrization to remove structural non-identifiability of the full quadratic tensor. The optimization is solved by an EM-based iterative algorithm that alternately updates drift coefficients via an inexpensive weighted least-squares solve and noise levels via a residual-variance update.

Result: On five stochastic benchmark systems exhibiting intermittency and chaos, SPOpInf recovers deterministic operators more accurately than OpInf, satisfies imposed constraints to machine precision, accurately estimates noise amplitudes, and reproduces long-time statistics. For a stochastic Burgers equation and a quasi-geostrophic double-gyre flow, it yields non-intrusive stochastic ROMs stable over long times where unconstrained alternatives blow up.

Conclusion: SPOpInf provides a principled, non-intrusive framework for learning stochastic ROMs with exact structural constraints and joint noise estimation, offering improved accuracy and long-time stability over deterministic operator inference.

Abstract: Quadratically nonlinear systems driven by additive noise arise throughout science and engineering, both as stochastic dynamical systems in their own right and as reduced-order models (ROMs) of turbulent flows. We propose stochastic physics-constrained operator inference (SPOpInf), a non-intrusive method that learns a stochastic differential equation, i.e., a quadratic--linear drift and a diffusion amplitude, from data. Unlike operator inference (OpInf), which fits a deterministic drift by least squares, SPOpInf maximizes an Euler--Maruyama increment likelihood in which the residual covariance is itself an unknown, so the deterministic and stochastic components are calibrated jointly; it enforces the energy-preserving quadratic structure exactly, through linear equality constraints imposed inside the optimization; and it works in an upper-triangular parametrization that removes the structural non-identifiability of the full quadratic tensor. The resulting optimization is solved by an expectation--maximization (EM)-based iterative learning algorithm that updates the drift coefficients and the noise levels in turn: each iteration requires only an inexpensive weighted least-squares solve for the drift and a residual-variance update for the noise. With five stochastic benchmark systems that show intermittency and chaos, SPOpInf recovers the deterministic operators more accurately than OpInf, satisfies the imposed constraints to machine precision, accurately estimates the noise amplitudes, and reproduces long-time statistics. Applied to a stochastic Burgers equation and a quasi-geostrophic double-gyre flow, the same estimator yields non-intrusive stochastic ROMs that remain stable over long times where unconstrained alternatives blow up.

</details>


<div id='physics.chem-ph'></div>

### [74] [A Green-function unification of weak interactions with environmental dressing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31728)
*Johannes Fiedler*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: A unified macroscopic-QED framework derives all weak interactions (electrostatic, induction, dispersion) from a single Hamiltonian, expressing them via electromagnetic Green functions, recovering Keesom, Debye, and London/van der Waals limits and enabling direct treatment of complex environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Weak non-covalent interactions (electrostatics, induction, dispersion) are conventionally treated as distinct mechanisms with separate models and approximations, lacking a single unifying description.

Method: Starting from macroscopic QED in arbitrary linear environments, the authors derive compact master formulas using a single interaction Hamiltonian and multipole expansion (monopoles, dipoles, quadrupoles), expressing interactions through contractions of multipole moments and response functions with (dressed) electromagnetic Green tensors.

Result: Permanent electrostatic, mixed induction, and fully induced (dispersion) interactions emerge on equal footing with a common structural form; environmental effects enter universally via dressed Green functions, and standard non-retarded free-space limits (Keesom, Debye, London/van der Waals) are recovered as validation.

Conclusion: Green functions act as a universal organizing principle for weak interactions: once general expressions are established, scenarios like interfaces, confinement, or polarisable continua are handled by direct substitution of the appropriate Green function without reformulation.

Abstract: Weak, non-covalent interactions such as electrostatics, induction, and dispersion are commonly treated as distinct physical mechanisms, often using separate models and approximations. Here, we present a unified quantum-electrodynamical framework in which all weak interactions between neutral or charged subsystems emerge from a single interaction Hamiltonian and are expressed entirely in terms of electromagnetic Green functions.
  Starting from macroscopic QED in arbitrary linear environments, we derive compact master formulas that generate permanent electrostatic, mixed induction, and fully induced (dispersion) interactions on equal footing. Monopoles, dipoles, and quadrupoles are treated consistently within the same multipole expansion, leading to a common structural form in which all interaction channels are obtained from contractions of multipole moments and response functions with Green tensors. Environmental effects enter universally through dressed Green functions.
  The standard non-retarded free-space limits, including Keesom, Debye, and London/van der Waals interactions, are recovered as a consistency check of the formalism. Beyond this validation, the formulation shows that once the general expressions are established, different physical scenarios, such as interfaces, confinement, or polarisable continua, can be treated by direct substitution of the corresponding Green function, without reformulating the interaction for each case. This elevates Green functions to a universal organising principle for weak interactions in complex environments.

</details>


### [75] [Correlation-Driven Singlet--Triplet Inversion and Optical Spectra of N-Substituted Phenalenyls: Insights from PPP-FCI and TDDFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31738)
*Medha Rakshit,Alok Shukla*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Using the PPP model with FCI, the authors benchmark parameters across ten N-substituted phenalenyl motifs to estimate negative singlet-triplet gaps, finding that conventional adiabatic TDDFT fails to reproduce the inverted S1-T1 ordering, while PPP-FCI reveals significant configuration mixing in the spectra.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atypical inversion of the lowest singlet (S1) and triplet (T1) excited states, violating Hund's rule, has been reported in nitrogen-substituted planar hydrocarbons such as poly-aza phenalenyl derivatives (cyclazine, heptazine), which possess a negative singlet-triplet gap. A reliable, benchmarked theoretical framework for estimating these negative gaps and characterizing their optical response is needed.

Method: The Pariser-Parr-Pople (PPP) semi-empirical correlated pi-electron Hamiltonian is used to investigate the singlet-triplet gap in ten N-substituted phenalenyl motifs. Two schemes of long-range Coulomb interactions are considered, with key parameters (on-site orbital energy, Hubbard U, hopping integrals) systematically tuned. Full configuration-interaction (FCI) incorporates electron correlation. Ground-state and T1 absorption spectra are computed with optimized PPP parameters reproducing experimental data. First-principles TDDFT with various exchange-correlation functionals is performed for validation.

Result: Conventional adiabatic TDDFT fails to reproduce the inverted S1-T1 ordering, indicating correlation-driven multiconfigurational effects are essential. Singlet TDDFT ground-state absorption spectra reproduce several qualitative features of the PPP-FCI spectra, though TDDFT excitation energies are systematically higher. Detailed analysis of ground-state and T1 absorption spectra reveals significant configuration mixing.

Conclusion: The PPP-FCI framework, with appropriately tuned parameters, reliably captures negative singlet-triplet gaps in N-substituted phenalenyl derivatives, whereas adiabatic TDDFT does not. Multiconfigurational, correlation-driven effects are essential for describing the inverted S1-T1 ordering, and the study provides comprehensive characterization of the optical response of these systems.

Abstract: Several researchers have reported an atypical inversion of the lowest singlet ($S_{1}$) and triplet ($T_{1}$) excited states in nitrogen-substituted planar hydrocarbons. Contrary to Hund's rule of maximum multiplicity, poly-aza derivatives of phenalenyl, such as cyclazine and heptazine, have long been known to possess a negative singlet-triplet gap, $ΔE_{ST}=E(S_{1})-E(T_{1})$. Using the well-known Pariser-Parr-Pople (PPP) model, a semi-empirical, wave-function-based Hamiltonian for correlated $π$-electrons, we investigate the singlet-triplet gap in ten N-substituted phenalenyl motifs. Two schemes of long-range Coulomb interactions are considered, and key parameters, including the on-site orbital energy, Hubbard U, and hopping integrals, are systematically tuned to benchmark the model for reliably estimating negative singlet-triplet gaps. Full configuration-interaction (FCI) method is employed to incorporate electron-correlation effects. Ground-state and $T_{1}$ absorption spectra are subsequently calculated using the optimized PPP parameters that best reproduce the available experimental data. For validation, first-principles TDDFT calculations using various exchange--correlation functionals are also performed. Conventional adiabatic TDDFT fails to reproduce the inverted ($S_{1}-T_{1}$) ordering, indicating that the inclusion of the correlation driven multiconfigurational effects are essential for describing it. We find that the singlet TDDFT ground-state absorption spectra reproduce several qualitative features of the PPP-FCI spectra, although the TDDFT excitation energies are systematically higher. Detailed analysis of the ground-state and $T_{1}$ absorption spectra reveals significant configuration mixing, providing a comprehensive characterization of the optical response of the N-substituted phenalenyl derivatives studied.

</details>


### [76] [Pressure and temperature dependence of methane pyrolysis and hydrogen production in a micro flow reactor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31741)
*Mohammad R. Razavi,Ömer L. Gülder*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Methane pyrolysis was studied in a micro flow reactor (1400–1800 K, 1–10 bar, 60 ms) to test chemical mechanism sensitivities; experiments and 1D discrete-sectional simulations agree on pressure trends at 1800 K but diverge on temperature sensitivity, with simulations under-predicting carbon/hydrogen yields and surface deposition while over-predicting temperature dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive kinetic models for methane pyrolysis (relevant to hydrogen production) require validation under elevated pressures and temperatures, yet such data are scarce. The study aims to assess the sensitivity of chemical mechanisms and the predictive capability of simulations under independently controlled reaction conditions.

Method: Methane pyrolysis experiments in a micro flow reactor providing independent control of reaction parameters, at 1400–1800 K, 1–10 bar, fixed 60 ms residence time. Hydrogen, unreacted methane, and solid carbon yields were quantified, along with mass of heterogeneous depositions. C2 and PAH species were measured by GC-MS. Results were compared with 1D simulations employing a discrete sectional model for particulate formation and surface deposition kinetics.

Result: Simulations reproduced the pressure trend well at 1800 K but disagreed on temperature sensitivity. Simulations generally predicted lower carbon and hydrogen yields and a stronger temperature sensitivity than experiments. Abundant PAH intermediates were measured at lower temperatures and higher pressures. Simulated surface deposition rates were lower than measured but exhibited similar profiles.

Conclusion: Micro flow reactors are useful for recreating pyrolysis environments for hydrogen production from methane. Predictive accuracy of simulations could be improved by advancing fuel decomposition and PAH formation/consumption kinetics at low temperatures and by detailed treatment of surface site density.

Abstract: Methane pyrolysis experiments were performed in a micro flow reactor to investigate the chemical mechanism sensitivities at elevated pressures and temperatures. The micro flow reactor used in this study provides independent control over reaction parameters. Experiments were performed at temperatures from 1400 to 1800 K and pressures from 1 to 10 bar at a fixed residence time of 60 ms. The yields of hydrogen, unreacted methane, and solid carbon were quantified for comparison to predictive models. The mass of heterogeneous depositions was also quantified. The presence of C2 and PAH species were measured by GC-MS. The results were compared to 1D simulations using a discrete sectional model for capturing the kinetics of particulate formation and surface deposition. The simulations replicated the pressure trend well at high temperatures (1800 K), but the sensitivity to temperature was in disagreement. Simulations generally found lower carbon and hydrogen yields and a stronger sensitivity to temperature than the experiment. An abundance of PAH intermediates was measured at lower temperatures and higher pressures. The simulated surface deposition rates were lower than the measured values but showed similar profiles. The results demonstrate the utility of micro flow reactors in recreating pyrolysis environments for hydrogen production from methane. The predictive capabilities of simulations could be improved by advancing the fuel decomposition and PAH formation and consumption kinetics at low temperatures and by detailed treatment of surface site density.

</details>


### [77] [ElectrolyteMD-Bench: How Well Do AI Agents Conduct Molecular Dynamics Studies across Electrolyte Solvation Regimes?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31743)
*Shukai Wu,Shuo Niu,Zhaoming Xu,Yan Luo,Wentao Lin*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: ElectrolyteMD-Bench evaluates whether AI agents can autonomously conduct scientifically reliable electrolyte MD studies, finding that while all runs produced valid trajectories, none of eight studies met all scientific requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents are increasingly automating molecular dynamics research for lithium-battery electrolyte discovery, but their ability to autonomously complete scientifically reliable studies remains insufficiently evaluated.

Method: The benchmark unifies dilute, high-concentration, and localized high-concentration electrolytes across distinct solvation regimes, linking local coordination, ionic association, single-particle dynamics, and collective transport in one continuous process. Agents autonomously perform system construction, equilibration, production sampling, property analysis, and interpretation under a frozen scientific model, deciding whether to correct errors, continue sampling, or stop. Each study is audited along four dimensions: simulation validity, analysis validity, evidence integrity, and outcome calibration.

Result: Across eight model–harness configurations, all 24 system runs produced valid production trajectories, yet none of the eight studies satisfied all scientific requirements. Failures included missing analyses, incorrect physical definitions or numerical implementations, insufficient statistical support, and completion claims inconsistent with retained evidence.

Conclusion: These results expose a gap between successful MD workflow execution and reliable scientific study completion. ElectrolyteMD-Bench provides a foundational test and defines a capability threshold that autonomous electrolyte simulation must cross to advance computation-first materials exploration.

Abstract: AI agents are advancing the automation of molecular dynamics (MD) research, with the potential to accelerate discovery and design of lithium-battery electrolytes. However, whether they can autonomously complete scientifically reliable electrolyte MD studies remains insufficiently evaluated. We introduce ElectrolyteMD-Bench, which unifies dilute, high-concentration, and localized high-concentration electrolytes in a comparative study across distinct solvation regimes. The benchmark connects local coordination, ionic association, single-particle dynamics, and collective transport within one continuous research process. Agents autonomously perform system construction, equilibration, production sampling, property analysis, and interpretation under a frozen scientific model, while deciding whether to correct errors, continue sampling, or stop. We audit each study along four dimensions: simulation validity, analysis validity, evidence integrity, and outcome calibration. Across eight model--harness configurations, all 24 system runs produced valid production trajectories, yet none of the eight studies satisfied all scientific requirements. Failures included missing analyses, incorrect physical definitions or numerical implementations, insufficient statistical support, and completion claims inconsistent with retained evidence. These results expose a gap between successful MD workflow execution and reliable scientific study completion. ElectrolyteMD-Bench therefore provides a foundational test and defines a key capability threshold that autonomous electrolyte simulation must cross to advance toward computation-first materials exploration.

</details>


### [78] [Heteroatom Position Controls Ultrafast Photodynamics of Oxazole and Isoxazole](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31875)
*Briony Downes-Ward,Paul Javed,Huynh V. S. Lam,Sajib K. Saha,Surjendu Bhattacharyya,Martin Centurion,Xinxin Cheng,R. Joel England,Casey Foley,Smita Ganguly,Patrick L. Kramer,Jinxin Lang,Randy Lemons,Ming-Fu Lin,Yusong Liu,Tu T. Nguyen,Joao Pedro Figueira Nunes,Chatura Perera,Alexander H. Reid,Ethan Ross,Artem Rudenko,Xiaozhe Shen,John Searles,Anbu Selvam Venkatachalam,Enliang Wang,Stephen P. Weathersby,Vinod Kumarappan,Arthur G. Suits,Christine Aikens,Daniel Rolles*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: First real-time structural study of photoinduced ring opening and fragmentation in oxazole/isoxazole via MeV ultrafast electron diffraction plus trajectory surface hopping simulations, revealing how heteroatom position reshapes dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultrafast photochemistry of heterocyclic compounds is central to photobiology and materials chemistry, but direct experimental observation of their structural dynamics is rare. The authors aim to resolve the mechanistic divergence between two structural isomers differing only in heteroatom connectivity.

Method: MeV ultrafast electron diffraction (UED) with 200 nm photoexcitation of oxazole and isoxazole, complemented by non-adiabatic trajectory surface hopping molecular dynamics; simulated diffraction patterns from trajectory ensembles convolved with the experimental instrument response function.

Result: Both isomers ring-open then fragment, but with distinct dynamics: isoxazole shows exclusive N-O cleavage within 40 fs, followed by sequential fragmentation into HCN + ketene and HCO + vinyl nitrene on the hundreds-of-femtoseconds timescale. Oxazole ring-opens more slowly (290 fs) with only 85% efficiency, primarily via O-C cleavage, accessing nitrile ylide and O-pyramidalized intermediates. Simulated patterns match UED branching ratios; experimental diffraction signatures are surprisingly similar for both isomers.

Conclusion: The synergy of UED and trajectory surface hopping provides an atomistic picture of how a simple interchange of heteroatom connectivity in structural isomers fundamentally reshapes excited-state potential surfaces, conical intersection accessibility, and photochemical outcomes.

Abstract: The ultrafast photochemistry of heterocyclic compounds is central to photobiology and materials chemistry, yet direct experimental observation of their structural dynamics remains rare. Here we present the first real-time structural characterization of photoinduced ring opening and subsequent fragmentation in the isomeric pair oxazole and isoxazole using MeV ultrafast electron diffraction (UED), complemented by non-adiabatic molecular dynamics simulations. Upon photoexcitation at 200 nm, both isomers undergo ring opening followed by fragmentation into various products, but with strikingly different dynamics governed by heteroatom positioning. Trajectory Surface Hopping simulations reproduce the experimental diffraction signatures, which are surprisingly similar for both isomers, and reveal distinct mechanistic pathways for the two isomers. For isoxazole, all trajectories exclusively undergo N-O bond cleavage within 40 fs, followed by sequential fragmentation into HCN + ketene and HCO + vinyl nitrene channels on the hundreds-of-femtoseconds timescale. Oxazole, by contrast, shows significantly slower ring opening (290 fs) with only 85% efficiency, proceeding primarily through O-C cleavage and accessing a richer landscape of intermediates including nitrile ylide and O-pyramidalized structures. Simulated diffraction patterns derived from trajectory ensembles, convolved with the experimental instrument response function, are in agreement with the branching ratios observed by UED. This synergy between UED and trajectory surface hopping provides an atomistic picture of how the simple interchange of heteroatom connectivity in structural isomers fundamentally reshapes excited-state potential surfaces, conical intersection accessibility, and photochemical outcome.

</details>


### [79] [Bridging Molecular Scales with Implicit Score Matching for Bottom-Up Coarse Graining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32040)
*Patrick G. Sahrmann,Nicholas Lubbers,Benjamin T. Nebgen,Emily Shinkle,Sergei Tretiak,Kipton Barros,Brenden W. Hamilton*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces an implicit score matching framework for coarse-grained molecular dynamics that avoids explicit coarse-grained force computation, enabling highly coarsened, machine-learned models that bridge atomic and mesoscopic scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molecular dynamics simulations are limited to small spatial and temporal scales. Coarse-grained models extend this reach by grouping atoms into effective sites, but constructing accurate, highly coarsened potentials is a grand challenge because coarse-grained forces depend on a statistical distribution of composite atomic configurations, making direct calculation computationally prohibitive even with modern machine-learning methods.

Method: The authors introduce an alternative framework based on implicit score matching that directly avoids the need for explicit coarse-grained forces, instead learning bottom-up, machine-learned coarse-grained models from data.

Result: The approach substantially reduces training data required relative to conventional force-matching methods and enables high-fidelity models in which each coarse-grained site represents hundreds to thousands of atoms. Demonstrated across a diverse range of chemical systems, these models enable efficient modeling at micrometer and millimeter length scales while retaining significant atomic scale fidelity.

Conclusion: Implicit score matching is established as a practical route toward chemically accurate simulations that bridge molecular and mesoscopic scales.

Abstract: Molecular dynamics simulations provide a computational microscope for atomic scale processes but remain restricted to relatively small spatial and temporal scales. Coarse-grained models extend their reach by representing groups of atoms as effective interaction sites. The number of particles is systematically reduced by embedding the collective behavior of groups of atoms into coarse-grained sites governed by effective potentials. However, constructing accurate, highly coarsened potentials, even with the assistance of modern machine-learning methods, is a grand challenge because coarse grained forces depend on a statistical distribution of composite atomic configurations making direct calculation computationally prohibitive. Here, we introduce an alternative framework based on implicit score matching that directly avoids the need for explicit coarse-grained forces. This approach both substantially reduces the training data required relative to conventional force-matching methods and enables high-fidelity models in which each coarse-grained site represents hundreds to thousands of atoms. We demonstrate the method's versatility and effectiveness across a diverse range of chemical systems and phenomena, developing bottom-up, machine-learned models. These enable efficient modeling at micrometer and millimeter length scales, while retaining significant amounts of the atomic scale fidelity. These results establish implicit score matching as a practical route towards chemically accurate simulations that bridge molecular and mesoscopic scales.

</details>


### [80] [Reinforcement learning amortizes transition-state physics into one-step flow models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32073)
*Yunyang Li,Zechang Sun,Kuang Yu,Wen Yan,Mark Gerstein,Hung Q. Pham*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Trèfle, a one-step flow model, amortizes physics-guided transition-state generation into training via a mean-flow objective, a DFT surrogate reward, and RL post-training, achieving 30× throughput and strong transfer/generalization for reaction discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transition-state searches are a bottleneck in reaction discovery because identifying valid saddle-point structures needs many expensive quantum-chemical calculations. Existing generative models can propose candidates from reactant/product geometries, but geometry-only supervised training does not enforce the physical conditions of a transition state, and enforcing them during sampling is costly.

Method: Trèfle is a one-step flow model that amortizes physics-guided generation into training. It uses a mean-flow objective to replace multi-step sampling, a low-cost surrogate to replace DFT reward evaluations, and reinforcement-learning post-training with physical-condition-enforcing rewards instead of inference-time guidance.

Result: Trèfle generates accurate transition states at 30× the throughput of multi-step baselines. It transfers few-shot to ten unseen transition metals and generalizes zero-shot to much larger molecules than training data. With DFT refinement and reaction-path validation, Trèfle-seeded searches recover all 13 unseen γ-ketohydroperoxide channels, and post-training raises per-attempt recovery from 28% to 50% across 85 unseen bimolecular channels.

Conclusion: Trèfle provides fast, reliable seeds for automated reaction discovery by combining one-step generation with physics-guided training.

Abstract: Transition-state searches remain a major bottleneck in reaction discovery, as identifying valid saddle-point structures requires numerous expensive quantum-chemical calculations. Generative models can reduce this burden by proposing candidates from reactant and product geometries, but supervised training on geometries alone does not enforce the physical conditions required of a transition state, while enforcing them during sampling is costly. We introduce Trèfle, a one-step flow model that amortizes physics-guided generation into training: a mean-flow objective replaces multi-step sampling, a low-cost surrogate replaces density functional theory (DFT) reward evaluations, and reinforcement-learning post-training with rewards that enforce those conditions replaces inference-time guidance. Trèfle generates accurate transition states at $30\times$ the throughput of multi-step baselines. It transfers few-shot to ten unseen transition metals and generalizes zero-shot to molecules far larger than any in training. With DFT refinement and reaction-path validation, Trèfle-seeded searches recover all 13 unseen $γ$-ketohydroperoxide channels, while post-training raises per-attempt recovery from 28% to 50% across 85 unseen bimolecular channels. Trèfle thus provides fast, reliable seeds for automated reaction discovery.

</details>


### [81] [The Same and Not the Same: A Machine-Checked Genealogy of Photochemical Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32251)
*Junyi Gong,Zijie Qiu,Ben Zhong Tang*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: PhotoLean is a machine-checked genealogy of 17 photochemical/photophysical theories formalized in Lean 4 over a shared kernel, providing a verified relation or registered reason for absence for all 136 theory pairs, and adjudicating three inherited conflations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chemistry's phenomenological theories proliferate with observables: the same physical reality is repeatedly named across different measurement contexts, and relations among theories lack an auditable carrier. As proposition generation shifts to machines, an audit layer for theory itself is needed.

Method: Formalize seventeen photochemical and photophysical theories in Lean 4 over a shared kernel. For every pair of theories (all 136 pairs), attach either a verified relation—equivalence, implication, composition with registered premises, or definitional certificate—or a registered reason for its absence. Three adjudications with if-and-only-if boundaries and witnesses resolve inherited conflations.

Result: Three adjudications: (1) the symmetry factor β equals one-half exactly when reactant and product force constants coincide; (2) Kasha's rule and the Kasha-Vavilov rule agree only under closed quantification with a loss channel; (3) intensity-only Stern-Volmer data cannot distinguish static from dynamic quenching, whereas the lifetime channel discriminates exactly.

Conclusion: Machine-checked relational ledgers offer a replicable audit layer for theory itself as proposition generation shifts to machines.

Abstract: Chemistry's phenomenological theories multiply with its observables: the same physical reality is repeatedly named in different measurement contexts, and the relations among theories have never had an auditable carrier. Here we present PhotoLean, a machine-checked genealogy of seventeen photochemical and photophysical theories formalized in Lean 4 over a shared kernel. Every pair of theories carries either a verified relation (equivalence, implication, composition with registered premises, definitional certificate) or a registered reason for its absence, covering all 136 pairs. Three adjudications resolve inherited conflations with if-and-only-if boundaries and witnesses: the symmetry factor $β$ equals one-half exactly when reactant and product force constants coincide; Kasha's rule and the Kasha-Vavilov rule agree only under closed quantification with a loss channel; and intensity-only Stern-Volmer data cannot distinguish static from dynamic quenching, whereas the lifetime channel discriminates exactly. As proposition generation shifts to machines, such machine-checked relational ledgers offer a replicable audit layer for theory itself.

</details>


### [82] [Reduced scaling implementation of the coupled cluster-based static embedding method, MPCC with density fitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32884)
*Avijit Shee,Fabian M. Faulstich,K. Birgitta Whaley,Lin Lin,Martin Head-Gordon*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This work presents a low-scaling density-fitting implementation of the MPCC quantum embedding method, achieving O(N^4)/O(N_frag^6) scaling with accuracy comparable to the original unfactorized approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original MPCC quantum embedding method is computationally expensive, limiting its applicability to larger systems; an efficient, low-scaling reformulation is needed without sacrificing accuracy.

Method: Density-fitting (DF) approximation is applied to electron-repulsion integrals across all three layers (low-level method, screened interaction, high-level method). An approximate factorized Sylvester-equation solution via Laplace transformation is used for the low-level step, and an approximate screened-interaction construction based on the distinguishable cluster approximation (DCA) is explored, neglecting certain higher-order exchange and ladder diagrams.

Result: An O(N^4) low-level algorithm, a non-iterative N_frag O(N^4) screened-interaction algorithm, and an iterative O(N_frag^6) fragment solver are obtained. Validation on trans polyacetylenes (n=1-8), the O3 potential energy surface, and selected S22 molecules shows significant cost reduction with accuracy comparable to the original implementation.

Conclusion: The DF-based MPCC implementation substantially lowers computational cost while preserving accuracy, enabling application to larger systems where MP2 performs poorly.

Abstract: An improved algorithm to implement our previously proposed static quantum embedding method MPCC (J. Chem. Phys. 161, 164107 (2024)) is presented. It uses the density-fitting (DF) approximation for the electron-repulsion integrals (ERIs) across all layers of the embedding method: the low-level method, the screened interaction, and the high-level method. Additionally, an approximate factorized solution of the Sylvester equation is employed based on the Laplace transformation for the low-level method. To build the screened interaction, we have explored an approximate construction of it based on the distinguishable cluster approximation (DCA) by Kats and Manby (J. Chem. Phys. 141, 061101 (2014)), which amounts to neglecting certain higher-order exchange and ladder diagrams. Combining all these techniques, we have obtained an $\mathcal{O}(N^4)$ algorithm for the low-level method; a non-iterative $N_{\rm frag} \mathcal{O}(N^4)$ algorithm for the screened interaction; and an iterative $\mathcal{O}(N_{\rm frag}^6)$ algorithm for the fragment solver, where $N_{\rm frag}$ is the number of fragment orbitals and $N$ is the total number of orbitals. The resulting low-scaling implementation of the MPCC method is applied to trans polyacetylenes, that is, C$_{2n}$H$_{2n+2}$ molecules for chain lengths $n=1-8$, and the timing and accuracy of the method are compared to the original unfactorized implementation. Moreover, the approximations are further validated by applying the method to the potential energy surface of the \ce{O3} molecule. Finally, we apply the method to the selected molecules in the S22 dataset for which the performance of the MP2 method is known to be poor. The results demonstrate that the DF-based implementation of the MPCC method provides a significant reduction in computational cost while maintaining accuracy comparable to that of the original implementation.

</details>


### [83] [Accelerating Quantum Simulations of Materials Through Parameter and Ansatz Transfer Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33617)
*Saurabh Shivpuje,Vinit Singh,Manas Sajjan,Sabre Kais*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This work develops methods to transfer variational information between related Hamiltonians—parameter reuse for equal embedded sizes and AARTI for different sizes—to accelerate quantum simulations of condensed-matter systems, demonstrated on Li$_x$CoO$_2$ cathode Hamiltonians.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical condensed-matter quantum simulations require solving large families of related Hamiltonians arising from k-point sampling, compositional variation, defects, and system-size changes, which is computationally expensive since each Hamiltonian is typically optimized independently.

Method: Transfer optimized variational parameters between related calculations of the same embedded size; introduce Augmented Ansatz Reuse for Target Initialization (AARTI) for different sizes, transferring compatible Pauli-generator structure and optimized source parameters then augmenting the ansatz with target-specific generators. The workflow integrates DFT, Wannier downfolding, embedded tight-binding Hamiltonians, variational quantum algorithms, and neural-network quantum states, with quantum-circuit simulations on NVIDIA's CUDA-Q platform.

Result: Using Li$_x$CoO$_2$ as a battery cathode, across delithiation levels, dense k-point meshes, and increasing sizes, parameter and ansatz transfer substantially reduce optimization steps while maintaining target accuracy, with lower terminal errors in several cases. The electronic-structure evolution with delithiation shows pronounced non-rigid-band behavior consistent with experiment.

Conclusion: The results establish a framework for exploiting similarity among related Hamiltonians to improve the efficiency and scalability of quantum workflows for realistic condensed-matter systems.

Abstract: Quantum computing offers a promising route for electronic-structure calculations, but practical condensed-matter applications often require solving large families of related Hamiltonians arising from $\mathbf{k}$-point sampling, compositional variation, defects, and changes in system size. Here, we develop strategies for transferring variational information between related Hamiltonians to accelerate quantum simulations. For Hamiltonians of the same embedded size, optimized variational parameters are transferred between related calculations. For different sizes, we introduce Augmented Ansatz Reuse for Target Initialization (AARTI), which transfers compatible Pauli-generator structure and optimized source parameters, then augments the ansatz with target-specific generators. Our workflow combines density functional theory, Wannier downfolding, embedded tight-binding Hamiltonians, variational quantum algorithms, and neural-network quantum states, with quantum-circuit simulations implemented using NVIDIA's CUDA-Q platform. Using Li$_x$CoO$_2$ as a representative battery cathode material, we study Hamiltonian families spanning multiple delithiation levels, dense $\mathbf{k}$-point meshes, and increasing sizes. Parameter and ansatz transfer substantially reduce the optimization steps required to solve these related Hamiltonians while maintaining the common target accuracy, with lower terminal errors in several cases. The calculated electronic-structure evolution with delithiation exhibits pronounced non-rigid-band behavior consistent with experiment. These results establish a framework for exploiting similarity among related Hamiltonians to improve the efficiency and scalability of quantum workflows for realistic condensed-matter systems.

</details>


### [84] [Basis Functions for Time-Dependent Kohn-Sham Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34164)
*Paul M. Zimmerman,Joshua Kammeraad,Duy-Khoi Dang,Harish S. Bhat*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Floquet theory is used to derive an inversion ansatz for time-dependent Kohn-Sham DFT, recovering exchange-correlation potentials from time-dependent wavefunctions with low density and current errors, aiding nonadiabatic functional development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-dependent Kohn-Sham DFT requires exchange-correlation potentials, and nonadiabatic functionals are hard to develop. Inverting time-dependent wavefunctions to obtain these potentials is therefore valuable, but a principled inversion scheme is lacking.

Method: Floquet theory is applied to time-dependent Kohn-Sham DFT. Mathematical derivations show that the fundamental frequencies of a time-dependent wavefunction solution are the leading-order harmonics of the TD-KS state, yielding a Floquet-based inversion ansatz. This ansatz is tested numerically in 1D and 3D for atomic and molecular cases.

Result: Numerical tests in 1D and 3D for atomic and molecular systems demonstrate the ansatz's utility, with low L2 errors in the time-dependent density and longitudinal current—even though currents were not explicitly optimized.

Conclusion: The proposed inversion ansatz extracts exchange-correlation potentials from time-dependent wavefunctions, is highly interpretable, and may substantially aid the development of nonadiabatic density functionals.

Abstract: Floquet theory provides insight into the inversion of time-dependent Kohn-Sham density functional theory. Specifically, mathematical derivations show that the fundamental frequencies of a time-dependent wavefunction solution are the leading-order harmonics for the TD-KS state. Numerical tests of the resulting ansatz in 1D and 3D for atomic and molecular cases demonstrate its utility. In particular, low $L_{2}$ errors in the time-dependent density and longitudinal current were found, even though currents were not an explicit optimization objective. In all, the proposed inversion ansatz provides exchange-correlation potentials from time-dependent wavefunctions, is highly interpretable, and may significantly help in the development of nonadiabatic density functionals.

</details>


### [85] [Machine-learned Laplacian-level density functional from exact exchange-correlation potentials and energies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34194)
*Arghadwip Paul,Bikash Kanungo,Sambit Das,Vikram Gavini*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: NNLap is a machine-learned Laplacian-level exchange-correlation functional that augments PBE with a neural-network correction based on density, gradient, and Laplacian. Trained on exact XC potentials/energies from only eight systems, it rivals meta-GGAs like SCAN and r2SCAN on thermochemistry and total energies, without orbital dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Meta-GGA functionals achieve high accuracy but rely on orbital-dependent ingredients (kinetic energy density). The authors aim to show that a Laplacian-level model, which avoids orbital dependence, can match meta-GGA accuracy when trained on exact XC potentials and energies.

Method: NNLap augments the PBE functional with a neural-network correction depending on the electron density, its gradient, and its Laplacian. It is trained on exact XC potentials and energies obtained via inverse DFT calculations on configuration-interaction densities, using only five atoms and three molecules.

Result: Despite training on few systems, NNLap achieves remarkable accuracy on thermochemistry benchmarks, competing with SCAN and r2SCAN. It also attains accurate total energies comparable to SCAN and better than r2SCAN and B3LYP.

Conclusion: A Laplacian-level model trained on exact XC potentials and energies can reach meta-GGA accuracy without orbital dependence, demonstrating the viability of orbital-free Laplacian-level machine-learned functionals.

Abstract: We present NNLap, a machine-learned Laplacian-level exchange-correlation (XC) functional that augments PBE with a neural-network correction depending on the electron density, its gradient, and its Laplacian. The model is trained on exact XC potentials and energies, obtained through inverse density-functional theory (DFT) calculations on configuration-interaction densities. Despite training on only a few systems -- five atoms and three molecules -- the model achieves remarkable accuracy on thermochemistry benchmarks, competing with the meta-GGA functionals SCAN and r2SCAN. It also attains accurate total energies, comparable to SCAN and better than r2SCAN and B3LYP. This shows that a Laplacian-level model, trained on exact XC potentials and energies, can reach the accuracy of meta-GGAs without their orbital dependence.

</details>


### [86] [Curvature-Aware Flow Matching for Molecular Structure Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34592)
*Samir Darouich,Juliane Geisler,Jacob W. Toney,Tanja Bien,Johannes Kästner,Heather J. Kulik,Mathias Niepert*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: CurvFM introduces curvature-constrained flow matching, supervising the Hessian of the potential energy surface via a frozen machine-learned predictor at generated and reference structures, improving physical fidelity of 3D molecular generation without quantum-chemical Hessians.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative models for 3D molecular structures achieve high geometric accuracy but are trained purely on atomic coordinate distributions without explicit physics. The potential energy surface (PES) captures local landscape information only indirectly through RMSD to a reference. First-order quantities (forces) vanish at stationary points like conformers and transition states, so only the Hessian (local curvature) carries useful signal. Quantum-chemical Hessians are too costly at training scale.

Method: CurvFM is a flow matching formulation whose training objective constrains the curvature at the generated structure. A frozen machine-learned Hessian prediction model is evaluated at both the generated endpoint and the reference structure, eliminating the need for quantum-chemical Hessians and keeping the residual cheap enough to apply at every training step.

Result: Across transition state and conformer generation tasks, CurvFM substantially improves physical fidelity while maintaining unchanged global structural accuracy. It reduces the median deviation in maximum force by 63% on GEOM-QM9 and raises the fraction of valid transition states by up to 10% across three reaction datasets.

Conclusion: Direct curvature supervision of the PES through a frozen ML Hessian predictor is an inexpensive and effective way to inject physical fidelity into molecular generative models, improving force accuracy and transition-state validity without sacrificing structural accuracy.

Abstract: Generative models produce three-dimensional molecular structures with high geometric accuracy, yet they are trained on distributions of atomic coordinates without explicit access to the underlying physics. The potential energy surface (PES) describes how the energy changes around a molecular geometry, and the RMSD between a generated structure and its reference captures this local landscape only indirectly. Supervising the PES during training could close this gap, but conformers and transition states (TSs) are stationary points at which the forces vanish, so first-order quantities carry little information about them. What remains is the local curvature, encoded in the Hessian of the PES. Quantum-chemical Hessians are too expensive to be available at the scale required for training, but recently proposed machine-learned predictors make curvature inexpensive enough to supervise directly. We introduce CurvFM, a flow matching (FM) formulation whose training objective constrains the curvature at the generated structure. A frozen Hessian prediction model is evaluated at the generated endpoint and at the reference structure, which removes the need for quantum-chemical Hessians and keeps the residual cheap enough to impose at every training step. Across TS and conformer generation, CurvFM substantially improves physical fidelity at unchanged global structural accuracy, reducing the median deviation in maximum force by 63% on GEOM-QM9 and raising the fraction of valid TSs by up to 10% across three reaction datasets.

</details>


### [87] [Symmetry-Aware Flow Matching for End-to-end Molecular Crystal Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34690)
*Wendi Cai,Fanyang Mo*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: SALA is a flow-matching generative model with state-context separation that evolves only asymmetric-unit coordinates and symmetry-compatible lattice parameters while conditioning on the full periodic environment, achieving 72.0% hit rate on 799 held-out crystals versus 8.1% for an atomistic full-cell baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molecular crystal packing governs properties from drug bioavailability to charge transport, but generating realistic structures is hard because it requires jointly handling molecular flexibility, intermolecular interactions, and crystallographic symmetry.

Method: SALA is a flow-matching model based on state-context separation: it generates only asymmetric-unit (ASU) atomic coordinates and symmetry-compatible lattice parameters, while predicting their updates from the full periodic environment. It is conditioned on molecular graphs and a space group, enabling end-to-end generation of conformation, lattice geometry, and packing under preserved symmetry.

Result: Across 799 held-out crystals covering four chemical classes and all seven crystal systems, SALA reaches a 72.0% hit rate from 50 candidates per target versus 8.1% for an atomistic full-cell generative baseline. Mean best-candidate packing RMSD drops from 4.48 to 1.70 Å, with the advantage growing as system size and symmetry multiplicity increase.

Conclusion: SALA enables end-to-end generation of complex, symmetry-constrained molecular crystals, with potential applications in crystal structure prediction, structure-based pharmaceutical polymorph design, and organic molecular materials discovery.

Abstract: Molecular crystal packing shapes properties from drug bioavailability to charge transport, yet generating realistic structures requires coordinating molecular flexibility, intermolecular interactions and symmetry. Here we introduce SALA, a flow-matching model built on state--context separation: it evolves only asymmetric-unit (ASU) atomic coordinates and symmetry-compatible lattice parameters while predicting their updates from the full periodic environment. Conditioned on molecular graphs and a space group, SALA generates molecular conformation, lattice geometry and packing end to end while preserving the specified symmetry. Across 799 held-out crystals spanning four chemical classes and all seven crystal systems, SALA achieves a 72.0\% hit rate from 50 candidates per target, compared with 8.1\% for an atomistic full-cell generative baseline. Mean best-candidate packing root-mean-square deviation decreases from 4.48 to 1.70~Å, with the advantage widening as system size and symmetry multiplicity increase. SALA thus enables end-to-end generation of complex, symmetry-constrained molecular crystals, with potential applications in CSP, structure-based pharmaceutical polymorph design and organic molecular materials discovery.

</details>


### [88] [Non-equilibrium cavity pumping of electronic molecular polaritons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35116)
*Yassir El Moutaoukal,Rosario R. Riso,Henrik Koch*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a photonic counterpart of collective strong coupling: a single molecule interacting with a cavity mode holding many photons (a steady pumped-cavity state). Using a photon-number-constrained QED Hartree-Fock approach with a Lagrange multiplier, the authors show the field behaves like a static classical field, offering photon density as a control parameter for cavity-modified molecular chemistry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strong light-matter coupling is typically achieved by collectively coupling many molecules to one cavity mode. The authors explore the opposite, photonic regime—one molecule coupled to a mode with many photons—as a route to non-invasively modify molecular properties. The challenge is that such a pumped steady state carries no transverse electric field, so it cannot be treated as an excited state of the light-matter system, requiring a new theoretical treatment.

Method: The photon number of a strong-coupling QED Hartree-Fock wave function is constrained with a Lagrange multiplier, and the energy is self-consistently minimized over all parameters. The constrained reference state carries the field of a coherent state with an average photon number, generated by a cavity mode whose effective free-field frequency is lowered by the multiplier. The framework is applied to benzene, a benzene-water complex, and hydrogen peroxide.

Result: For benzene and the benzene-water complex, the pumped cavity polarizes the molecules and modifies their interaction in a manner analogous to a static classical field. For hydrogen peroxide, the cavity field reshapes the torsional potential and the far-infrared torsional spectrum. The classical driven-field limit is shown to emerge naturally from the quantized mean-field description.

Conclusion: The classical driven-field limit emerges from a quantized mean-field description, and photon density becomes a control parameter for cavity-modified chemistry, enabling non-invasive modification of molecular properties via a single molecule coupled to a many-photon cavity mode.

Abstract: Strong light-matter coupling in optical cavities offers a non-invasive route to modify molecular properties, and it is usually reached by collectively coupling many molecules to the same mode. Here we consider the photonic counterpart of the collective regime, in which a single molecule interacts with a cavity mode holding many photons, a steady state of the pumped cavity. Since the eigenstates of the Pauli-Fierz Hamiltonian carry no transverse electric field, such a state cannot be described as an excited state of the light-matter system. We therefore constrain the photon number of the strong coupling QED Hartree-Fock wave function with a Lagrange multiplier and self consistently minimize the energy with respect to all parameters. The constrained reference carries the field of a coherent state with an average number of photons, generated by a cavity mode whose effective free-field frequency is lowered by the multiplier. We apply this framework to benzene and a benzene-water complex, where the pumped cavity polarizes the molecules and modifies their interaction as with a static classical field. For hydrogen peroxide, the field can reshape the torsional potential and the far-infrared torsional spectrum. The classical driven field limit thus emerges from a quantized mean field description, and the photon density becomes a control parameter for cavity-modified chemistry.

</details>


### [89] [Experimental and numerical study of shock wave propagation in water generated by pulsed arc electrohydraulic discharges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35305)
*Wen Chen,Olivier Maurel,Christian La Borderie,Thierry Reess,Antoine Silvestre de Ferron,Mohammed Matallah,Gilles Pijaudier-Cabot,Jacques Antoine,Franck Rey-Bethbeder*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: A finite element simulation (EUROPLEXUS) reproduces underwater shock wave propagation from pulsed arc electrohydraulic discharges, aimed at distributed microcracking of tight gas reservoir rocks as an alternative to hydraulic fracturing; predicted pressures match experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work supports a broader research program on an alternative stimulation technique to conventional hydraulic fracturing in tight gas reservoirs, seeking a distributed microcracking state in rock rather than a localized fracture. Dynamic blasts are considered as a means to increase rock permeability.

Method: The shock wave is generated in water by pulsed arc electrohydraulic discharges producing pressures of several kilobars within microseconds. The injected electrical energy is modeled in a simplified way as a local augmentation of water enthalpy. Fast fluid dynamics computations are carried out with the finite element code EUROPLEXUS.

Result: The predicted pressure agrees with experimental results. The simulation also yields shock wave propagation characteristics that serve as reference data for designing underwater structural elements and for underwater explosion engineering.

Conclusion: The simplified enthalpy-augmentation approach combined with EUROPLEXUS is validated against experiments and provides a useful predictive tool for electrohydraulic shock wave stimulation and underwater explosion applications.

Abstract: The objective of this study is to simulate the propagation of the shock wave in water due to an explosion. The study is part of a global research program on the development of an alternative stimulation technique to conventional hydraulic fracturing in tight gas reservoirs aimed at inducing a distributed state of microcracking of rocks instead of localized fracture. We consider the possibility of increasing the permeability of rocks with dynamic blasts. The blast is a shock wave generated in water by pulsed arc electrohydraulic discharges. The amplitude of these shock waves is prescribed by the electrohydraulic discharges which generate high pressures of several kilobars within microseconds. A simplified method has been used to simulate the injected electrical energy as augmentation of enthalpy in water locally. The finite element code EUROPLEXUS is used to perform fluid fast dynamic computation. The predicted pressure is consistent with the experimental results. In addition, shock wave propagation characteristics predicted with simulation can be valuable reference for design of underwater structural elements and engineering of underwater explosion.

</details>

### [90] [A reduced-order analysis for multi-reflection-driven laser absorptance in vaporization-induced cavities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32089)
*Haolin Liu,Haoran Shi,Xuzhe Zeng,Brian J. Simonds,Wenda Tan,Anthony D. Rollett,Levent Burak Kara*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: A reduced-order Beer-Lambert-type framework using an effective per-reflection optical depth (EPROD) predicts laser absorptance in vaporization-induced cavities, achieving ~2.61% median error with a single fitted parameter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multiple internal reflections govern laser absorption in vaporization-induced cavities (e.g., keyholes), but linking ray histories, cavity geometry, and absorptance conventionally demands full ray tracing, which is computationally expensive. A reduced-order model is needed for efficient energy-coupling prediction.

Method: The authors develop an EPROD-based attenuation framework. A power-weighted formulation defines an absorptance-equivalent interaction count under constant effective single-interaction absorptance, and a cumulant expansion separates the EPROD-independent mean interaction count from ray-path heterogeneity corrections. Validation spans nine simulation groups (three alloys, three spot diameters) plus an independent synchronized Ti-64 experiment.

Result: The mean interaction count follows a unit-intercept linear depth relation (R-squared 0.7824-0.9843). Its slope varies only 3.4%-6.3% across alloys at fixed spot diameter and decreases with increasing spot diameter. Fixing this slope from ray statistics yields a one-parameter absorptance-depth model with 2.61% MAPE. The independent Ti-64 experiment matches simulation parameters within ~2.4%.

Conclusion: The framework cleanly separates geometric interaction accumulation from optical attenuation, enabling efficient energy-coupling prediction and absorptance-based keyhole characterization without full ray tracing.

Abstract: Multiple internal reflections enhance laser absorption in vaporization-induced cavities, but connecting ray histories, cavity geometry, and absorptance typically requires full ray tracing. We develop a reduced-order framework based on Beer-Lambert-type attenuation governed by an effective per-reflection optical depth (EPROD). A power-weighted formulation defines an absorptance-equivalent interaction count under constant effective single-interaction absorptance, while a cumulant expansion separates the EPROD-independent mean interaction count from ray-path heterogeneity corrections. Across nine simulation groups spanning three alloys and three spot diameters, the mean interaction count follows a unit-intercept linear depth relation with R-squared values of 0.7824-0.9843. Its slope varies by only 3.4%-6.3% across alloys at fixed spot diameter and decreases with increasing spot diameter. Fixing this slope from ray statistics yields an absorptance-depth model with one fitted parameter and a median prediction error (MAPE) of 2.61%. An independent synchronized Ti-64 experiment yields model parameters within approximately 2.4% of corresponding simulation values. This framework separates geometric interaction accumulation from optical attenuation, enabling efficient energy-coupling prediction and absorptance-based keyhole characterization.

</details>


### [91] [A 20-kV Optical-Injection-Enhanced 4H-SiC Reverse-Conducting IGBT With Snapback-Free Operation and Low On-State Voltage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32437)
*Yujian Chen,Guoliang Zhang,Ruijun Zhang,Liangxun Yue,Zhanwei Shen,Feng Zhang,Rong Zhang*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: A 20-kV 4H-SiC optical-injection-enhanced RC-IGBT (OIE-RC-IGBT) uses integrated LEDs for optical injection, achieving snapback-free operation and low on-state voltage with reduced MOS cells and improved turn-off performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional ultrahigh-voltage SiC RC-IGBTs suffer from the snapback effect and high on-state voltage, requiring many MOS cells to suppress snapback, which degrades performance. A method is needed to eliminate snapback while lowering on-state voltage and improving turn-off characteristics.

Method: LEDs are integrated into the gate structure to provide optical injection during forward conduction. The emitted light generates high-concentration photogenerated carriers in the carrier-storage layer (CSL) and drift region, reducing JFET and drift region resistance, increasing device current, and turning on the collector-side P+/N junction earlier. This suppresses snapback, facilitates bipolar conduction transition, and enhances conductivity modulation.

Result: TCAD simulations show that compared with the conventional RC-IGBT (C-RC-IGBT), the proposed device achieves snapback-free operation while reducing required MOS cells from 25 to 5. On-state voltage, turn-off fall time, and turn-off energy loss are reduced by 22.37%, 25.06%, and 19.69%, respectively.

Conclusion: Optical injection is a promising approach for improving the conduction and turn-off performance of ultrahigh-voltage SiC RC-IGBTs.

Abstract: This letter proposes a 20-kV 4H-SiC optical-injection-enhanced reverse-conducting IGBT (OIE-RC-IGBT) with snapback-free operation and low on-state voltage. Light-emitting diodes (LEDs) are integrated into the gate structure to provide optical injection during forward conduction. The emitted light generates a high concentration of photogenerated carriers in the carrier-storage layer (CSL) and drift region, thereby reducing the resistance of the JFET and drift regions and increasing the device current. Consequently, the collector-side P+/N junction is turned on earlier, effectively suppressing the snapback effect and facilitating the transition to bipolar conduction. After bipolar conduction is established, the photogenerated carriers further enhance conductivity modulation, resulting in a reduced on-state voltage. TCAD simulation results show that, compared with the conventional RC-IGBT (C-RC-IGBT), the proposed device achieves snapback-free operation while reducing the required number of MOS cells from 25 to 5. Moreover, the on-state voltage, turn-off fall time, and turn-off energy loss are reduced by 22.37%, 25.06%, and 19.69%, respectively. These results demonstrate the potential of optical injection for improving the conduction and turn-off performance of ultrahigh-voltage SiC RC-IGBTs.

</details>


### [92] [Adaptive metamaterial resonators: a discrete and continuous mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34632)
*Vanessa Cool,Elke Deckers,Frank Naets*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This work introduces two adaptive resonator concepts enabling in-situ tuning of locally resonant metamaterials' resonance characteristics via discrete and continuous mechanisms, providing a passive alternative to active control for time-varying applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Locally resonant metamaterials offer lightweight vibration and noise attenuation but suffer from a bandgap fixed at the design stage. Since excitation frequencies drift with operating conditions and loading, fixed-frequency resonators lose effectiveness. Existing remedies—active control (requiring sensors, actuators, and continuous power) or broadband/multi-resonator designs—introduce cost or trade attenuation depth for bandwidth.

Method: Two adaptive resonator concepts are presented that allow in-situ modification of local resonance characteristics through discrete and continuous adaptation mechanisms.

Result: The proposed passive adaptive resonators enable adaptation of resonance characteristics directly within the operating environment, avoiding the attenuation-depth-versus-bandwidth trade-off and removing reliance on active control hardware and power.

Conclusion: Passive adaptive resonators broaden the applicability of locally resonant metamaterials to real-world, time-varying scenarios, offering a route beyond fixed-bandgap and active-control limitations.

Abstract: Locally resonant metamaterials are an established route to lightweight attenuation of vibration and noise, but their performance is tied to a bandgap fixed at the design stage. In practice, excitation frequencies are rarely constant, as they can vary with operating conditions and loading, causing fixed-frequency resonators to lose effectiveness as conditions drift from their design point. Existing strategies to address this rely on active control, introducing sensors, actuators, and a continuous power supply, or on broadband and multi-resonator designs that trade attenuation depth for bandwidth. A passive alternative, capable of adapting its resonance characteristics directly within the operating environment, would remove this trade-off and open locally resonant metamaterials to a much wider range of real-world, time-varying applications. In this work, we present two adaptive resonator concepts that enable in-situ modification of local resonance characteristics through discrete and continuous adaptation mechanisms.

</details>


### [93] [Far-field excitation of symmetry-protected bound states in the continuum by nonlinear virtual sources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34979)
*Marc Martí-Sabaté,Yijie Zhang,Shengming Sun,Ruxin Li,Yabin Jin,Junfei Li,Daniel Torrent Martí*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates non-invasive excitation of a symmetry-protected acoustic bound state in the continuum (BIC) using nonlinear virtual sources generated at the structure surface, achieving efficient excitation without perturbing the protected state's non-radiative nature or degrading its quality factor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BICs offer complete wave confinement with ideally infinite quality factors and extreme field enhancement, but suffer a fundamental paradox: the mechanism suppressing radiation also prevents efficient far-field excitation. Existing approaches introduce external coupling channels that perturb the protected state and reduce confinement. A method is needed to inject energy into a BIC without opening additional radiative channels.

Method: The authors focus time-modulated ultrasonic beams onto a metamirror, where nonlinear demodulation produces localized low-frequency sources whose spatial phase matches the symmetry of the target mode while avoiding physical coupling to the resonator. They develop a theoretical framework describing mode formation and symmetry protection, realize a compact experimental implementation through symmetry reduction, and directly measure the confined pressure field and its quality factor.

Result: Nonlinear demodulation generated localized low-frequency virtual sources that excited the symmetry-protected acoustic BIC without opening additional radiative channels, preserving its intrinsic non-radiative character. The confined pressure field and its quality factor were directly measured, confirming successful non-invasive excitation through symmetry reduction.

Conclusion: Nonlinear virtual sources provide a route to accessing symmetry-protected states in open systems while maintaining their exceptional confinement properties. This offers a general strategy for wave manipulation and high-sensitivity resonant devices across diverse physical platforms.

Abstract: Bound states in the continuum (BICs) enable complete wave confinement within open systems through destructive interference or symmetry protection, giving rise to ideally infinite quality factors and extreme field enhancement. Their practical exploitation, however, is hindered by a fundamental paradox: the same mechanism that suppresses radiation also prevents efficient excitation from the far field. Existing experimental approaches typically overcome this limitation by introducing external coupling channels that inevitably perturb the protected state and reduce its confinement. Here we demonstrate the non-invasive excitation of a symmetry-protected acoustic BIC through nonlinear virtual sources generated directly at the surface of the structure. By focusing time-modulated ultrasonic beams onto a metamirror, nonlinear demodulation produces localized low-frequency sources whose spatial phase matches the symmetry of the target mode while avoiding any physical coupling to the resonator. This approach injects energy into the BIC without opening additional radiative channels, preserving its intrinsic non-radiative character. We develop a theoretical framework describing the formation and symmetry protection of the mode, realize a compact experimental implementation through symmetry reduction, and directly measure the confined pressure field and its quality factor. Our results establish nonlinear virtual sources as a route to accessing symmetry-protected states in open systems while maintaining their exceptional confinement properties, providing a general strategy for wave manipulation and high-sensitivity resonant devices across physical platforms.

</details>
