### [94] [Constrained surrogates for arbitrary families of continuous probability distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31695)
*Jack Murdoch Moore,Eduardo G Altmann*

Main category: physics.data-an

TL;DR: The paper introduces a general framework for constructing constrained surrogates that represent arbitrary families of continuous distributions, providing algorithms for canonical distributions (powerlaw, lognormal, exponential, truncated Gaussian) to enable unbiased estimation and model validity tests without parameter specification, illustrated across diverse applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parametrized families of probability distributions are widely used to interpret and explain complex systems, but a recurring problem is determining the extent to which such descriptions are supported by observations and how they can characterize the underlying system. Existing methods typically require specifying or estimating model parameters, which can introduce bias and limit the rigor of model validity assessment.

Method: The authors develop a general framework for constructing constrained surrogates capable of representing arbitrary families of continuous distributions. They provide explicit algorithms for several canonical distributions in statistical physics, including continuous powerlaw, lognormal, exponential, and truncated Gaussian distributions. These constrained surrogates are designed to support unbiased estimation and accurate tests of model validity based on arbitrary physically meaningful sample statistics, without requiring specification or estimation of model parameters.

Result: The framework enables unbiased estimation and accurate model validity tests that avoid parameter specification or estimation. The theoretical advantages translate into more informative and nuanced conclusions when applied to diverse empirical systems, including bushfire magnitudes, word usage in texts, brain connectomes, and city populations.

Conclusion: Constrained surrogates offer a general and effective tool for validating and interpreting parametrized distributional descriptions of complex systems, yielding richer, parameter-free inferences across varied real-world applications.

Abstract: Empirical regularities described by parametrized families of probability distributions can shape how complex systems are interpreted and explained. A recurring problem is to determine the extent to which these descriptions are supported by observations, and how they can be used to characterize the underlying system. Here, we introduce a general framework for constructing constrained surrogates capable of representing arbitrary families of continuous distributions. We demonstrate its utility by providing explicit algorithms for several canonical distributions in statistical physics, including continuous powerlaw, lognormal, exponential, and truncated Gaussian distributions. These constrained surrogates enable unbiased estimation and accurate tests of model validity based on arbitrary (physically meaningful) sample statistics, without requiring specification or estimation of model parameters. We show how these theoretical advantages translate into more informative and nuanced conclusions in applications ranging from bushfire magnitudes and word usage in texts to brain connectomes and city populations.

</details>

### [95] [Nanodiamond Sensing of Stray Fields during Domain Reversal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31950)
*Kin On Ho,Hamza Abudayyeh,Andrew Perez,Hui-Ping Chang,Liang Juan Chang,Junwei Tong,Can Cui,Jean Anne C. Incorvia,Xiaoqin Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Nanodiamonds with ensemble NV centers act as local quantum sensors to quantitatively map domain-reversal stray fields in a CoFeB strip, revealing three distinct position-dependent responses explained by micromagnetic simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantitative measurement of nanoscale stray magnetic fields during domain reversal is important for understanding and optimizing magnetic memory and logic devices, but achieving high spatial resolution with minimally invasive probes remains challenging.

Method: Nanodiamonds hosting an ensemble of nitrogen vacancy (NV) centers are used as local quantum sensors of domain-reversal stray fields in a CoFeB strip. Optically detected magnetic resonance spectra are analyzed via a fully constrained fitting procedure to enable robust field extraction even when resonances are only partially resolved.

Result: During magnetic-field-driven reversal, the ND sensors resolve three distinct local responses depending on position relative to the strip: a positive frequency jump at the edge, an unexpected negative jump just outside the strip, and a negligible change near the center. These contrasting signals are quantitatively explained by magnetostatic boundary fields from micromagnetic simulations.

Conclusion: The results establish NV-ND magnetometry as a simple, transferable, and quantitative approach for characterizing spatially heterogeneous magnetic fields in thin films and spintronic devices.

Abstract: Quantitative measurement of nanoscale stray magnetic fields during domain reversal is important for understanding and optimizing magnetic memory and logic devices. Yet, achieving high spatial resolution with minimally invasive probes remains challenging. Here we demonstrate nanodiamonds (NDs) hosting an ensemble nitrogen vacancy (NV) centers as local quantum sensors of domain-reversal stray fields in a CoFeB strip. During magnetic-field-driven reversal, the ND sensors resolve three distinct local responses depending on their positions relative to the strip: a positive frequency jump at the edge, an unexpected negative jump just outside the strip, and a negligible change near the center. These contrasting signals are quantitatively explained by magnetostatic boundary fields from micromagnetic simulations. We further implement a fully constrained fitting procedure for optically detected magnetic resonance spectra, enabling robust field extraction even when resonances are partially resolved. Together, these results establish NV-ND magnetometry as a simple, transferable, and quantitative approach for characterizing spatially heterogeneous magnetic fields in thin-films and spintronic devices.

</details>


### [96] [Magnetic Q-balls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32059)
*A. J. Balseyro Sebastian,Keisuke Ohashi,Muneto Nitta*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper studies charged soliton branches (polar Q-balls, Q-kinks, and charged droplets) in quasi-1D chiral magnets with DM interaction, easy-axis anisotropy, and Zeeman coupling, showing that their existence and properties depend crucially on whether the dynamics are antiferromagnetic (second-order time derivatives) or ferromagnetic (Berry-phase).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify how charged-soliton mechanisms depend on the underlying magnetic dynamics even when the static chiral energy functional is identical, by comparing two dynamical completions of the same static model.

Method: On the DM-selected helical branch, the problem is reduced to an analytically tractable 1D system for the polar angle of the order parameter. Existence conditions for polar Q-balls are derived from the curvature of the reduced effective potential and a nonzero turning point. The analysis is carried out separately for an antiferromagnetic model (second-order time derivatives) and a ferromagnetic model (Berry-phase dynamics).

Result: In the antiferromagnetic case, the allowed frequency window is symmetric and can be fully closed by combined DM coupling and Zeeman field; at zero Zeeman field, antiferromagnetic Q-kinks appear with explicit profiles, charges, energies, and reduced-sector fission criteria. In the ferromagnetic case, the Berry phase makes rotation frequency act as a shifted Zeeman field, so north- and south-pole charged droplets are selected by opposite signs of the shifted rotation. A formal pole-to-pole solution of the ferromagnetic mechanical problem generally does not correspond to a finite-energy magnetic soliton, since the Berry term does not renormalize the physical Hamiltonian.

Conclusion: Charged-soliton mechanisms in quasi-1D chiral magnets are qualitatively different for antiferromagnetic versus ferromagnetic dynamics, despite sharing the same static chiral energy, underscoring the role of the dynamical completion in determining soliton existence and character.

Abstract: We study charged soliton branches in quasi-one-dimensional chiral magnetic systems with Dzyaloshinskii--Moriya (DM) interaction, easy-axis anisotropy, and Zeeman coupling. The same static magnetic functional is equipped with two different dynamical completions: an antiferromagnetic model with second-order time derivatives and a ferromagnetic model with Berry-phase dynamics. On the helical branch selected by the static DM interaction, the problem reduces to an analytically tractable one-dimensional system for the polar angle of the order parameter. We derive the existence conditions for polar Q-balls from the curvature of the reduced effective potential and the presence of a nonzero turning point. In the antiferromagnetic case, the allowed frequency window is symmetric and can be completely closed by the combined effect of the DM coupling and the Zeeman field. At zero Zeeman field, the same reduction also supports antiferromagnetic Q-kinks, for which we obtain explicit profiles, charges, energies, and reduced-sector fission criteria. In the ferromagnetic case, the Berry phase makes the rotation frequency act as a shifted Zeeman field. As a result, north- and south-pole charged droplets are selected by opposite signs of the shifted rotation. We also show that a formal pole-to-pole solution of the ferromagnetic mechanical problem does not generally correspond to a finite-energy magnetic soliton, because the Berry term does not renormalize the physical Hamiltonian. These results clarify how charged-soliton mechanisms depend on the underlying magnetic dynamics, even when the static chiral energy is the same.

</details>


### [97] [In-Memory AM Demodulation Using an All-Silicon Independent-Dual-Gate Gain-Cell Memory with $<10^{-22}$ A Leakage Determined by Single-Electron Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32122)
*Katsuhiko Nishiguchi,Toshiaki Hayashi,Kensaku Chida,Takase Shimizu,Gento Yamahata,Seiya Kasai*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: An all-silicon independent-dual-gate memory with leakage below 10^-22 A and retention exceeding 1000 s enables in-memory AM demodulation via the access transistor's subthreshold nonlinearity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultra-low-leakage memories are needed for in-memory sensing and computing, but silicon memories struggle to achieve retention long enough for analog signal processing due to leakage through the access transistor.

Method: The authors fabricated an all-silicon independent-dual-gate memory and inferred its leakage current from single-electron counting statistics. They exploited the access transistor's subthreshold nonlinearity to perform in-memory amplitude-modulation (AM) demodulation without disturbing the stored charge.

Result: The memory exhibits leakage current below 10^-22 A and a measured retention time exceeding 1000 s. In-memory AM demodulation is demonstrated owing to the extremely low leakage.

Conclusion: The proposed memory offers a CMOS-compatible platform for ultra-low-power signal processing and in-memory sensing.

Abstract: Ultra-low-leakage memories are attracting increasing attention for in-memory sensing and computing. However, achieving sufficiently long retention in silicon memories for analog signal processing remains challenging because of leakage through the access transistor. In this work, we demonstrate an all-silicon independent-dual-gate memory whose leakage current, inferred from single-electron counting statistics, is below $10^{-22}$ A, giving a measured retention time exceeding 1000 s. Owing to the extremely low leakage, the subthreshold nonlinearity of the access transistor can be exploited without disturbing the stored charge, enabling in-memory amplitude-modulation (AM) demodulation. The proposed memory provides a CMOS-compatible platform for ultra-low-power signal processing and in-memory sensing.

</details>


### [98] [Enhancing magnonic frequency combs via geometric nonlinearity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32173)
*Yushun Fang,Weichao Yu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper proposes geometric nonlinearity, arising from the unit-norm constraint of the magnetization vector, as an independent resource for generating magnonic frequency combs (MFCs). Via transverse Floquet engineering, a transverse drive is converted into a longitudinal parametric modulation, enabling low-threshold comb generation in the linear regime with modulation amplitude scaling quadratically with the driving field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MFC generation approaches rely on dynamic nonlinearity, which typically requires high power thresholds and stringent momentum conservation, limiting performance and accessibility. There is a need for an alternative nonlinear mechanism that circumvents these constraints.

Method: The authors exploit the geometric nonlinearity intrinsic to magnetic systems, which originates from the unit-norm constraint of the magnetization vector. Using transverse Floquet engineering, this geometric constraint converts a transverse drive into a longitudinal parametric modulation. In the low-frequency limit, the four-particle process is reduced to an effective two-magnon modulation.

Result: The approach enables low-threshold comb generation operating in the linear regime. The modulation amplitude scales quadratically with the driving field, and enhanced flatness arises from geometric harmonics.

Conclusion: Geometric nonlinearity provides a new theoretical foundation for enhancing MFC performance and deepens understanding of frequency combs and magnon nonlinear interactions.

Abstract: Magnonic frequency combs (MFCs) generated via internal magnetic nonlinearities have exhibited rich physics beyond their optical counterparts. However, existing approaches rely on dynamic nonlinearity, which typically demands high power thresholds and stringent momentum conservation. Here we show that the geometric nonlinearity intrinsic to magnetic systems, originating from the unit-norm constraint of the magnetization vector, can serve as an independent nonlinear resource for MFC generation. Through transverse Floquet engineering, this geometric constraint converts a transverse drive into a longitudinal parametric modulation. In the low-frequency limit, the four-particle process reduces to an effective two-magnon modulation, enabling low-threshold comb generation in the linear regime, with the modulation amplitude scaling quadratically with the driving field and enhanced flatness arising from geometric harmonics. These results provide a deeper understanding of frequency combs and magnon nonlinear interactions, and offer a new theoretical foundation for enhancing MFC performance.

</details>


### [99] [Helicity-Controlled Hall Transport in Hybrid Topological Magnetic Textures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32372)
*Anton V. Hlushchenko,Julia Kharlan,Mykhailo I. Bratchenko,Aleksei V. Chechkin,Jarosław W. Kłos,Oleg A. Tretiakov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper develops a unified spin-space transformation framework showing that helicity acts as a geometric control parameter to tune, suppress, or steer the Hall effect in current-driven topological magnetic textures, validated by analytical theory and micromagnetic simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological magnetic textures are promising information carriers driven by spin currents, but their motion suffers from a Hall effect that deflects them away from the current direction, reducing transport efficiency and controllability. A systematic understanding of how to control this deflection is lacking.

Method: The authors develop a unified spin-space transformation framework to systematically study hybrid spin textures with different helicities (skyrmions, antiskyrmions, bimerons, antibimerons). They combine analytical theory with micromagnetic simulations, deriving an analytical solution of the generalized Thiele equation that links helicity to current-driven transport.

Result: The analytical solution identifies helicity as a geometric control parameter and yields a simple expression for the Hall angle, enabling its continuous tuning, complete suppression, and deterministic steering along arbitrary in-plane directions. Micromagnetic simulations confirm that the generated spin textures remain stable under Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert dynamics and validate the analytical predictions.

Conclusion: Helicity is established as a versatile control parameter for programmable transport of topological magnetic textures, offering a route to efficient and controllable current-driven motion.

Abstract: Topological magnetic textures can serve as information carriers driven by spin currents. However, their motion is generally accompanied by a Hall effect that deflects them from the current direction, limiting transport efficiency and controllability. We develop a unified spin-space transformation framework enabling a systematic study of hybrid spin textures with different helicities, such as skyrmions, antiskyrmions, bimerons, and antibimerons. Combining analytical theory with micromagnetic simulations, we establish the relation between helicity and current-driven transport. An analytical solution of the generalized Thiele equation identifies helicity as a geometric control parameter and yields a simple expression for the Hall angle, enabling its continuous tuning, complete suppression, and deterministic steering along arbitrary in-plane directions. Micromagnetic simulations confirm that the generated spin textures remain stable under Landau--Lifshitz--Gilbert dynamics and validate the analytical predictions. These results establish helicity as a versatile control parameter for programmable transport of topological magnetic textures.

</details>


### [100] [Electrically switchable one-dimensional quadrupolar excitons in lateral double heterojunctions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32392)
*Ryo Kitaura*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A continuum effective-mass model of a type-II WS2-MoS2-WS2 double heterojunction shows that inter-interface coupling creates parity-split 1D exciton doublets that electrically crossover from quadrupolar (quadratic Stark) to dipolar (linear Stark) states, with geometric tuning via strip width.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neighboring lateral interfaces in a single semiconductor monolayer offer a spatial degree of freedom for controlling one-dimensional charge-transfer excitons, motivating a quantitative understanding of how interface coupling and external fields govern their energy structure and Stark response.

Method: An effective-mass two-particle Hamiltonian with a screened Coulomb interaction is used to model a type-II WS2-MoS2-WS2 double heterojunction, including spatially resolved calculations, projection tests, and variation of strip width and perpendicular electric field.

Result: At zero field, inter-interface coupling yields energetically split even- and odd-parity exciton states with zero permanent dipole. A perpendicular electric field converts the lower state from a quadrupolar superposition with quadratic Stark shift into a predominantly single-interface dipolar exciton with approximately linear shift. Binding energies are about 104 and 100 meV, doublet splitting 4.6 meV, and crossover field 0.87 V/um for a 1.5-nm MoS2 strip. Strip width tunes coupling much more strongly than binding energy.

Conclusion: The results establish a continuum-model route to electrically reconfigurable one-dimensional quadrupolar excitons, with strip width providing geometric control of low-field Stark sensitivity.

Abstract: Two neighboring lateral interfaces provide a spatial degree of freedom for controlling one-dimensional charge-transfer excitons within a single semiconductor monolayer. We investigate a type-II WS2-MoS2-WS2 double heterojunction using an effective-mass two-particle Hamiltonian with a screened Coulomb interaction. For equivalent left and right interfaces at zero electric field,inter-interface coupling produces energetically split even- and odd-parity exciton states, each with zero permanent dipole. An electric field perpendicular to the interfaces continuously converts the lower state from a quadrupolar superposition with a quadratic Stark shift into a predominantly single-interface dipolar exciton with an approximately linear shift. The spatially resolved calculation gives binding energies of approximately 104 and 100 meV, a doublet splitting of 4.6 meV, and a crossover field of 0.87 V/um for a representative 1.5-nm MoS2 strip. Projection tests show that the lowest doublet controls the response near this crossover. Strip width tunes the coupling much more strongly than the binding energy, providing geometric control of the low-field Stark sensitivity. These results establish a continuum-model route to electrically reconfigurable one-dimensional quadrupolar excitons.

</details>


### [101] [Bare-Die Antiferromagnetic Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33460)
*Yu Liu,Zhuoting Han,Zexin Feng,Peixin Qin,Zhiyuan Duan,Yuhao Ye,Zengwei Zhu,Chengyan Zhong,Li Liu,Guojian Zhao,Wenbin Shen,Jingyu Li,Sixu Jiang,Xiaoyang Tan,Xiaoning Wang,Ziang Meng,Chengbao Jiang,Zhiqi Liu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: An intrinsically resilient, strain-mediated antiferromagnetic MnIr/PMN-PT edge processor performs analog in-memory computing (feature extraction and classification) directly on raw signals, remaining functional under extreme conditions (500 K, 55 T, 1.5 Mrad) and enabling speech/object recognition and autonomous drone navigation at ~0.2 fJ per operation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Silicon-based electronics face fundamental limits from quantum tunneling and charge-based mechanisms, restricting miniaturization, write-speed scaling, and environmental robustness. These limits are especially prohibitive for deep-space exploration, where extreme temperatures, ultra-strong magnetic fields, and intense radiation disable conventional electronics without heavy shielding. New device platforms are thus needed for energy-efficient computing in extreme environments.

Method: The authors develop a strain-mediated antiferromagnetic edge processor based on a MnIr/PMN-PT heterostructure, exploiting the magnetoelectric coupling of the piezoelectric PMN-PT substrate to modulate the antiferromagnetic MnIr layer. An input-modulated in situ self-refreshing encoding mechanism is used to perform nonlinear feature extraction and classification directly from raw analog signals, forming an analog computing architecture that requires no time-frequency transformation or digital preprocessing.

Result: The bare-die device operates reliably at temperatures up to 500 K, magnetic fields of 55 T, and radiation doses of 1.5 Mrad. It achieves 99.8% accuracy in speech recognition without digital preprocessing and 100% accuracy in astronaut visual object recognition. An all-hardware integrated drone vision system demonstrates real-time in situ command execution and autonomous navigation with terahertz-level response frequency and ~0.2 fJ energy consumption per operation.

Conclusion: This work extends antiferromagnetic devices beyond memory and logic to physical computing, establishing them as a promising materials platform for energy-efficient physical computing and autonomous intelligence in extreme environments.

Abstract: Semiconductor electronic devices are increasingly constrained by fundamental quantum tunneling effects and charge-based mechanisms, which severely limit further miniaturization, write-speed scaling, and environmental robustness of silicon-based technologies. These limitations are particularly prohibitive for deep-space exploration, where extreme temperatures, ultra-strong magnetic fields, and intense radiation rapidly incapacitate conventional electronics without massive shielding. Here, we present an intrinsically resilient, strain-mediated antiferromagnetic MnIr/PMN-PT edge processor that operates reliably as a bare die under temperatures up to 500 K, magnetic fields of 55 T, and radiation doses of 1.5 Mrad. By exploiting an input-modulated in situ self-refreshing encoding mechanism, the device performs nonlinear feature extraction and classification directly from raw analog signals, enabling an analog computing architecture that requires no time-frequency transformation. This architecture achieves 99.8% accuracy in speech recognition without digital preprocessing and 100% accuracy in astronaut visual object recognition. Furthermore, an all-hardware integrated drone vision system demonstrates real-time in situ command execution and autonomous navigation, delivering a terahertz-level response frequency and an ultra-low energy consumption of approximately 0.2 fJ per operation. This work expands the functional scope of antiferromagnetic devices beyond memory and logic, establishing them as a promising materials platform for energy-efficient physical computing and autonomous intelligence in extreme environments.

</details>


### [102] [Finite-temperature phase transitions across spin symmetries in two-dimensional magnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32644)
*Chen Chen,Moritz A. Goerzen,Megha Arya,Lionel Calmels,Yongping Du,Dongzhe Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Large-scale Monte Carlo simulations of the classical 2D Heisenberg model across easy-plane, isotropic, and easy-axis regimes reveal that finite-temperature characteristic temperatures are governed by the interplay of finite-size effects, spin symmetry, and vortex-antivortex proliferation, with easy-plane anomalies deviating from BKT theory and instead linked to entropy gain from accelerated vortex nucleation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent progress on intrinsic 2D magnets has created a need for a unified understanding of finite-temperature phase transitions across different spin-symmetry classes, which is currently lacking.

Method: The authors perform large-scale Monte Carlo simulations of the classical 2D Heisenberg model, continuously spanning the easy-plane, isotropic, and easy-axis regimes. They analyze characteristic temperatures, Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) physics in the easy-plane regime, and the proliferation of vortex-antivortex pairs.

Result: Characteristic temperatures are strongly affected by the interplay of finite-size effects and spin symmetry induced by magnetocrystalline anisotropy, being most pronounced in the isotropic regime and reduced toward the easy-plane and easy-axis limits. In the easy-plane regime, temperatures estimated from magnetization and heat capacity deviate significantly from BKT theory predictions but match a previously reported XY-model anomaly. Characteristic temperatures correlate with vortex-antivortex pair proliferation, indicating entropy gain from accelerated nucleation as the anomaly's origin.

Conclusion: The thermodynamic characteristic temperature in finite easy-plane Heisenberg magnets, including the deviation from BKT theory, is subject to the interplay between finite-size spin correlations and vortex proliferation. These results clarify how experimentally and technologically relevant temperature scales evolve with magnetic anisotropy, spin symmetry, and system size in finite 2D magnets.

Abstract: Recent advances in intrinsic two-dimensional (2D) magnets have created a need for a unified understanding of finite-temperature phase transitions across different spin-symmetry classes. Here, we investigate finite-temperature magnetism in the classical 2D Heisenberg model using large-scale Monte Carlo simulations, continuously spanning the easy-plane, isotropic, and easy-axis regimes. We find that characteristic temperatures are strongly affected by the interplay of finite-size effects and spin symmetry, induced by magnetocrystalline anisotropy, being most pronounced in the isotropic Heisenberg regime and substantially reduced toward the easy-plane and easy-axis limits. Examining Berezinskii--Kosterlitz--Thouless (BKT) physics in the easy-plane regime, we find that the characteristic temperatures independently estimated from the magnetization and heat capacity deviate significantly from those proposed by BKT theory, but match a previously reported anomaly in the XY-model. We report that characteristic temperatures instead are correlated with the proliferation of vortex-antivortex pairs, suggesting the gain in entropy by their accelerated nucleation as the origin of the anomaly. Establishing this connection both theoretically and numerically, we argue that the thermodynamic characteristic temperature in finite easy-plane Heisenberg magnets, including the deviation from BKT theory, is subject to the interplay between finite-size spin correlations and vortex proliferation. These results clarify how experimental and technologically relevant temperature scales evolve with magnetic anisotropy, spin symmetry, and system size in finite 2D magnets.

</details>


### [103] [Observing Colossal and Tunable Near-field Thermal Radiation with A Highly Sensitive Annular Micro-thermocouple Junction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34355)
*Ken Araki,Wei Han Won,Liping Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A glass-fiber annular micro-thermocouple sensor enables measurements of near-field radiative heat transfer down to 10-nm gaps, showing enhancements up to 2600x over the blackbody limit and up to 430-fold tunability using a VO2 phase-transition emitter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Near-field radiative heat transfer can exceed the far-field blackbody limit via evanescent surface-wave coupling, but >100x enhancement typically requires sub-50-nm vacuum gaps. Parallel-plate configurations with millimeter-scale samples are limited by wafer bow and contaminant particles, while sphere-plate configurations using bimaterial cantilevers or micro-thermocouple tips suffer from sophisticated fabrication, low sensitivity, and weak signals, leaving the technique much less developed.

Method: An annular micro-thermocouple junction was fabricated at the end of a glass fiber via straightforward thin-film deposition, achieving a large Seebeck coefficient of 25 uV/K and thermal resistance of 8.7e6 K/W. A silica microsphere was attached beneath the junction to form a sphere-plate configuration. Near-field heat transfer was measured against quartz and doped silicon, and a VO2 thin-film emitter was used to exploit its phase transition for tunability. Data were compared with rigorous modeling based on fluctuational electrodynamics and the Derjaguin approximation, with mechanisms analyzed via energy transmission calculations.

Result: Colossal near-field radiative heat transfer over the blackbody limit was observed down to a 10-nm gap, reaching 2600 times with quartz and 900 times with doped silicon. Using the VO2 phase transition, tunable near-field heat transfer up to 430-fold enhancement was demonstrated at a 15-nm gap with 64% reduction. Experimental data agreed well with rigorous modeling.

Conclusion: The glass-fiber annular micro-thermocouple approach overcomes prior sensitivity and fabrication challenges, enabling accurate near-field measurements at extreme nanoscale gaps. These results advance both the experimental study and fundamental understanding of energy transport at nanoscale gaps, and demonstrate active tunability via a phase-transition emitter.

Abstract: Near-field radiative heat transfer between two objects has been theoretically predicted and experimentally demonstrated to exceed far-field blackbody limit across nanoscale vacuum gap distance enabled by evanescent surface waves coupling, while significant enhancement by more than 100 times usually requires sub-50-nm vacuum gaps around room temperature. This is challenging for parallel-plate configuration with millimeter sample sizes due to intrinsic wafer bow and contaminant particles. Sphere-plate configuration with a microsphere attached to bimaterial cantilevers or micro-thermocouple tips has been used to experimentally demonstrate near-field radiative heat transfer down to 30-nm gaps, but it is much less developed because of the challenges in the sophisticated sensor fabrication, low sensitivity and weak signals. In this work, we overcome these challenges by an annular micro-thermocouple junction fabricated at the end of a glass fiber with straightforward thin-film deposition to achieve high measurement accuracy with large Seebeck coefficient 25 uV/K and thermal resistance 8.7e6 K/W. With a silica microsphere attached underneath the micro-thermocouple junction, we report experimental observation of colossal near-field radiation heat transfer over blackbody limit down to 10-nm gap up to 2600 times with quartz and 900 times with doped silicon. Upon phase transition of VO2 thin film emitter, tunable near-field heat transfer up to 430-fold enhancement is experimentally demonstrated at 15-nm gap with 64% reduction. Experimental data agrees well with rigorous modeling based on fluctuational electrodynamics and Derjaguin approximation, and underlying mechanism is understood by energy transmission calculations. The results will advance the experimental study and fundamental understanding of energy transport at nanoscale gaps.

</details>


### [104] [Topological chiral edge modes in the continuum of a trivial bulk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34000)
*Li Ge*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper introduces "chiral edge modes in the continuum" (CEMICs): chiral edge states that exist inside a bulk band rather than in a band gap, induced by an imaginary magnetic flux via asymmetric edge couplings in a 2D topologically trivial system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chiral edge modes in topological matter conventionally reside in band gaps. The authors aim to show that chiral edge modes can instead exist in the continuum, i.e., inside a bulk band, and to clarify their physical origin beyond the usual non-Hermitian skin effect.

Method: An imaginary magnetic flux is introduced into an otherwise topologically trivial two-dimensional system by imposing asymmetric couplings along the edge. The resulting edge localization and its relation to the non-Hermitian skin effect are analyzed, with attention to energy exchange at the asymmetric coupling junctions and the associated parity-time (PT) transition in the Brillouin zone.

Result: CEMICs are induced by the imaginary flux, with the stronger couplings setting a preferred circulation direction. Their edge localization does not arise from the non-Hermitian skin effect but from energy exchange between the system and environment at the asymmetric coupling junctions, producing a PT transition in the Brillouin zone. The onset of CEMICs is size-independent in the macroscopic limit and is governed by the golden ratio between the asymmetric couplings and their geometric average.

Conclusion: Chiral edge modes can exist in the continuum rather than only in band gaps, and their edge localization is driven by PT-transition physics at asymmetric coupling junctions rather than the non-Hermitian skin effect, with a size-independent onset set by the golden ratio.

Abstract: Chiral edge modes in topological states of matter are routinely found inside band gaps, which may be deformed and shifted as a function of the lattice momentum. Here we show that they can exist inside a bulk band instead, i.e., in the continuum. These chiral edge modes in the continuum (CEMICs) are induced by introducing an imaginary magnetic flux to an otherwise topologically trivial two-dimensional system, realized by imposing asymmetric couplings along the edge. While the stronger couplings provide a preferred circulation direction, we find that the edge localization of CEMICs does \textit{not} originate from the non-Hermitian skin effect, which is typically associated with systems featuring asymmetric couplings and the open boundary condition. Instead, this edge localization is due to the energy exchange between the system and the environment at the asymmetric coupling junctions, which leads to a parity-time (PT) transition in the Brillouin zone. Intriguingly, the onset of CEMICs is independent of the system size in the macroscopic limit, which is given by the golden ratio between the asymmetric couplings and their geometric average.

</details>


### [105] [Capacitance sensing in bilayer graphene with gate reflectometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35125)
*Tamás Kalmár,Gergő Fülöp,Bálint Szentpéteri,Olivér Kürtössy,Kenji Watanabe,Takashi Taniguchi,Szabolcs Csonka,Péter Makk*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper introduces a gate reflectometry readout method using a lumped-element LC resonator coupled to the top gate of an hBN-encapsulated bilayer graphene/WSe2 heterostructure, enabling reliable tracking of a displacement-field-induced band gap, identification of hBN-BLG alignment, extraction of capacitance changes, and proposing a multiplexed double-resonator scheme for layer polarizability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Capacitance measurement is central to probing topological states in bilayer graphene, but existing bridge-type readouts and source-drain reflectometry setups are affected by sample disorder and percolating transport channels. A more robust, scalable readout method is needed.

Method: A gate reflectometry technique is demonstrated, probing the capacitance of a BLG flake encapsulated between hBN and WSe2 through a lumped-element LC resonator coupled to the top gate electrode. Fixed-frequency microwave readout is combined with DC transport measurements and a comprehensive capacitance network model. A multiplexed double-resonator readout scheme is also proposed.

Result: The microwave readout reliably tracks the displacement-field-induced band gap opening, is less affected by disorder-induced percolating transport channels than simultaneous DC transport measurements, identifies a small-angle hBN-BLG alignment, and allows extraction of the device capacitance change between insulating and metallic states, explained via the capacitance network model.

Conclusion: Gate reflectometry is established as a highly scalable and powerful methodology for exploring topological phases and internal charge dynamics in van der Waals heterostructures, with the proposed double-resonator scheme enabling simultaneous top/bottom gate capacitance measurement for layer polarizability extraction.

Abstract: In the search for topological states in bilayer graphene, capacitance measurement methods are widely utilized in bridge type readouts or source-drain reflectometry setups. In this paper we demonstrate an alternative readout method based on gate reflectometry. We probe the capacitance of a BLG flake encapsulated between hBN and WSe$_2$ through a lumped-element LC resonator coupled to the top gate electrode of the heterostructure. The microwave readout reliably tracks the opening of a displacement field-induced band gap in the device. Compared to simultaneous DC transport measurements, the RF signal proves to be less affected by the percolating transport channels caused by sample disorder. Furthermore, we use the gate based readout to identify a small-angle alignment between the hBN and BLG flake. From the fixed frequency microwave readout, we extract the device capacitance change between the insulating and metallic state of the bilayer and utilize a comprehensive capacitance network model to explain its origin. Finally, we propose an advanced, multiplexed double-resonator readout scheme capable of simultaneously measuring top and bottom gate capacitances to extract the layer polarizability of the system. This work establishes gate reflectometry as a highly scalable and powerful methodology for exploring topological phases and internal charge dynamics in van der Waals heterostructures.

</details>


### [106] [(Sub)nanoscale Visualization of Reconstruction-Driven Moiré Exciton Localization and Delocalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34104)
*Medha Dandu,Sriram Sankar,Giovanny Espitia,Rwik Dutta,Patrick J. Hays,Daria Blach,Takashi Taniguchi,Kenji Watanabe,James R. Chelikowsky,Seth Ariel Tongay,Peter Ercius,Jordan A. Hachtel,Mit H. Naik,Archana Raja,Sandhya Susarla*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Real-space visualization via cryogenic monochromated EELS, combined with GW-BSE calculations and optical spectroscopy, reveals that structural reconstruction extent and external strain—not the mere appearance of new moiré exciton resonances—govern exciton localization in WSe2/WS2 moiré superlattices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spectral fingerprints in optical absorption and emission are typically treated as signatures of exciton localization in twisted moiré bilayers, but the mechanism by which excitons become confined to specific stacking sites and the associated optical signature remain experimentally unresolved. This work aims to directly probe the link between structural reconstruction, real-space exciton localization, and optical response.

Method: Cryogenic monochromated electron energy loss spectroscopy (EELS) is used to visualize in real space how tuning the extent of structural reconstruction localizes and delocalizes moiré excitons in a WSe2/WS2 moiré superlattice. Results are combined with first-principles GW-Bethe Salpeter equation (GW-BSE) calculations and optical spectroscopy.

Result: The study uncovers the physical mechanism correlating twist-angle-dependent structural transformations, real-space localization of moiré excitons, and their optical signatures. Contrary to prevailing understanding, the emergence of new moiré exciton resonances in optical spectra alone is insufficient to establish exciton localization; rather, the extent of structural reconstruction and external strain drive localization.

Conclusion: Exciton localization in twisted moiré bilayers is governed by the extent of structural reconstruction and external strain rather than by the appearance of new spectral resonances. This insight provides new design principles for engineering moiré excitons and strain-aware quantum optoelectronic devices.

Abstract: Spectral fingerprints in optical absorption and emission have typically been used as a signature of exciton localization in twisted moiré bilayers. However, the mechanism by which excitons become confined to specific stacking sites and their associated optical signature is experimentally unresolved. Here, we directly visualize in real space how tuning the change in extent of structural reconstruction leads to localization and delocalization of moiré excitons in the WSe2/WS2 moiré superlattice. Using cryogenic monochromated electron energy loss spectroscopy, together with first-principles GW-Bethe Salpeter equation calculations and optical spectroscopy, we uncover the physical mechanism that drives the correlation between twist-angle-dependent structural transformations, the real-space localization of moiré excitons, and their optical signatures. Surprisingly, and in contrast to the prevailing understanding, we show that the emergence of new moiré exciton resonances in the optical spectra alone is insufficient to establish exciton localization. Instead, the extent of structural reconstruction and external strain drives exciton localization, leading to new design principles for engineering moiré excitons and strain-aware quantum optoelectronic devices.

</details>


### [107] [Probing hydrodynamics in graphene and quark matter via Seebeck coefficient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34544)
*Subhalaxmi Nayak,Jayanta Dey,Sabyasachi Ghosh*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The Seebeck coefficient is studied as a probe of collective hydrodynamic transport in graphene and quark-gluon plasma via Boltzmann transport theory, showing deviations from the Mott relation in the Dirac fluid regime that match graphene experiments and suggest a shared fluid response across the two systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify a transport observable that can distinguish collective hydrodynamic behavior from conventional Fermi-liquid transport, and to test whether strongly correlated quantum systems as different as graphene and quark-gluon plasma share a common fluid-like response.

Method: The Seebeck coefficient is computed using a kinetic-theory approach in the hydrodynamic regime, solving the Boltzmann transport equation under the relaxation-time approximation, with the formalism subsequently extended to the ultra-relativistic quark-gluon plasma.

Result: At high carrier density (Fermi-liquid domain) the result converges to the conventional Mott relation, while pronounced deviations appear in the low-density Dirac fluid regime, in agreement with experimental graphene Seebeck data; the enthalpy per particle governs the Seebeck coefficient in the Dirac fluid regime, and a similar Mott-relation deviation is found for quark-gluon plasma.

Conclusion: The observed Mott-relation deviations signal the emergence of collective hydrodynamic transport in graphene and support a similar fluid response in the Seebeck coefficient across two markedly different strongly correlated quantum systems—graphene and quark matter.

Abstract: We investigate the Seebeck coefficient as a probe of collective transport behavior in graphene and quark-gluon plasma using a kinetic-theory approach in the hydrodynamic regime. The Seebeck coefficient is obtained by solving the Boltzmann transport equation in the relaxation-time approximation. At high-charge-carrier density, corresponding to the Fermi-liquid domain, our result approaches the behavior expected from the conventional Mott relation. In contrast, significant deviations from the Mott relation are observed in the low-carrier-density regime, corresponding to the Dirac fluid domain. These facts are in good agreement with experimental Seebeck coefficient data for graphene. This behavior indicates the emergence of collective hydrodynamic transport in graphene. The enthalpy per particle plays a key role for the Seebeck coefficient in the Dirac fluid regime. We extend our formalism to the ultra-relativistic quark-gluon plasma. A similar deviation from the Mott relation is observed, supporting a similar fluid response in the Seebeck coefficient across two markedly different strongly correlated quantum systems - graphene and quark matter.

</details>


### [108] [Sub-20 nm spin textures with arbitrary topological charge stabilized by higher-order interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34706)
*Zhen Sun,Shiwei Zhu,Moritz A. Goerzen,Megha Arya,Changsheng Song,Dongzhe Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Using an atomistic spin model from first-principles, the authors predict sub-20 nm high-topological-charge (|Q| up to 5) spin textures in Janus van der Waals monolayers, showing that higher-order spin interactions are essential for their stabilization and for parity-dependent collapse pathways.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spin textures with different topological charges in the same system are attractive for applications, but while coexistence of skyrmions and antiskyrmions (|Q|=1) has been reported in inversion-symmetric magnets, extending this to high-Q (|Q|>1) textures with arbitrary charge remains elusive.

Method: An atomistic spin model parameterized from first-principles calculations is used to simulate Janus monolayers, exploring skyrmion and antiskyrmion rings and skyrmion bags with |Q| up to 5, and characterizing their nucleation mechanisms, thermal stability, and collapse pathways.

Result: Sub-20 nm high-Q textures emerge in Janus monolayers. Higher-order spin interactions (HOI) are essential for stabilizing these textures, substantially enhancing energy barriers while leaving size nearly unchanged, and stabilizing them even without Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction. Rings remain thermally stable at zero magnetic field. Skyrmion bags nucleate only with HOI, with the four-spin three-site interaction being key to preventing collapse into the ferromagnetic state. Parity-dependent collapse mechanisms are identified.

Conclusion: HOI are established as an overlooked mechanism for high-Q nucleation, enabling high-topological-charge spin textures in Janus van der Waals magnets.

Abstract: Topological magnetization textures play a central role in modern magnetism. For many applications, spin textures with different topological charges $Q$ in the same system are particularly attractive. Although the coexistence of skyrmions and antiskyrmions ($|Q| = 1$) has been reported in inversion-symmetric magnets, extending this to high-$Q$ ($|Q| > 1$) textures with arbitrary charge remains elusive. Here, using an atomistic spin model parameterized from first-principles calculations, we predict the emergence of sub-20 nm high-$Q$ textures in Janus monolayers, van der Waals magnets of growing interest. We explore skyrmion and antiskyrmion rings as well as skyrmion bags with $|Q|$ up to 5, and characterize their nucleation mechanisms, thermal stability, and collapse pathways. We find that higher-order spin interactions (HOI), which extend the conventional bilinear-exchange Hamiltonian, are essential for stabilizing these high-$Q$ spin textures. The rings remain thermally stable at zero magnetic field. HOI substantially enhance their energy barriers while leaving their size nearly unchanged and stabilize them even in the absence of Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction. Skyrmion bags, in contrast, nucleate only in the presence of HOI. In particular, the four-spin three-site interaction is the key ingredient preventing high-$Q$ textures from collapsing into the ferromagnetic state. Finally, we identify previously unreported parity-dependent collapse mechanisms for high-$Q$ textures. Our results establish HOI as an overlooked mechanism for high-$Q$ nucleation.

</details>


### [109] [Moiré Quantum Layer Hall Effect](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34941)
*Jin-Xin Hu,Wen-Bo Dai,X. C. Xie*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work proposes that moiré engineering via electrostatic superlattice potentials from control layers can realize the quantum layer Hall effect (QLHE) in even-layer topological antiferromagnetic thin films such as MnBi2Te4, without breaking PT symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The layer Hall effect (LHE) in even-layer topological antiferromagnets like MnBi2Te4 arises from layer-locked Berry curvature and can be tuned by a vertical displacement field, but achieving its quantized version (QLHE) remains experimentally challenging.

Method: The authors propose placing moiré control layers in close proximity to the surfaces of a MnBi2Te4 thin film without breaking PT symmetry. They analyze the resulting band structure and Hall conductances, and identify the microscopic mechanism as an emergent layer-U(1) gauge field generated by the scalar moiré potential, producing opposite periodic pseudomagnetic fields on the top and bottom layers.

Result: The system exhibits a vanishing net Hall conductance but a quantized layer Hall conductance of e^2/h. A weak gate electric field breaks PT symmetry and drives the system into a layer-polarized quantum anomalous Hall (QAH) phase. When a single control layer is introduced on only one surface, the layer-polarized QAH emerges even without an external electric field.

Conclusion: Moiré engineering provides a feasible pathway toward realizing the QLHE in antiferromagnetic thin films, with the mechanism rooted in a moiré-induced layer-U(1) gauge field creating opposite pseudomagnetic fields on opposite layers.

Abstract: Even-layer topological antiferromagnetic thin films such as MnBi$_2$Te$_4$ exhibit an unconventional layer Hall effect (LHE) arising from a layer-locked Berry curvature. This effect manifests as a layer-polarized anomalous Hall effect that can be controlled by a vertical displacement field. However, achieving a quantized version of the LHE remains experimentally challenging. In this work, we propose that moiré engineering, using an electrostatic superlattice potential from control layers, can realize the quantum layer Hall effect (QLHE). Specifically, by placing moiré control layers in close proximity to the surfaces of a MnBi$_2$Te$_4$ thin film without breaking $PT$ symmetry, the resulting system exhibits a vanishing net Hall conductance but a quantized layer Hall conductance of $e^2/h$. A weak gate electric field then breaks $PT$ symmetry and drives the system into a layer-polarized quantum anomalous Hall (QAH) phase. Furthermore, when a single control layer is introduced on only one surface, the layer-polarized QAH emerges even in the absence of an external electric field. We identify the microscopic origin of the QLHE as an emergent layer-$U(1)$ gauge field generated by the scalar moiré potential, which in turn produces opposite periodic pseudomagnetic fields on the top and bottom layers. Our work provides a feasible pathway toward realizing the QLHE in antiferromagnetic thin films.

</details>


### [110] [Plasmon modes in three-dimensional nodal-ring semimetals: tilt and gap effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35373)
*Ipsita Mandal*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper computes the density-density response, Drude weight, and RPA plasmon dispersion in 3D nodal-ring semimetals (gapless PT-symmetric PTNR and gapped GNR), for untilted, in-plane-tilted, and axially-tilted configurations, with closed-form results in the partially-gapped regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nodal-ring semimetals host exotic low-energy excitations, and their plasmonic response is not yet systematically understood, especially when the ring is tilted or gapped and part of the Fermi surface becomes partially gapped. A comparative study of gapless vs. gapped cases and of different tilt directions is needed.

Method: Low-doping regime with linearised Hamiltonian; computation of the non-interacting density-density response function, Drude weight, and plasmon dispersion within the random-phase approximation, for the untilted configuration, in-plane tilt along k_x, and axial tilt along k_z; closed-form results derived in the small-tilt and small-gap limits for the partially-gapped regimes.

Result: For in-plane-tilted GNR, a sufficiently strong tilt or small chemical potential drives part of the ring into a partially-gapped regime with only a partial Fermi surface, yielding closed-form results in the small-tilt and small-gap limits; the small-gap limit gives a fractional-power correction to the Drude weight and plasmon frequency. For axially-tilted GNR, the partially-gapped window exhibits a double-valued local Fermi surface, for which the density of states and Drude weights are obtained in closed form.

Conclusion: The tilt and mass gap strongly modify the plasmon dispersion and the anisotropy between axial and in-plane directions, providing a systematic comparison between the gapless and gapped cases and between different tilt directions.

Abstract: We study plasmon modes in three-dimensional nodal-ring semimetals, considering both the gapless $\mathcal{PT}$-symmetric nodal ring (PTNR) and its gapped counterpart (GNR). Working in the low-doping regime where the linearised Hamiltonian is valid, we compute the non-interacting density-density response function, the Drude weight, and the plasmon dispersion within the random-phase approximation. We treat the untilted configuration as well as in-plane tilt along $k_x$ and axial tilt along $k_z$. For the in-plane tilted GNR, a strong-enough tilt or a small enough chemical potential pushes part of the ring into a partially-gapped regime. Only part of the Fermi surface survives there. We obtain closed-form results for this regime in two limits: a small tilt and a small gap. The small-gap limit produces a fractional-power correction to the Drude weight and the plasmon frequency. For the axially-tilted GNR, the partially-gapped window has a double-valued local Fermi surface for which we obtain the density-of-states and the Drude weights in closed forms. We analyse how the tilt and the mass-gap affect the plasmon dispersion and the anisotropy between axial and in-plane directions. Our results provide a systematic comparison between the gapless and gapped cases and between different tilt directions.

</details>


### [111] [Extracting the anyonic exchange phase from co-tunneling through an open quantum dot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35480)
*Felix Puster,Matthias Thamm,Bernd Rosenow*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A three-QPC chiral edge interferometer is proposed where one segment acts as an open quantum dot. A third-order Keldysh calculation shows the interference current carries a co-tunneling contribution that reveals the anyonic exchange phase θ=πν for Laughlin states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Elementary excitations of fractional quantum Hall states obey anyonic statistics characterized by an exchange phase θ between bosonic and fermionic values. Measuring this phase requires an interferometer, but existing antidot interferometers are hard to tune; a simpler setting that makes the relevant co-tunneling mechanism accessible is desirable.

Method: An interferometer with three chiral edge segments pairwise connected by three quantum point contacts (QPCs), where one segment acts as an open quantum dot with a continuous spectrum, enabling resonant tunneling without gate tuning. A non-equilibrium Keldysh calculation to third order in the tunneling amplitudes yields the interference current, including finite-temperature and finite-QPC-separation effects.

Result: The interference current contains a co-tunneling contribution involving the exchange of two quasiparticles, so for Laughlin states the exchange phase θ=πν appears as the phase shift between Aharonov-Bohm oscillations measured in two voltage configurations. A closed analytic expression is obtained for the interference current. If voltage-induced area changes are sufficiently screened, θ is extracted from a robust phase-plateau difference; alternatively, simultaneous measurements at two drains remove the screening assumption, with time-of-flight phases quantified analytically and θ extracted by controlled extrapolation at a few-percent systematic error.

Conclusion: The proposed three-QPC open-quantum-dot interferometer provides a simple platform for probing anyonic exchange statistics, allowing extraction of the Laughlin exchange phase θ=πν via either a phase-plateau difference (with sufficient screening) or dual-drain measurements with analytical time-of-flight corrections.

Abstract: The elementary excitations of fractional quantum Hall states obey anyonic statistics, described by an exchange phase $θ$ intermediate between the bosonic and fermionic values. We study an interferometer in which three chiral edge segments are pairwise connected by three quantum point contacts, so that, depending on the applied voltages, one of the segments acts as an open quantum dot: its spectrum is continuous, and tunneling through it is resonant without any gate tuning. This setup realizes the co-tunneling mechanism of a recently proposed antidot interferometer in a particularly simple setting. Using a non-equilibrium Keldysh calculation to third order in the tunneling amplitudes, we show that the interference current contains a co-tunneling contribution in which two quasiparticles are exchanged, so that for Laughlin states the exchange phase $θ=πν$ appears as the phase shift between the Aharonov-Bohm oscillations measured in two voltage configurations. We obtain a closed analytic expression for the interference current at finite temperature and finite separation between the quantum point contacts and show that, provided voltage-induced changes of the enclosed area are sufficiently screened, the exchange phase can be extracted from a robust phase-plateau difference. Alternatively, simultaneous measurements of the interference currents at two drains avoid this screening assumption and time-of-flight phases can be quantified analytically, allowing the exchange phase to be extracted by a controlled extrapolation with a few-percent systematic error.

</details>


### [112] [Dynamical transition in non-Hermitian Chern insulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35492)
*Zhi-Qiang Zhang,Yuan-Hang Ren,Ming Lu,Hua Jiang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work reveals a dynamical transition for wave-packet propagation in non-Hermitian Chern insulators: edge-launched packets need not stay at the boundary but can penetrate into the bulk, governed by the competition between conventional topological localization and the non-Hermitian skin effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In Hermitian Chern insulators, a wave packet launched at the edge propagates along the boundary, its evolution dictated by the topological Chern number. The aim is to understand how this picture changes in non-Hermitian Chern insulators, where the non-Hermitian skin effect can compete with topological edge localization.

Method: The authors analyze wave-packet dynamics in non-Hermitian Chern insulators, examining the competition between localization induced by conventional topology and the non-Hermitian skin effect, and relate the observed dynamics to the generalized-Brillouin-zone framework.

Result: A peculiar dynamical transition is unveiled: when topological localization dominates, the wave packet evolves along the boundary; otherwise, it spreads into the bulk, penetrating the interior rather than staying at the edge.

Conclusion: The propagation of topological edge wave packets in non-Hermitian Chern insulators is not solely determined by the topological Chern number. While the generalized-Brillouin-zone framework reliably predicts topological phase transitions, it does not by itself specify the dynamical transitions.

Abstract: We unveil a peculiar dynamical transition for the propagation of wave packets in non-Hermitian Chern insulators, where the evolution of the topological wave packets at the edge is not solely determined by the topological Chern number. Unlike the Hermitian Chern insulator, where a wave packet initiated at the edge propagates along the system boundary, here it may instead penetrate into the bulk. This behavior is attributed to the competition between the localization induced by conventional topology and the non-Hermitian skin effect. Specifically, when the former dominant, the wave packet will evolve along the boundary; otherwise, it will spread into the bulk. These features demonstrate that while the generalized-Brillouin zone framework reliably predicts topological phase transitions, it does not on its own specify the dynamical transitions.

</details>


### [113] [Superfluid Spin Transport in the Van der Waals Antiferromagnet CrCl$_3$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35513)
*Peisen Yuan,Xiaomin Guo,Vincent Flynn,Benedetta Flebus,Fèlix Casanova,Luis E. Hueso*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Superfluid spin transport is discovered and controlled in the easy-plane van der Waals antiferromagnetic insulator CrCl₃ using a nonlocal device, showing ultra-long-range (~90 μm) weakly decaying transport that is suppressed by strong magnetic fields and elevated temperatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spin superfluidity promises phase-gradient-driven, ultra-low-loss spin transport and has become a central goal of spintronics, yet experimental progress is limited by the lack of suitable material systems.

Method: Nonlocal magnon transport measurements combined with theoretical modelling on the easy-plane van der Waals antiferromagnetic insulator CrCl₃, probed under canted AFM spin configurations and varying magnetic field and temperature.

Result: Spin superfluidity emerges in CrCl₃ under canted AFM spin configurations, yielding ultra-long-range (~90 μm) weakly decaying spin transport. Strong magnetic fields and elevated temperatures suppress the superfluid state, restoring rapid exponential decay with distance characteristic of incoherent magnons.

Conclusion: These findings highlight the potential of spin superfluidity in two-dimensional magnetic insulators and establish CrCl₃ as a promising platform for energy-efficient, long-distance spin transport in next-generation spintronic applications.

Abstract: Over the past decade, the quest for spin superfluidity has moved to the forefront of spintronics, driven by the promise of phase-gradient-driven, ultra-low-loss spin transport. However, experimental investigations remain limited, primarily due to the lack of suitable material systems. Here, we report on the discovery and control of a superfluid spin transport in the easy-plane van der Waals antiferromagnetic (AFM) insulator CrCl$_3$ by a nonlocal device structure. Combining nonlocal magnon transport measurements with theoretical modelling, we demonstrate that spin superfluidity emerges in CrCl$_3$ under canted AFM spin configurations, where it gives rise to ultra-long range (around 90 $μ$m), weakly decaying spin transport. We also provide direct evidence that strong magnetic fields and elevated temperatures suppress the superfluid state, restoring the rapid exponential decay with distance of incoherent magnons. These findings underscore the potential of spin superfluidity in two-dimensional magnetic insulators and establish CrCl$_3$ as a promising platform for energy-efficient, long-distance spin transport in next-generation spintronic applications.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.stat-mech'></div>

# cond-mat.stat-mech [[Back]](#toc)

### [114] [Odd stochastic density functional theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32080)
*Yael Avni,Michel Fruchart,David Martin,Tali Khain,Vincenzo Vitelli*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The authors extend stochastic density functional theory (SDFT) to "odd SDFT," a framework for systems with a chiral overdamped limit, derived by taking the overdamped limit of a fluctuating hydrodynamic description of underdamped particles under a Lorentz force. They validate it via the collective mobility tensor and apply it to electrolyte Hall conductivity, deriving the full conductivity tensor beyond mean-field theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard SDFT, as a density-based formulation of coupled Langevin equations with white noise, cannot capture systems ranging from magnetized electrolytes to chiral active matter that possess a chiral overdamped limit, where a relaxation timescale and an oscillation period vanish simultaneously. A formulation covering this regime is needed.

Method: The authors derive "odd SDFT" by taking the overdamped limit of a fluctuating hydrodynamic description of underdamped particles subject to a Lorentz force. They validate the framework by computing the collective mobility tensor of interacting particles using both direct linear response and a Green-Kubo relation. They then apply the theory to the Hall conductivity of electrolytes in magnetic fields, using two distinct Green-Kubo relations to go beyond mean-field theory.

Result: The collective mobility tensor of interacting particles is obtained via both direct linear response and a Green-Kubo relation, confirming the framework's validity. For electrolytes in magnetic fields, the full conductivity tensor is derived, extending the relaxation part of the Debye-Hückel-Onsager theory to the case of finite magnetic fields.

Conclusion: Odd SDFT provides a valid and useful framework for the dynamics of chiral-overdamped systems, including magnetized electrolytes and chiral active matter. It enables derivation of the full conductivity tensor and generalizes the Debye-Hückel-Onsager relaxation theory to finite magnetic fields.

Abstract: Stochastic density functional theory (SDFT) provides a powerful framework for studying the dynamics of many-body systems both in and out of equilibrium. In its standard form, it can be viewed as a density-based formulation of coupled Langevin equations with white noise. Here, we extend SDFT to a class of systems, ranging from magnetized electrolytes to chiral active matter, that possess a chiral overdamped limit in which a relaxation timescale and an oscillation period are simultaneously taken to zero. We derive this formulation, which we dub ``odd SDFT", by taking the overdamped limit of a fluctuating hydrodynamic description of underdamped particles subject to a Lorentz force. We establish the validity and utility of the resulting framework by calculating the collective mobility tensor of interacting particles using both direct linear response and a Green-Kubo relation. We then apply the theory to the Hall conductivity of electrolytes in magnetic fields. Going beyond mean-field theory and using two distinct Green-Kubo relations, we derive the full conductivity tensor, extending the relaxation part of the Debye-Hückel-Onsager theory to the case of finite magnetic fields.

</details>


### [115] [Continuously varying exponents in the distribution of waiting times in the symmetric exclusion process on a percolation cluster](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32314)
*Arpan Chatterjee,Kabir Ramola,Deepak Dhar*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Waiting-time distributions of hard-core particles in the symmetric exclusion process on lattices with side branches (a model for trapping on a percolation cluster) show strong dynamical heterogeneity at high density, with logarithmically spaced peaks and algebraic decay t^{-ω} of the return probability, where ω varies continuously above a threshold density ρ*.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand trapping and dynamical heterogeneity of interacting particles in geometries with dead-end branches, using a two-dimensional carpet as a simplified model for trapping on a supercritical percolation cluster.

Method: Steady-state numerical simulations combined with an approximate analytical treatment of particles trapped in side branches, applied to the symmetric exclusion process on one- and two-dimensional lattices with side branches at each site.

Result: At high densities the system exhibits strong dynamical heterogeneity, with the distribution of logarithms of waiting times developing well-separated peaks corresponding to trapping at different depths from the backbone. The probability that a tagged particle remains at the same position decays algebraically as t^{-ω}, with ω varying continuously with particle density above a threshold ρ*<1.

Conclusion: Trapping in side branches induces heterogeneous, algebraically decaying dynamics governed by a density-dependent exponent, and correlations between successive waiting times along a tagged particle's trajectory are further investigated.

Abstract: We study the waiting-time distribution of hard-core interacting particles in the symmetric exclusion process on one- and two-dimensional lattices with side branches attached to each lattice site in the steady state. We use numerical simulations together with an approximate analytical treatment of particles trapped in side branches in the steady state. Such a two-dimensional carpet serves as a simplified model for trapping of a supercritical percolation cluster. At high particle densities, the system exhibits strong dynamical heterogeneity, with the distribution of logarithms of waiting times developing well-separated peaks corresponding to particles trapped at different depths from the backbone. We show that the probability that a tagged particle occupies the same position at time $t_0+t$ as at time $t_0$ decays algebraically as $t^{-ω}$, where the exponent $ω$ varies continuously with particle density for densities above a threshold value $ρ^*<1$. We also investigate the correlations between successive waiting times along the trajectory of a tagged particle.

</details>


### [116] [Backward Kolmogorov Transport: Sampling Invariant Laws of Reversible Diffusions from Trajectory Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32664)
*Yuanchao Xu,Isao Ishikawa*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper introduces Backward Kolmogorov Transport (BKT), a sampler for the invariant law of a reversible diffusion with unknown drift, which transports particles using generator eigenpairs estimated from trajectories instead of estimating density, score, or drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trajectory-based samplers that rely on density or score estimation inherit estimation errors, particularly at barriers between metastable states. The trajectories also encode the dynamics themselves, which can be exploited directly rather than discarded after density/score fitting.

Method: For a reversible diffusion, the ratio between the process law and its invariant law solves the backward Kolmogorov equation, so generator eigenpairs propagate it in closed form. BKT evaluates this ratio using eigenpairs estimated from trajectories and coefficients computed once from initial particles, then moves particles along the Wasserstein gradient flow of the Kullback-Leibler divergence. Particles do not interact; BKT defines a transport map, and no density, score, or drift is estimated.

Result: With exact eigenpairs, BKT transports the spectral projection of the initial law exactly when that projection is positive. For estimated eigenpairs, the authors prove a transport identity where truncation enters only through the initial law and estimation only through the eigenpair residual, plus a local stability bound in 2-Wasserstein distance. With coefficients computed from transported particles, the sampling error of retained modes cancels, and a central limit theorem identifies the asymptotic variance. Experiments on Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes, multi-well potentials, separable products, and alanine dipeptide, including comparisons with density-estimate particle methods, agree with the theory.

Conclusion: BKT provides a principled transport-based sampling framework that avoids density, score, and drift estimation and can match or outperform independent samples when the spectrum is accurate.

Abstract: We consider a reversible diffusion with unknown drift, observed through trajectories, and construct samplers of its invariant law. The trajectories contain samples of this law, but a sampler built from them by density or score estimation inherits the errors of these estimates, in particular on the barriers between metastable states. The trajectories also contain the dynamics, and for a reversible diffusion the ratio between the law of the process and its invariant law solves the backward Kolmogorov equation, so that the eigenpairs of the generator propagate it in closed form. Backward Kolmogorov transport (BKT) evaluates this ratio with eigenpairs estimated from the trajectories and coefficients computed once from the initial particles, and moves the particles along the Wasserstein gradient flow of the Kullback-Leibler divergence. The particles do not interact, BKT defines a transport map, and no density, score or drift is estimated. With exact eigenpairs BKT transports the spectral projection of the initial law exactly when this projection is positive. For estimated eigenpairs we prove a transport identity in which truncation enters only through the initial law and estimation only through the residual of the eigenpairs, and a local stability bound in the 2-Wasserstein distance. With coefficients computed from the transported particles, the sampling error of the retained modes cancels, and a central limit theorem identifies the asymptotic variance. Experiments on Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes, multi-well potentials, separable products and alanine dipeptide, including comparisons with particle methods based on density estimates, agree with these results, and with an accurate spectrum the errors of BKT reach or fall below those of independent samples.

</details>


### [117] [Criticality without Temperature in an Ising Spin System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33361)
*Christos Kakalis,Fotios K. Diakonos*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: An Ising system with Hamiltonian-independent, temperature-free adaptive cluster dynamics exhibits spontaneous symmetry breaking, power-law cluster size distributions, and singular energy response, revealing a route to nonequilibrium criticality without energy-based dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Criticality in the Ising model is conventionally generated by Hamiltonian dynamics and controlled by temperature. The authors aim to show that critical-like behavior can emerge without any temperature-like parameter and with dynamics completely independent of the Ising Hamiltonian.

Method: The authors introduce an adaptive cluster dynamics in which spin connectivity is controlled by a local quiet time variable—the time elapsed since a spin was last updated. The Ising Hamiltonian enters only as an observable characterizing the resulting configurations, not as a driver of dynamics. They vary a parameter α that controls the connectivity strength.

Result: As α is varied, the system undergoes spontaneous symmetry breaking accompanied by strong collective fluctuations. The cluster size distribution develops an extended power-law regime, terminated by a small finite-size hump at the largest scales, while the Ising energy exhibits a singular response to variations in α.

Conclusion: Memory-dependent connectivity alone can generate collective critical behavior, revealing a route to nonequilibrium criticality without energy-based dynamics.

Abstract: Criticality in the Ising model is conventionally generated by Hamiltonian dynamics and controlled by temperature. Here we show that critical like behavior can emerge in an Ising system whose dynamics is completely independent of the Ising Hamiltonian and contains no temperature like parameter. We introduce an adaptive cluster dynamics in which spin connectivity is controlled by a local quiet time variable, the time elapsed since a spin was last updated. The Ising Hamiltonian enters only as an observable characterizing the resulting configurations. As the parameter $α$, controlling the connectivity strength, is varied, the system undergoes spontaneous symmetry breaking accompanied by strong collective fluctuations. The cluster size distribution develops an extended power law regime, terminated by a small finite size hump at the largest scales, while the Ising energy exhibits a singular response to the variation of $α$. These results show that memory dependent connectivity alone can generate collective critical behavior, revealing a route to nonequilibrium criticality without energy based dynamics.

</details>


### [118] [Mpemba effect without a wall](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32871)
*Siddharth S. Sane,John Bechhoefer*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech
