TL;DR: The authors introduce a wall-free potential to study the Mpemba effect in a colloidal system, showing the effect only appears when the bath-temperature quench is faster than a critical quench rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior experimental demonstrations of the Mpemba effect in colloidal systems used an instantaneous temperature quench, with initial particle positions drawn from a high-temperature Boltzmann distribution confined by two walls. It was speculated that these walls might be essential to the Mpemba effect, so the authors aim to clarify whether walls are required and to investigate finite-quench-rate effects.

Method: They design a new potential lacking walls and use it both for the initial high-temperature state and for subsequent evolution, then perform simulations to investigate the Mpemba effect at finite quench rates.

Result: The Mpemba effect appears only for bath-temperature quenches faster than a critical quench rate.

Conclusion: The results clarify intuitions about the existence of the Mpemba effect and demonstrate that walls are not required for it to occur.

Abstract: Experimental demonstrations of the Mpemba effect in a colloidal-particle system have imposed an instantaneous temperature quench via an initial condition where individual particle trajectories are drawn from a high-temperature Boltzmann distribution and then evolve in a bath at lower temperature. The potential used for the high-temperature distribution has had two walls that impose a finite range of initial positions, even at effectively infinite temperatures. The potential for evolution in the bath had no walls and matched that used for the initial condition over its support. For low bath temperatures, the difference between the dynamics with and without walls is negligible. Nonetheless, it has been speculated that the walls are perhaps more important than they seem and might even be required for the Mpemba effect to occur. Here, we introduce a new potential that lacks walls and use it for both the initial high temperature state and for subsequent evolution. This new potential then allows us to investigate by simulation Mpemba effects at finite-quench rates. Our results help clarify intuitions concerning the existence of a Mpemba effect and show that the Mpemba effect appears only for bath-temperature quenches faster than a critical quench rate.

</details>


### [119] [Periodically modulated traveling waves in integrate-and-fire networks: recursive speed law and propagation failure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33006)
*Jie Nissel,Ricardo Erazo-Toscano,Rosahn Bhattarai,Marius Osan,Mandeep Chauhan,Remus Osan*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper studies how traveling waves of neural activity are halted by spatial inhomogeneity in synaptic coupling, using an integrate-and-fire network with exponentially decaying coupling. It reduces the leading-edge firing map to a scalar equation for wave speed, analyzes fold termination of positive periodic waves for slow and fast modulation, and validates results against direct simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traveling waves of activity in neural tissue can be halted by spatial inhomogeneity in synaptic coupling, yet the mechanisms governing wave termination under periodic modulation of coupling are not fully understood. The paper aims to characterize when and how positive periodic waves cease to exist and to distinguish loss of the periodic wave from failure of a particular launch.

Method: An integrate-and-fire network is analyzed in which each neuron fires once and coupling decays exponentially. The leading-edge firing map is reduced exactly to a scalar equation for the wave speed in space, with a slow unstable and fast stable homogeneous speed, c1 and c2, and a pointwise periodic modulation of coupling. Fold behavior is studied for slowly and rapidly varying modulation, including a perturbative recursion in amplitude to obtain the speed profile explicitly.

Result: Positive periodic waves terminate in folds. For slowly varying modulation, the fold amplitude approaches a plateau set by the trough of the modulation, where the local bottleneck speed is sqrt(c1*c2), with the approach governed by the local geometry of the trough. For rapidly varying modulation, the fold amplitude grows linearly with frequency, with slope given by a full-amplitude average over the integrated modulation profile; a weak-ripple truncation overestimates this slope about 2.5-fold at the default coupling.

Conclusion: The scalar reduction and fold analysis clarify how spatial inhomogeneity halts traveling waves, distinguishing loss of the periodic wave from failure of a launch and separating the excitatory regime from its sign-changing extension. Results are confirmed by direct integration of the reduced equation and first-spike firing-map simulations of the network.

Abstract: Traveling waves of activity in neural tissue can be halted by spatial inhomogeneity in synaptic coupling. We study an integrate-and-fire network in which each neuron fires once and the coupling decays exponentially. For this model the leading-edge firing map reduces exactly to a scalar equation for the wave speed in space, with a slow unstable and a fast stable homogeneous speed, $c_1$ and $c_2$, and a periodic modulation of the coupling enters this equation pointwise. Positive periodic waves terminate in folds. For slowly varying modulation the fold amplitude approaches a plateau set by the trough of the modulation, where the local bottleneck speed is $\sqrt{c_1 c_2}$; the approach to the plateau is set by the local geometry of the trough. For rapidly varying modulation the fold amplitude grows linearly with frequency, with a slope given by a full-amplitude average over the integrated modulation profile; a weak-ripple truncation of that average overestimates the slope about 2.5-fold at the default coupling. A perturbative recursion in the amplitude gives the speed profile explicitly. We distinguish loss of the periodic wave from failure of a particular launch, and the excitatory regime $ε\le 1$ from its sign-changing extension. The results are checked against direct integration of the reduced equation and first-spike firing-map simulations of the network.

</details>


### [120] [Boolean Cumulants and Exact Reduced Descriptions of Renewal-Driven Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33621)
*Marco Bianucci,Riccardo Mannella*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: For memoryless (Kubo-Anderson) step noise, the reduced dynamics admit two exact complementary descriptions—a Boolean-cumulant kernel representation and a frozen-noise stationary-density representation—showing that reduced finite-correlation dynamics are controlled not just by variance and correlation time but by the underlying Boolean combinatorial structure of the renewal process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduced descriptions of unresolved fluctuations usually rely on Gaussian processes, even though many realistic forcings have finite correlation times and a renewal structure. Gaussian-based reductions therefore miss the combinatorial structure of such noise, creating a need for an exact reduced theory for finite-correlation (memoryless step) noise.

Method: The authors analyze memoryless step (Kubo-Anderson) noise and derive two exact, complementary reduced descriptions: (1) a kernel representation governed by Boolean cumulants of the jump distribution, and (2) a frozen-noise representation for stationary probability densities. For exponentially distributed waiting times, they show the totally time-ordered G-cumulants equal the Boolean cumulants of the jump law, enabling exact resummation of the memory kernel via the Boolean generator η on a resolvent operator. They examine closure errors and apply the frozen-noise representation to the linear system with linear multiplicative interaction (LIMI/CAM) model.

Result: Second-order closure is exact if and only if the jump distribution is symmetric Bernoulli; for general jump laws the leading closure error is controlled by the universal combination (b4/b2)λ^2. The frozen-noise representation reduces the LIMI/CAM model to a random affine recursion, yielding exact stationary densities and Kesten tail indices. The Boolean hierarchy acts on the reduced kernel rather than on the stationary measure (unlike classical cumulant expansions). Rates, moments, and closure errors are governed by the Boolean hierarchy, whereas stationary-density properties are set by the geometry of the jump distribution through the frozen dynamics.

Conclusion: Finite-correlation reductions are controlled not only by the variance and correlation time of the forcing, but also by the underlying combinatorial structure of the renewal process; for memoryless renewal noise that structure is Boolean. The two exact descriptions together separate dynamical quantities (rates, moments, closure errors) from stationary-density properties.

Abstract: Reduced descriptions of unresolved fluctuations are commonly based on Gaussian processes, despite the fact that many realistic forcings possess finite correlation times and a renewal structure. For memoryless step (Kubo-Anderson) noise, we show that the reduced dynamics admit two exact and complementary descriptions: a kernel representation governed by the Boolean cumulants of the jump distribution, and a frozen-noise representation for stationary probability densities. For exponentially distributed waiting times, the totally time-ordered $G$-cumulants coincide exactly with the Boolean cumulants of the jump law, leading to an exact resummation of the memory kernel. The resulting kernel is expressed through the Boolean generator $η$ evaluated on a resolvent operator. Within this hierarchy, second-order closure is exact if and only if the jump distribution is symmetric Bernoulli. For general jump laws, the leading closure error is controlled by the universal combination $(b4/b2)λ^2$. Unlike classical cumulant expansions, however, the Boolean hierarchy acts on the reduced kernel rather than directly on the stationary measure. A complementary exact description is provided by the frozen-noise representation. For the linear system with linear multiplicative interaction (LIMI/CAM) model, it reduces the dynamics to a random affine recursion and yields exact expressions for stationary densities and Kesten tail indices. The analysis reveals that rates, moments, and closure errors are governed by the Boolean hierarchy, whereas stationary-density properties are determined by the geometry of the jump distribution through the frozen dynamics. Finite-correlation reductions are therefore controlled not only by the variance and correlation time of the forcing, but also by the underlying combinatorial structure of the renewal process. For memoryless renewal noise, that structure is Boolean.

</details>


### [121] [Quantum Dynamics of Full Counting Statistics in Fermionic Lattices with Localized Gain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33724)
*Bijay Kumar Agarwalla,Manas Kulkarni*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper develops an analytical framework for the full counting statistics of fermion number growth in a driven open 1D lattice with localized particle injection, using the GKSL master equation and Schwinger-Keldysh path integral, obtaining a Levitov-Lesovik-type formula and showing linear-in-time cumulant growth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding quantum fluctuations and full counting statistics in driven open fermionic systems with localized particle injection remains a challenge, particularly for arbitrary times and beyond steady-state descriptions. Existing Levitov-Lesovik formulas were derived for boundary-driven setups only in the steady state, leaving the transient and general-time behavior unexplored.

Method: The authors model the dynamics via the GKSL quantum master equation for a 1D non-interacting lattice with localized particle gain at one edge. They recast the counting problem from counting particles at every lattice site to one involving only the local injected site, and employ the Schwinger-Keldysh path integral formalism within the GKSL framework to derive an analytical expression for the cumulant generating function at arbitrary times.

Result: The authors derive an analytical cumulant generating function valid at arbitrary times and obtain a Levitov-Lesovik-type formula in the long-time limit. For clean lattices with short- or long-range hopping supporting delocalized single-particle eigenstates, all cumulants grow linearly with time asymptotically. Analytical results agree excellently with direct numerical simulations.

Conclusion: The work establishes a general framework for characterizing full counting statistics and quantum fluctuations in driven open fermionic systems with localized particle injection, extending Levitov-Lesovik-type results to arbitrary times and providing a basis for studying fluctuation dynamics in such setups.

Abstract: We investigate the dynamics of full counting statistics (FCS) of the growth of the total number of fermions in a one-dimensional non-interacting lattice subjected to a localized particle gain (source) at one edge. The dynamics of the setup is modeled by the Gorini--Kossakowski--Sudarshan--Lindblad (GKSL) quantum master equation. We recast the counting problem that involves counting particle number at every lattice site to a problem where only the local injected site is involved. We employ the Schwinger-Keldysh path integral formalism within the GKSL framework and derive an analytical expression for the cumulant generating function at arbitrary times and obtain a Levitov-Lesovik type formula in the long-time limit, earlier derived for boundary driven setups in the steady-state. For clean lattices with either short- or long-range hopping, supporting single-particle delocalized eigenstates, we show that all cumulants grow linearly with time in the asymptotic regime. Our analytical results are in excellent agreement with direct numerical simulations. These findings establish a general framework for characterizing FCS and quantum fluctuations in driven open fermionic systems with localized particle injection.

</details>


### [122] [Non-stationary Statistics and Energetics of Brownian Motion under Stochastic Harmonic Confinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33751)
*Deepak Gupta,Sabine H. L. Klapp*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This work studies a Brownian particle in a harmonic trap with a stiffness fluctuating as the square of a Brownian process, deriving positional statistics and thermodynamic quantities for this non-stationary process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stiffness fluctuations in harmonic traps are physically relevant, but previously studied models typically involve stationary processes. This paper addresses a fluctuating-stiffness model that is inherently non-stationary, filling a gap in the understanding of such systems' positional statistics and thermodynamics.

Method: The stiffness is modeled as the square of a Brownian process. The probability density function of the particle position at time t is derived first for the no-thermal-bath case, followed by a general expression for the nth positional moment. The first four moments and their long-time asymptotics are evaluated. Exact expressions for average work and average heat exchanged with the bath are also obtained.

Result: Analytical results for the positional PDF, the nth positional moment, and the first four positional moments show non-stationary long-time behavior. The average work and average heat also exhibit long-time behavior reflecting the non-stationary nature of the process. Theoretical predictions are validated by numerical simulations.

Conclusion: The model provides a tractable, exactly solvable description of a trapped Brownian particle with non-stationary, Brownian-squared stiffness fluctuations. Both positional statistics and thermodynamic quantities confirm the non-stationary character, contrasting with previously studied stationary fluctuating-stiffness models, and are supported by simulations.

Abstract: We investigate the positional statistics and thermodynamic properties of a Brownian particle confined in a Harmonic trap. The stiffness of the particle fluctuates in time as the square of the Brownian process. First, in the absence of a thermal bath, we investigate the probability density function of the position of the particle at time $t$. Then, we provide an expression to compute the $n$th positional moment. We evaluate the first four positional moments and discuss their asymptotic behavior in the long-time limit. In contrast to previously studied models of fluctuating stiffness, the current model describes a non-stationary process. Furthermore, we provide the exact expression for the average work performed on the system and the average heat exchanged by the particle with the bath. Their long time behavior again reflects the non-stationary nature of the process. Our theoretical predictions are supported by numerical simulations.

</details>


### [123] [Hall conductance of dilute electrolytes from odd stochastic density functional theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33797)
*Yael Avni,Michel Fruchart,David Martin,Tali Khain,Vincenzo Vitelli*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper develops "odd SDFT," a stochastic density functional framework for electrolytes in a magnetic field, and computes Hall conductivity via three independent approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing theories cannot quantitatively account for the measured Hall conductivities of electrolytes under an external magnetic field, so a new theoretical framework is needed to bridge this gap.

Method: The authors extend stochastic density functional theory (SDFT) into "odd SDFT" by incorporating transverse mobilities and chiral time-correlated noise. Hall conductivity is calculated in the absence of hydrodynamic solvent effects using three approaches: (i) direct response, (ii) Green-Kubo relation, and (iii) a hybrid fluctuation-dissipation relation.

Result: The framework yields Hall conductivity calculations via the three methods and is shown to apply more broadly to fluids with broken time-reversal and mirror symmetry, including odd-diffusive fluids and chiral active matter.

Conclusion: Odd SDFT generalizes the relaxation correction of Debye-Hückel-Onsager theory to magnetized electrolytes and provides a foundation for incorporating additional solvent-mediated effects that could quantitatively reconcile theory and experiments.

Abstract: Electrolytes under an external magnetic field exhibit a Hall conductivity, much like metals and semiconductors. However, existing theories fail to quantitatively account for their measured Hall conductivities. To make progress toward such a theory, we develop a stochastic density functional framework for electrolytes in a magnetic field. This framework, which we dub odd SDFT, extends the celebrated stochastic density functional theory (SDFT) to incorporate transverse mobilities and chiral time-correlated noise. Using this framework, we calculate the Hall conductivity in the absence of hydrodynamic solvent effects through three approaches: (i) direct response, (ii) Green-Kubo relation, and (iii) a hybrid fluctuation-dissipation relation. We further show that odd SDFT applies more broadly to fluids with broken time-reversal and mirror symmetry, including odd-diffusive fluids and chiral active matter. Our results generalize the relaxation correction of Debye-Hückel-Onsager theory to magnetized electrolytes and provide a foundation for incorporating additional effects mediated by the solvent that could quantitatively reconcile theory and experiments.

</details>


### [124] [Second largest eigenvalue does not bound stationary entropy production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34352)
*Naoto Shiraishi*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The conjectured spectral dissipation-coherence trade-off inequality by Oberreiter et al. is disproved via an explicit counterexample featuring finite coherent number with vanishing stationary entropy production, excluding a broad class of thermodynamic bounds based on the second largest eigenvalue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Oberreiter et al. conjectured that the coherent number derived from the second largest eigenvalue provides a lower bound on stationary entropy production per unit oscillation, and this work tests whether that spectral bound actually holds.

Method: Explicit construction of a counterexample model.

Result: The constructed model exhibits a finite coherent number accompanied by vanishing stationary entropy production, disproving the conjecture and ruling out a wide class of thermodynamic bounds involving the real and imaginary parts of the second largest eigenvalue of the transition rate matrix.

Conclusion: The second largest eigenvalue does not necessarily characterize the degree of coherent oscillation.

Abstract: We examine the spectral dissipation-coherence trade-off inequality conjectured by Oberreiter {\it et al}. [Phys. Rev. E 106, 014106 (2022)], stating that the coherent number, defined from the second largest eigenvalue, provides a lower bound on the stationary entropy production per unit oscillation. We disprove this conjecture by explicitly constructing a counterexample, which accompanies finite coherent number with vanishing stationary entropy production. This model also excludes a wide class of thermodynamic bound with the real and imaginary parts of the second largest eigenvalue of the transition rate matrix. Our result implies that the second largest eigenvalue does not necessarily characterize the degree of coherent oscillation.

</details>


### [125] [Fluctuations and multifractality in stochastic models of interface growth and population dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34468)
*Maximilien Bernard*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This thesis investigates how disorder and fluctuations govern large-scale growth phenomena across three settings: fluctuating interfaces (anomalous scaling), random spring chains (Anderson localization via spectral properties), and random multiplicative growth with redistribution (inequality/concentration and phase diagrams).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disorder and fluctuations drive large-scale growth phenomena in superficially unrelated systems, yet their common mechanisms are not fully understood. Specifically: the origin of anomalous scaling (local vs. global exponent mismatch) is unclear; the strong-disorder regime of Anderson localization eludes standard weak-disorder expansions; and random multiplicative growth with competing redistribution lacks a systematic understanding of its phase behavior.

Method: Three complementary approaches: (1) two interface-growth models—a heterogeneous elastic line and the stochastic porous medium equation—to contrast statistical versus genuine anomaly origins; (2) spectral analysis of random spring chains with random masses and spring constants, using a new combinatorial method for the strong-disorder regime; (3) study of random multiplicative growth models with site-specific quenched growth rates plus transient fluctuations competing with redistribution.

Result: The apparent anomaly in the heterogeneous elastic line is a purely statistical effect, while the anomaly in the stochastic porous medium equation (strongly nonlinear) is genuine. The new combinatorial method probes strong-disorder localization where weak-disorder expansions break down. Random multiplicative growth with redistribution yields rich phase diagrams from competition between quenched advantage/disadvantage, transient fluctuations, and homogenizing redistribution.

Conclusion: Disorder and fluctuations unify diverse large-scale growth phenomena, producing anomalous scaling, strong-disorder localization, and inequality/concentration with rich phase behavior, with applications to population dynamics, wealth distribution, city growth, and ecology.

Abstract: This thesis studies how disorder and fluctuations shape large-scale growth phenomena in three related settings: fluctuating interfaces, random spring chains, and random multiplicative growth. The first part concerns interface growth and a phenomenon denoted anomalous scaling, where local and global exponents differ.To clarify its origin, we study two models in which this mismatch has different causes. The first is a heterogeneous elastic line, where we show that the apparent anomaly is a purely statistical effect. The second is the stochastic porous medium equation, a strongly nonlinear model in which the anomaly is genuine. The second part concerns Anderson localization, which appears here through the spectral properties of the heterogeneous elastic lines. We study chains with both random masses and random spring constants, with particular emphasis on the strong-disorder regime, where standard weak-disorder expansions break down. We develop a new combinatorial method to probe this regime. The last part concerns random multiplicative growth with redistribution. In such models, changes in wealth, population, or mass, for instance, are proportional to the amount already present, so that small differences are amplified over time. This naturally generates broad distributions and provides a simple mechanism for the emergence of inequality and concentration, with applications to population dynamics, wealth distribution, city growth, and ecology. We in particular study models in which each site has its own quenched growth rate, representing a persistent advantage or disadvantage, and is also subject to transient fluctuations. Redistribution competes with these mechanisms by homogenizing the system. This competition leads to rich phase diagrams.

</details>


### [126] [Out-of-equilibrium finite-time percolation transitions and spinodal-like behaviors after quenches across magnetic first-order transitions of Ising systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35485)
*Andrea Pelissetto,Davide Rossini,Ettore Vicari*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper studies the out-of-equilibrium relaxational dynamics of 2D Ising-like ferromagnetic systems driven across the low-temperature magnetic first-order transition (FOT) line by sudden and slow changes of a homogeneous magnetic field. A dynamic percolation transition emerges at a finite critical time during post-quench evolution, mirroring the standard random-percolation finite-size scaling, though with an h-dependent critical exponent, and marks the metastable-to-stable phase crossing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: First-order transitions (FOTs) and their out-of-equilibrium crossing dynamics, particularly whether percolation-like phenomena arise during relaxation, are not well understood. The authors aim to characterize the relaxational dynamics when ferromagnetic Ising-like systems are driven across the low-temperature magnetic FOT line by sudden and slow magnetic-field variations.

Method: Quench protocols: instantaneously change the homogeneous field from h_i < 0 to h > 0, driving the system from the negatively to the positively magnetized phase. The evolving spin configurations are analyzed via cluster percolation of positive/negative spins, supplemented by dynamic (Kibble-Zurek-like) protocols with slow field variations crossing the FOT line.

Result: A dynamic percolation transition appears at a finite critical time t_c(h) for finite, sufficiently small h, marked by percolation of the largest positive-spin cluster and antipercolation of the largest negative-spin cluster. It displays finite-size scaling as in standard random percolation; the fractal dimension of percolating clusters agrees with the random-percolation value, but the exponent controlling the approach to criticality differs and depends on h. The percolation transition marks the passage from the metastable negatively-magnetized phase to the stable positively-magnetized one. In the small-h limit the percolation critical behavior relates to the spinodal-like behavior of magnetization, so t_c(h) shows a spinodal-like exponential dependence on h. Analogous behavior is observed in slow (Kibble-Zurek-like) protocols.

Conclusion: The dynamic percolation transition at magnetic Ising-like FOTs is likely a generic phenomenon, occurring both under sudden quenches and slow Kibble-Zurek-like driving; its critical behavior links out-of-equilibrium cluster percolation to spinodal-like magnetization dynamics.

Abstract: We analyze the out-of-equilibrium relaxational dynamics of ferromagnetic Ising-like systems driven across their low-temperature magnetic first-order transition (FOT) line, by sudden and slow variations of an external homogenous magnetic field $h$. As a paradigmatic example, we consider a two-dimensional Ising system driven across its low-temperature FOT line by suddently quenching $h$ from $h_i<0$ to $h>0$, inducing a transition from the negatively to the positively magnetized phase. We show the emergence of a dynamic percolation transition at a finite critical time $t_c(h)$ along the post-quench evolution for finite (sufficiently small) values of $h$, which is marked by the percolation of the largest positive-spin cluster and the antipercolation of the largest negative-spin cluster. This out-of-equilibrium percolation transition displays a finite-size scaling behavior as in the standard random-percolation case. However, while the fractal dimension of the percolating clusters is consistent with the random-percolation value, the exponent controlling the approach to criticality differs and depends on $h$. We also show that the percolation transition marks the passage from the metastable negatively-magnetized phase to the stable positively-magnetized one. Therefore, in the small-$h$ limit, the percolation critical behavior is related to the spinodal-like behavior of the magnetization, implying that the percolation time $t_c(h)$ exhibits a spinodal-like exponential dependence on $h$. The existence of percolation transitions is likely a generic phenomenon at magnetic Ising-like FOTs. For example, we observe an analogous behavior in dynamic (Kibble-Zurek-like) protocols entailing slow variations of the magnetic field driving the crossing of the FOT line.

</details>


### [127] [Speed-Fisher Information: Chaos and Irreversibility in Classical and Quantum Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35687)
*Nachiket Karve,Nathan Rose,David Campbell,Anatoli Polkovnikov*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified framework for classical and quantum chaos based on how stationary states respond to slow driving, quantified by a "speed-Fisher information" that diverges with protocol time for chaotic dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical and quantum chaos are typically characterized by distinct, framework-specific diagnostics. A unified perspective connecting both regimes, and linking chaos to thermodynamic/response quantities, is lacking.

Method: The authors probe a system's sensitivity of stationary states to slow driving via susceptibility to the average protocol speed, defining the "speed-Fisher information," and relate it to irreversible entropy production. They analyze its scaling with protocol time and connect the divergence to the perturbation's low-frequency spectral weight. The approach is extended to non-Hamiltonian classical flows, and a short-time quantum regime below the Planckian timescale is identified.

Result: Chaotic dynamics manifests as a divergence of the speed-Fisher information with protocol time, controlled by the perturbation's low-frequency spectral weight. The framework applies to classical and quantum Hamiltonian systems alike, extends to non-Hamiltonian classical flows, and reveals a uniquely quantum scaling of the speed-Fisher information at times shorter than the Planckian timescale.

Conclusion: A unified, response-based characterization of chaos is established, tying chaotic sensitivity to slow driving and irreversible entropy production, with a distinct quantum signature at sub-Planckian times, illustrated through simple classical and quantum examples.

Abstract: We propose a unified perspective on classical and quantum chaos based on the sensitivity of a system's stationary states to slow driving. We probe this sensitivity via the system's susceptibility to the average protocol speed, which we call the ``speed-Fisher information," and relate it to irreversible entropy production in the system. We show that chaotic dynamics manifests as a divergence of the speed-Fisher information with the protocol time, and that this response is controlled by the perturbation's low-frequency spectral weight. This approach to chaos applies to both classical and quantum Hamiltonian systems, and naturally extends to non-Hamiltonian classical flows. Additionally, we identify a quantum regime at times shorter than the Planckian timescale, in which the speed-Fisher information exhibits a uniquely quantum scaling. We illustrate this framework with simple classical and quantum examples

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.str-el'></div>

# cond-mat.str-el [[Back]](#toc)

### [128] [Emergent low-energy many-body Hilbert spaces in Chern bands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31831)
*Chen-Xin Jiang,Zi-Xiang Hu,Bo Yang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper shows that the dominant many-body subspace selected by realistic two-body interactions in Landau levels and Chern bands depends strongly on particle density and can be systematically built from conformal Hilbert spaces, with particles favoring the smallest accommodating CHS for short-range Coulomb interactions, explaining suppressed band mixing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding which many-body subspace dominates the low-energy physics of interacting particles in Landau levels and Chern bands is crucial for describing fractional quantum Hall-like physics. However, it was unclear how the relevant subspace is selected by realistic two-body interactions alone, how it depends on particle density, and why band mixing is suppressed even under strong interactions.

Method: The authors exploit the hierarchical structure of conformal Hilbert spaces (CHS), each possessing a highest-density vacuum state. They compute, for both fermionic and bosonic systems, the many-body quantum metric that quantifies interaction energy scales of these subspaces. They analyze a large family of short-range, Coulomb-based interactions to determine which CHS particles preferentially occupy.

Result: The most relevant many-body subspace depends strongly on particle density and can be constructed systematically. For short-range Coulomb-based interactions, particles preferentially occupy many-body states in the smallest accommodating CHS. The low-energy physics can thus be described via appropriate anyonic degrees of freedom. Band mixing tends to be suppressed even in the strong-interaction limit, with suppression stronger when the band deviates less from the ideal trace condition and when the two-body interaction is shorter-ranged.

Conclusion: The selection of the relevant many-body subspace by realistic interactions is density-dependent and systematically derivable from the CHS hierarchy, enabling an anyonic description of low-energy physics and providing a general explanation for suppressed band mixing in any Chern band, whether lattice or continuum.

Abstract: We show that in Landau levels and more generic Chern bands, the most relevant many-body subspace selected by realistic two-body interactions alone (e.g., in the limit of vanishing bandwidth and band gap) depends strongly on the particle density and can be constructed systematically. Using the well-defined hierarchical structure of conformal Hilbert spaces (CHS), each with a highest-density vacuum state, we compute, for both fermionic and bosonic systems, the many-body quantum metric that quantifies the interaction energy scales of these subspaces. For a large family of short-range, Coulomb-based interactions, particles preferentially occupy the many-body states in the smallest accommodating CHS. This allows us to describe the low-energy physics of the interacting system in terms of the appropriate anyonic degrees of freedom. Our results also explain why, in any Chern band (in lattice or continuum systems), band mixing tends to be suppressed even in the limit of strong interaction; this suppression is stronger when the band deviates less from the ideal trace condition and when the two-body interaction is shorter-ranged.

</details>


### [129] [Zoology of chiral superconductors in Chern bands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31839)
*André Grossi Fonseca,Aidan Reddy,Ahmed Abouelkomsan,Liang Fu,Marin Soljačić*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The authors adapt a target-phase optimization method, combining a differentiable pair-binding-energy loss with gradient-based optimization and rigorous screening, to search for chiral superconductors in a minimal interacting Chern-band model. Using exact diagonalization of spinless electrons in periodically modulated Landau levels with screened Coulomb interactions, they uncover a broad family of chiral superconducting phases at ν=2/3 and ν=1/2, including a first-reported direct transition between chiral superconductors and composite Fermi liquids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chiral superconductivity has recently been observed in van der Waals systems such as rhombohedral multilayer graphene and twisted bilayer MoTe2, where superconductivity emerges near a fractional Chern insulator. This raises the open question of what kinds of superconductors can arise from electrons in a topological (Chern) band with strong repulsive interactions. Existing approaches lack a systematic strategy for searching for such chiral superconducting phases in strongly correlated settings.

Method: The authors adapt the target-phase optimization method to search for chiral superconductors in a minimal model of interacting electrons in a Chern band. They construct a differentiable loss function for superconductors based on the sign oscillation of the pair-binding energy, and combine gradient-based optimization with a rigorous screening procedure to identify and characterize superconducting phases. The framework is applied within exact diagonalization to spinless electrons in periodically modulated Landau levels with screened Coulomb interactions.

Result: They uncover a broad family of chiral superconducting phases. At filling ν=2/3, they recover the previously identified f−if hole superconductors and additionally find p±ip and f+if superconductors of both electrons and holes, occurring near and far from the ideal quantum geometry limit. At ν=1/2, they identify p−ip electron and f−if hole superconductors and find, for the first time, a direct transition between chiral superconductors and composite Fermi liquids.

Conclusion: Chiral superconductivity in Chern bands comprises a diverse landscape of competing pairing instabilities. The target-phase optimization approach is established as a general strategy for searching for quantum phases in complex interacting systems.

Abstract: Evidence for chiral superconductivity has recently been observed in several van der Waals systems including rhombohedral multilayer graphene and twisted bilayer MoTe$_2$. In the latter, superconductivity emerges at carrier densities near a fractional Chern insulator. This raises the question of what kinds of superconductors may emerge in a system of electrons in a topological band with strong repulsive interactions. Here, we adapt the target-phase optimization method to search for chiral superconductors in a minimal model of interacting electrons in a Chern band. We construct a differentiable loss function for superconductors from the sign oscillation of the pair-binding energy and combine gradient-based optimization with a rigorous screening procedure to identify and characterize superconductors. Applying this framework within exact diagonalization to spinless electrons in periodically modulated Landau levels with screened Coulomb interactions, we uncover a broad family of chiral superconducting phases. At filling $ν=2/3$, we recover the previously identified $f-\mathrm{i} f$ hole superconductors and find additional $p\pm \mathrm{i} p$ and $f+\mathrm{i} f$ superconductors of both electrons and holes, occurring near and far from the limit of ideal quantum geometry. At $ν=1/2$, we identify $p- \mathrm{i} p$ electron and $f-\mathrm{i} f$ hole superconductors and find, for the first time, a direct transition between chiral superconductors and composite Fermi liquids. Our results reveal that chiral superconductivity in Chern bands comprises a diverse landscape of competing pairing instabilities and establish target-phase optimization as a general strategy for searching for quantum phases in complex interacting systems.

</details>


### [130] [Read-Rezayi fractional Chern insulators in modulated Bernal graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31846)
*Ruth Mora-Soto,André Grossi Fonseca,Raul Perea-Causin,Hui Liu,Emil J. Bergholtz,Marin Soljačić*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper identifies a Read-Rezayi fractional Chern insulator (Fibonacci-anyon host) at ν=3/5 in gate-screened, periodically modulated Bernal bilayer graphene at zero magnetic field, using target-phase optimization plus band-projected exact diagonalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fibonacci anyons enable universal topological quantum computation and appear as excitations in the Z3 Read-Rezayi phase of the fractional quantum Hall effect, but realistic magnetic-field-free microscopic realizations of this phase have remained elusive.

Method: Model of periodically modulated Bernal bilayer graphene with gate-screened Coulomb interactions. Combines the recently developed target-phase optimization method with band-projected exact diagonalization.

Result: At filling ν=3/5, a region of parameter space is found whose ground state is consistent with a Read-Rezayi fractional Chern insulator. The partially filled band belongs to a two-band complex mimicking geometric aspects of the lowest and first Landau levels, with the ν=1/2 ground state consistent with the Moore-Read state.

Conclusion: Modulated Bernal graphene can realize delicate non-Abelian fractional quantum Hall states at zero magnetic field; target-phase optimization is a practical route to discovering such phases in realistic, high-dimensional microscopic models.

Abstract: Fibonacci anyons provide a universal platform for topological quantum computation, and emerge as low-energy excitations in the $\mathbb{Z}_3$ Read-Rezayi phase in the fractional quantum Hall effect. However, realistic microscopic realizations of this phase in the absence of a magnetic field have remained elusive. We study a model of periodically modulated Bernal bilayer graphene with gate-screened Coulomb interactions. Using the recently developed target-phase optimization method in conjunction with band-projected exact diagonalization, we identify at filling $ν=3/5$ a region of parameter space whose ground state is consistent with a Read-Rezayi fractional Chern insulator. The partially filled band from which it arises is a part of a two-band complex which mimics geometric aspects of the lowest and first Landau levels, with the ground state at $ν=1/2$ consistent with the Moore-Read state. Our results suggest that modulated Bernal graphene can realize delicate non-Abelian fractional quantum Hall states at zero magnetic field, while demonstrating target-phase optimization as a practical route to discovering such phases in realistic, high-dimensional microscopic models.

</details>


### [131] [Chiral Weyl-Kondo semimetallic state through enhanced correlation in CeGaGe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32012)
*Arushi,Kevin Allen,Yuan Fang,Kuan-Sen Lin,Mounica Mahankali,Hari Bhandari,Karthik Rao,Alberto Ruiz Biestro,Christopher Lane,Jian-Xin Zhu,Sanu Mishra,Geoffroy Hautier,Qimiao Si,Emilia Morosan*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: CeGaGe is reported as the first material realization of a chiral Weyl-Kondo semimetal (cWKSM), combining crystal chirality, non-symmorphic symmetry, and Kondo correlations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A cWKSM state—where Kondo hybridization pins chirality-induced Kramers-Weyl crossings near the Fermi level—requires a material uniting crystal chirality, non-symmorphic symmetry, and Kondo correlations. No known material satisfied all three requirements, leaving the state experimentally unrealized.

Method: Magnetization and Hall resistivity measurements (with H || c) on CeGaGe, combined with structural characterization and first-principles calculations for the chiral P4_3 structure.

Result: CeGaGe undergoes a structural transition from achiral I4_1md to chiral P4_3, exhibits antiferromagnetic order below T_N = 4.7 K with a canted magnetic structure, and shows an anomalous Hall conductivity constant below T_K ≈ 7 K that drops sharply above it, tracking Kondo coherence rather than magnetic order. Calculations attribute the AHC to an intrinsic Berry-curvature contribution from Kondo hybridization.

Conclusion: CeGaGe is a rare platform where crystal chirality, strong electronic correlations, and non-trivial band topology coexist, providing the first material realization of a Kondo-driven cWKSM state.

Abstract: Strongly correlated chiral materials have been proposed to host a chiral Weyl-Kondo semimetal (cWKSM) state, in which Kondo hybridization pins chirality-induced Kramers-Weyl point crossings, together with additional symmetry-enforced band crossings, near the Fermi level. Realizing this state requires a material that combines crystal chirality, non-symmorphic symmetry, and Kondo correlations, and currently there are no known materials that combine all these requirements. Here we report evidence for a cWKSM state in CeGaGe, a member of the RXY (R = rare earth; X = Al, Si; Y = Ga, Ge) family. Magnetization measurements confirm long-range antiferromagnetic order below T$_N$ = 4.7 K with a complex, canted magnetic structure. Unique to CeGaGe among the known RXY systems is a structural transition from an achiral tetragonal I4$_{1}$md structure (at high temperatures) to the chiral tetragonal P4$_{3}$ structure (at low temperatures). The structural transition in CeGaGe allows us to highlight the role of chirality in stabilizing the Kramers cWKSM state. Hall resistivity measurements with $H || c$ reveal an anomalous Hall conductivity (AHC) that is constant below the Kondo temperature $T_K \approx 7$ K and drops sharply above it. This crossover tracks the onset of Kondo coherence rather than the magnetic ordering, which occurs at T$_N$ = 4.7 K. Together with first-principles calculations for the $P4_3$ structure, this identifies the AHC as an intrinsic, Berry-curvature-driven contribution generated by Kondo hybridization, rather than a consequence of extrinsic scattering or dynamic scalar spin chirality. These results establish CeGaGe as a rare experimental platform in which crystal chirality, strong electronic correlations, and non-trivial band topology coexist, providing the first material realization of a Kondo-driven cWKSM state.

</details>


### [132] [Symmetry-permuting entanglers for non-invertible symmetry-protected topological phases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32266)
*Minyoung You*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Three Rep(D8) SPT phases, though not connectable by symmetric entanglers, are shown to be connected by symmetry-permuting finite-depth local unitary circuits realizing TQFT-predicted dualities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In 1+1D, SPT phases with invertible symmetry are characterized by symmetric entanglers (globally symmetric finite-depth local unitary circuits generating representative ground states from a product state). For non-invertible symmetries, distinct SPT phases such as the three Rep(D8) SPT phases may not be connected by any symmetric entangler, leaving unclear how their dualities are realized.

Method: The authors construct symmetry-permuting finite-depth local unitary circuits connecting the Rep(D8) SPT phases. In particular, they build an explicit matrix product unitary connecting two of the Rep(D8) SPT phases and analyze its action on string order parameters.

Result: The three Rep(D8) SPT phases can be connected by symmetry-permuting finite-depth local unitary circuits, realizing dualities predicted by TQFT. The explicit matrix product unitary's action on string order parameters reproduces the anyon permutation predicted by the TQFT duality.

Conclusion: Symmetry-permuting finite-depth local unitary circuits provide a mechanism to connect distinct SPT phases of non-invertible symmetry, explicitly realizing TQFT dualities.

Abstract: 1+1D symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases with invertible symmetry can be characterized by the symmetric entanglers: globally symmetric finite-depth local unitary circuits that generate representative ground states from a product state. For non-invertible symmetries, however, distinct SPT phases, such as the three $\mathrm{Rep}(D_8)$ SPT phases, may not be connected by any symmetric entangler. We show that the three $\mathrm{Rep}(D_8)$ SPT phases can nevertheless be connected by \emph{symmetry-permuting} finite-depth local unitary circuits, realizing dualities predicted by topological quantum field theory (TQFT). We construct an explicit matrix product unitary connecting two of the $\mathrm{Rep}(D_8)$ SPT phases, and show that its action on string order parameters reproduces the anyon permutation predicted by the TQFT duality.

</details>


### [133] [GEM: An implementation of the ghost-Gutzwiller approximation for simulating interacting quantum systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32633)
*Samuele Giuli,Tsung-Han Lee,Yong-Xin Yao,Ina Park,Harrison LaBollita,Ivan Pasqua,Nicola Lanatà,Olivier Gingras*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: GEM is an open-source Python package implementing the ghost-Gutzwiller approximation for efficient computation of equilibrium properties in strongly correlated multi-orbital electronic systems, integrated with TRIQS and validated through multiorbital and finite-temperature applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Studying strongly correlated electronic systems, particularly multi-orbital lattice models, demands approaches that are computationally tractable yet accurate. Existing quantum embedding methods are often computationally demanding, creating a need for a more efficient framework for equilibrium calculations.

Method: The paper formulates the ghost-Gutzwiller approximation method theoretically and implements it via GEM, an open-source Python package. GEM supports zero- and finite-temperature calculations and symmetry-broken phases, and integrates with the TRIQS ecosystem for model construction, self-consistent solution, and evaluation of physical observables.

Result: The implementation is validated against established results. GEM's capabilities are illustrated through multiorbital and finite-temperature applications, and its computational cost is compared with more demanding quantum embedding approaches, showing improved efficiency.

Conclusion: GEM provides a computationally efficient, open-source framework for equilibrium studies of strongly correlated multi-orbital systems via the ghost-Gutzwiller approximation, offering a practical and less costly alternative to heavier quantum embedding methods.

Abstract: We present GEM (Ghost Embedding Method) from the title here, an open-source software package written in Python for computing equilibrium properties of strongly correlated electronic systems within the ghost-Gutzwiller approximation method. GEM provides a computationally efficient framework for studying multi-orbital lattice models. It supports zero- and finite-temperature calculations and symmetry broken phases. It is integrated with the TRIQS ecosystem, providing tools for model construction, self-consistent solution, and evaluation of physical observables. We first detail the method's theoretical formulation, then we present the software architecture, and finally we introduce some practical workflow, which also validates the implementation against established results. In particular, we illustrate the capabilities of GEM through multiorbital and finite-temperature applications and discuss its computational cost relative to more demanding quantum embedding approaches.

</details>


### [134] [How Much Do We Understand Ce L3 XANES? Ab Initio Insights into Configuration-Specific Screening at the Multiplet--Continuum Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32968)
*Alessandro Mirone*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper addresses how local multiplet signatures, collective core-hole screening, and an extended photoelectron reconcile in Ce L3 XANES. Using the XSpectruplet method that couples local multiplet dynamics with continuum photoelectron propagation in self-consistent Kohn-Sham screening potentials, it identifies two stationary screening states for Ce4+ in CeO2 with white-line maxima ~7.3 eV apart but the same effective local 4f0 configuration, weighted ~63% and ~30%, and uses a transferred correction to estimate the CePO4 pre-edge-white-line gap as 6.38 eV.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivated by a question raised by Kvashnina [Chem. Eur. J. 30, e202400755 (2024)], the study seeks to reconcile the tension among local multiplet signatures, collective core-hole screening, and an extended photoelectron in Ce L3 x-ray absorption near-edge spectra.

Method: The authors use XSpectruplet, which couples local multiplet dynamics to continuum photoelectron propagation in self-consistent Kohn-Sham screening potentials, applying a heuristic search for stationary screening states. They analyze Ce4+ in CeO2 and Ce3+ in monazite CePO4, computing total-energy differences, squared overlaps with the unrelaxed sudden reference for statistical weights, and a transferred net correction for the CePO4 gap.

Result: For Ce4+ in CeO2, primary and secondary stationary screening states have white-line maxima ~7.3 eV apart after including their independently calculated total-energy difference, both retaining the same effective local 4f0 sector, with squared overlaps giving weights of ~63% and ~30%. Their contrasting screening arises from collective rehybridization of occupied orbitals, not an extra localized 4f spectator, and contributes to both white-line and satellite regions. Ce3+ in CePO4 shows a localized 4f1 spectator in multiplet dynamics. Independent relaxation of neutral 4f and white-line states gives a CeO2 pre-edge-white-line gap of 10.51 eV, close to the ~10.7 eV experimental separation; transferring the net correction gives an estimated CePO4 gap of 6.38 eV versus ~8.1 eV experimentally.

Conclusion: Distinct spectral contributions can arise from collective screening without any change in the effective local configuration, reconciling local multiplet signatures, collective core-hole screening, and extended photoelectron behavior in Ce L3 XANES.

Abstract: How can local multiplet signatures, collective core-hole screening, and an extended photoelectron be reconciled in Ce $L_3$ x-ray absorption near-edge spectra? Motivated by the question raised by Kvashnina [Chem. Eur. J. 30, e202400755 (2024)], we address this challenge using XSpectruplet, which couples local multiplet dynamics to continuum photoelectron propagation in self-consistent Kohn--Sham screening potentials. For Ce$^{4+}$ in CeO$_2$, our heuristic search identifies primary and secondary stationary screening states whose calculated white-line maxima lie about 7.3~eV apart after including their independently calculated total-energy difference, yet both retain the same effective local spectroscopic $4f^0$ sector. Their squared overlaps with the unrelaxed sudden reference give approximate statistical weights of 63\% and 30\%, respectively, within an independent-channel description. Their contrasting screening responses arise from collective rehybridization of occupied orbitals, rather than occupation of an additional localized $4f$ spectator, and produce contributions in both the main white-line and satellite regions. Ce$^{3+}$ in monazite CePO$_4$ provides the contrasting case of a localized $4f^1$ spectator participating explicitly in the multiplet dynamics. Independent relaxation of neutral $4f$ and white-line states gives a CeO$_2$ pre-edge--white-line gap of 10.51~eV, close to the experimental separation of approximately 10.7~eV. Its excess over the fixed-background spectral gap defines a net correction, which we transfer heuristically to CePO$_4$ without changing its local multiplet structure. This gives an estimated CePO$_4$ gap of 6.38~eV, compared with approximately 8.1~eV experimentally. Distinct spectral contributions can thus arise from collective screening without a change in the effective local configuration.

</details>


### [135] [Kitaev-Heisenberg model on the square-hexagon-dodecagon lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33454)
*Saeed Barari,Yasir Iqbal,Ganapathy Baskaran,Saeed S. Jahromi,Chunxiao Liu,Julien Vidal*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The spin-1/2 Kitaev-Heisenberg model on the square-hexagon-dodecagon lattice with tri-colored Kitaev exchanges is studied. The pure Kitaev limit hosts a gapped toric code phase (except at a Dirac point) with three distinct vortex anyon assignments; adding isotropic Heisenberg interactions yields four collinear magnetic phases plus a special product-state point.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kitaev physics on non-honeycomb, tricoordinated lattices (square-hexagon-dodecagon) is relatively unexplored, and the interplay between Kitaev exchange and Heisenberg interactions on such geometries—especially the anyon structure of vortex excitations and the resulting magnetic phase diagram—remains an open question.

Method: For the pure Kitaev model, the standard mapping to a quadratic Majorana-fermion problem in a Z2 gauge field is used, with vortex-sector fermion parity determining relative e/m anyon assignments and zero crossings of vortex-bound Majorana levels locating their boundaries. For the isotropic Kitaev-Heisenberg model, graph-based projected entangled-pair-state (PEPS) calculations, exact diagonalization, and linear spin-wave theory are combined.

Result: The pure Kitaev model exhibits a gapped spectrum throughout most of the positive-coupling phase diagram, except at one point where twofold-degenerate bands form a Dirac cone; the gapped region is a toric code phase with three distinct relative assignments of e and m anyons to square, hexagonal, and dodecagonal vortex excitations. Including isotropic Heisenberg interactions yields, besides robust toric code topological phases near the Kitaev limits, four collinear magnetically ordered phases characterized by local and relative hexagonal-plaquette orderings, and a special point whose ground state is a product state.

Conclusion: The square-hexagon-dodecagon Kitaev(-Heisenberg) model realizes a rich interplay of a gapped toric code phase with nontrivial anyon assignments and multiple collinear magnetic orders, providing a platform for studying vortex anyon physics and topological-to-magnetic transitions on a tricoordinated lattice.

Abstract: We study the spin-$1/2$ Kitaev-Heisenberg model on the square-hexagon-dodecagon lattice with a symmetry-preserving tri-coloring of the Kitaev exchanges. We focus on two cases: (i) the pure Kitaev model with anisotropic exchanges and (ii) the Kitaev-Heisenberg model with isotropic Kitaev exchange. In the absence of Heisenberg interactions, we use the standard mapping of the Kitaev model onto a quadratic Majorana-fermion problem in a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ gauge field. We find a gapped energy spectrum throughout the positive-coupling phase diagram, except at a single point where the twofold-degenerate energy bands form a Dirac cone. This unique gapped region corresponds to a toric code phase. However, depending on the couplings, vortex excitations on square, hexagonal, and dodecagonal plaquettes realize three distinct relative assignments to the $e$ and $m$ anyons. We determine these relative assignments from the physical fermion parity of the vortex sectors and locate their boundaries by tracking zero crossings of vortex-bound Majorana levels. For the isotropic Kitaev-Heisenberg model, we combine graph-based projected entangled-pair-state calculations, exact diagonalizations, and linear spin-wave theory to study the entire phase diagram. Apart from the toric code topological phases that are robust around the Kitaev limits, we obtain four collinear magnetically ordered phases that can be characterized by their local and relative orderings of hexagonal plaquettes. We also identify a special point at which the ground state is a product state.

</details>


### [136] [Generalized η-pairing eigenstates in three-component Hubbard models under a transverse field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33568)
*F. X. Liu,Z. Song*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper studies three-component Hubbard models on a bipartite lattice with a transverse field, constructing exact eigenstates via a generalized η-pairing operator and restricted spectrum-generating algebra, revealing off-diagonal long-range order, quantum many-body scars, and long-range magnetic order, with quench protocols validated numerically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unlike the two-component Hubbard model, whose η-pairing symmetry survives a transverse field, the three-component model breaks both η-pairing symmetry and per-component particle-number conservation, motivating the search for generalized symmetry structures and exact solvable eigenstates in this more complex setting.

Method: The authors introduce a generalized η-pairing operator as a hybridization of three types of two-component pairing operators and apply the restricted spectrum-generating algebra to construct exact eigenstates. They also build an exact tensor-product state and propose three quench-dynamics protocols, verified through numerical simulations.

Result: The constructed exact eigenstates exhibit off-diagonal long-range order. The tensor-product state shows coherent periodic dynamics realizing quantum many-body scar dynamics while simultaneously displaying long-range magnetic order. The three proposed preparation protocols via quench dynamics are confirmed effective by numerical simulation.

Conclusion: The three-component Hubbard model with a transverse field supports a generalized η-pairing structure yielding exact eigenstates with off-diagonal long-range order and a scarred tensor-product state with long-range magnetic order, whose quench-based preparation is numerically feasible.

Abstract: We investigate three-component Hubbard models on a bipartite lattice in the presence of a trans?verse field. Unlike the two-component Hubbard model, where the η-pairing symmetry survives the transverse field, the three-component model possesses neither η-pairing symmetry nor conservation of the particle number of each component. We introduce a generalized η-pairing operator, formed as a hybridization of three types of two-component pairing operators, and employ the restricted spectrum-generating algebra to construct a family of exact eigenstates. These eigenstates exhibit off-diagonal long-range order. Furthermore, we construct an exact tensor-product state whose co?herent periodic dynamics provide an exact realization of quantum many-body scar dynamics while simultaneously exhibiting long-range magnetic order. We also propose three protocols for preparing this state via quench dynamics, and verify their effectiveness through numerical simulations.

</details>


### [137] [Bogoliubov coupled-cluster theory for su(2) Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34128)
*Swarnamoy Ghosh,Thomas M. Henderson,Gustavo E. Scuseria*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This work applies Bogoliubov coupled-cluster theory to su(2)-algebra Hamiltonians (pairing, XXZ, J1-J2), using BCS and spin-BCS symmetry-broken references, and computes energies, response/relaxed density matrices, and correlation functions with systematically improvable accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coupled-cluster theory is accurate for weakly correlated systems but breaks down for strongly correlated systems where the mean-field reference is qualitatively poor. Symmetry-broken references (unrestricted for spin symmetry breaking, Bogoliubov for number symmetry breaking) can ameliorate these failures, motivating a unified treatment of su(2) Hamiltonians.

Method: Bogoliubov coupled-cluster is applied to Hamiltonians with an underlying su(2) algebra, exploiting the correspondence between fermionic-pair operators and spin-1/2 operators. The pairing Hamiltonian uses a BCS quasiparticle vacuum reference; spin systems (XXZ, J1-J2) use an analogous spin-BCS reference that breaks Sz symmetry. Correlation is included via a hierarchy of coupled-cluster approximations. Response density matrices are computed for spin-spin correlation functions, pairing parameter, and particle-number variance, and relaxed density matrices are derived to include orbital relaxation effects.

Result: Benchmark calculations for the half-filled pairing Hamiltonian and spin models show that Bogoliubov coupled cluster provides a systematically improvable, wave-function-based approach for su(2) Hamiltonians, including geometrically frustrated spin systems that require complex and noncollinear BCS references.

Conclusion: Bogoliubov coupled cluster with symmetry-broken references is a viable and systematically improvable framework for strongly correlated su(2) Hamiltonians, extending its applicability from pairing to frustrated spin systems.

Abstract: Coupled-cluster theory is the method of choice for weakly correlated systems, but in strongly correlated systems where the mean-field reference is qualitatively poor, it can break down badly. These failures can be ameliorated by using symmetry-broken mean-field references. For systems that spontaneously break spin symmetry, we might favor an unrestricted reference. In systems that instead break number symmetry, we turn to Bogoliubov coupled cluster theory. In this work, we apply Bogoliubov coupled-cluster to Hamiltonians with an underlying su(2) algebra to study the pairing Hamiltonian and the spin XXZ and J1-J2 models by exploiting the correspondence between fermionic-pair operators and spin-1/2 operators. For the pairing problem, the reference is the usual Bardeen--Cooper--Schrieffer (BCS) quasiparticle vacuum, while for spin systems we use the analogous spin-BCS reference, which breaks Sz symmetry and provides a flexible starting point for strongly correlated regimes. Correlation is incorporated through a hierarchy of coupled-cluster approximations. Beyond energies, we compute the response density matrices for the evaluation of spin--spin correlation functions for the XXZ and J1-J2 Heisenberg models, and the pairing parameter and particle-number variance for the pairing Hamiltonian. We also derive the corresponding relaxed density matrices, including the effects of orbital relaxation. Benchmark calculations for the half-filled pairing Hamiltonian and spin models demonstrate that Bogoliubov coupled cluster provides a systematically improvable wave-function-based approach for su(2) Hamiltonians, including geometrically frustrated spin systems requiring complex and noncollinear BCS references.

</details>


### [138] [Scaling Theory for Learning Low-Energy Quantum Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33777)
*Guijing Duan,Chen Mo,Di Luo*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Low-energy subspace (not individual eigenstates) is the right object to learn across a Hamiltonian family; cancelling the first q nonconstant response orders bounds energy-density error by d^{2(q+1)}, sharpened to d^2(nd^2)^q by locality, giving log-polynomial sampling cost and a finite-size probe of criticality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing phase diagrams and energy surfaces requires low-energy states over a family of Hamiltonians, but solving each parameter point independently is prohibitively expensive. The key question addressed is how much information about the low-energy sector is contained in a small set of reference wavefunctions, rather than how a single labeled eigenstate behaves under parameter variation.

Method: The paper identifies the isolated low-energy subspace as the physically relevant object, arguing it remains well defined through degeneracies and level crossings and can be learned efficiently from nearby sampled states. Working within this subspace, it analyzes sampling patterns that cancel the first q nonconstant response orders and derives error bounds for the retained levels, then specializes to gapped local ground states on n sites to exploit locality.

Result: A sampling pattern cancelling the first q nonconstant response orders yields an energy-density error bounded by d^{2(q+1)} for every retained level, where d is the parameter-space sampling distance. For a gapped local ground state on n sites, locality sharpens this to d^2(nd^2)^q, identifying nd^2 as the natural scaling variable. In a local analytic regime the sampling cost is K = O([log(1/ε)]^D) at fixed parameter dimension D. Numerical results on Heisenberg and transverse-field Ising chains confirm the distance scaling law and the nd^2 collapse.

Conclusion: The low-energy subspace is learnable from a small set of sampled wavefunctions, with error scaling governed by the sampling distance d and, under locality, by the variable nd^2. This characterizes learnability and, beyond it, provides a finite-size probe of phase transitions near criticality via the scaling behavior.

Abstract: Phase diagrams and energy surfaces require low-energy states across a family of Hamiltonians, but solving each parameter point independently is prohibitively expensive. An important question is not how a single labeled eigenstate behaves under parameter variation, but how much information about the low-energy sector is contained in a small set of reference wavefunctions. We show that the physically relevant object is the isolated low-energy subspace itself: it remains well defined through degeneracies and level crossings, and it can be learned efficiently from nearby sampled states. Working in this subspace, we prove that a sampling pattern cancelling the first $q$ nonconstant response orders yields an energy-density error bounded by $d^{2(q+1)}$ for every retained level, where $d$ is the parameter-space sampling distance. For a gapped local ground state on $n$ sites, locality sharpens this to $d^2(nd^2)^q$, identifying $nd^2$ as the natural scaling variable governing the effectiveness of higher-order information from sampled wavefunctions. In a local analytic regime this gives a sampling cost $K=\mathcal{O}([\log(1/\varepsilon)]^D)$ at fixed parameter dimension $D$. Numerical results on Heisenberg and transverse-field Ising chains confirm the distance scaling law and the $nd^2$ collapse. Beyond characterizing learnability, the scaling behavior further provides a finite-size probe of phase transitions near criticality.

</details>


### [139] [t-J model at 50](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35178)
*Józef Spałek*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The abstract reviews the t-J model derived from the Hubbard model, its distinctive features (advantages and shortcomings), its connection to magnetism, unconventional superconductivity, and Mott-Hubbard localization, and its extension to a three-orbital setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The author aims to succinctly overview the original formulation of the t-J model starting from the Hubbard model and to emphasize its unique features—advantages and shortcomings—when discussing its physical properties.

Method: The paper provides a conceptual overview: it derives the t-J model from the Hubbard model, characterizes the link between magnetism, unconventional superconductivity, and Mott-Hubbard localization of correlated carriers in a narrow band, and then generalizes the model to a three-orbital situation.

Result: The abstract reports that the t-J model's basic physics is organized around the interplay of magnetism, unconventional superconductivity, and Mott-Hubbard localization, and that the model can be generalized to three orbitals.

Conclusion: The main purpose is to list some basic features of the physics associated with the t-J model, both in its original single-band form and in its three-orbital generalization.

Abstract: I briefly overview the original formulation of the t-J model starting from Hubbard model, as well as stress its unique features, i.e., advantages and shortcomings when discussing its physical properties. Particular emphasis is put on a brief characterization of connection between magnetism, unconventional superconductivity, and the Mott-Hubbard localization of the correlated carriers in a narrow band. In the second part I summarize the model generalization to the three--orbital situation. The main purpose is to list some basic features of the physics associated with t--J model.

</details>


### [140] [Electronic and topological properties of Ce-based honeycomb ferromagnet Ce$_2$Zn$_6$Ge$_3$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34477)
*Yanen Huang,Zihan Yang,Jiawen Zhang,Yuwei Zhou,Lubin Wang,Gang Li,Michael Smidman,Chao Cao,Yu Liu,Huiqiu Yuan*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Ce$_2$Zn$_6$Ge$_3$, a rare Ce-based honeycomb ferromagnet, is studied via quantum oscillations, TDO measurements, and DFT. Localized 4$f$ electrons dominate the ferromagnetism, and negative longitudinal magnetoresistance, a non-zero Berry phase, and calculated Weyl points near $E_F$ indicate nontrivial topology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ce$_2$Zn$_6$Ge$_3$ is a rare Ce-based honeycomb ferromagnet, offering a potential platform to explore the interplay of magnetism, topology, and quantum criticality, yet its Fermi surface and topological properties remain experimentally unexplored.

Method: The authors performed quantum oscillation measurements via magnetoresistance and tunnel diode oscillator (TDO) techniques, complemented by density functional theory (DFT) calculations, examining the angle dependence of oscillation frequencies and comparing with DFT scenarios for localized versus itinerant 4$f$ electrons.

Result: Four fundamental frequencies were observed in the quantum oscillations, with angle dependence more consistent with DFT assuming localized 4$f$ electrons. Negative longitudinal magnetoresistance, a non-zero Berry phase, and two pairs of Weyl points near the Fermi level in the calculated band structure were found.

Conclusion: The results suggest a localized nature of ferromagnetism in Ce$_2$Zn$_6$Ge$_3$ and provide strong evidence for nontrivial topology, positioning it as a unique platform for studying magnetism, topology, quantum criticality, and their interplay.

Abstract: Ce$_2$Zn$_6$Ge$_3$ is a rare example of the Ce-based honeycomb ferromagnet. Here, we report its Fermi surface and topological properties by quantum oscillations via the magnetoresistance and tunnel diode oscillator (TDO) based measurements, in combination with the density functional theory (DFT) calculations. Four fundamental frequencies are observed in the quantum oscillations, and their angle dependence is more compatible with the DFT calculations assuming that the 4$f$-electrons are localized, suggesting a localized nature of ferromagnetism in Ce$_2$Zn$_6$Ge$_3$. Furthermore, the observations of negative longitudinal magnetoresistance and non-zero Berry phase, as well as the existence of two pairs of Weyl points near the Fermi level as revealed from the calculated electronic structures, provide strong evidence for nontrivial topology in Ce$_2$Zn$_6$Ge$_3$. These findings suggest that Ce$_2$Zn$_6$Ge$_3$ could provide a unique platform to study magnetism, topology, quantum criticality and their interplay.

</details>


### [141] [Enhanced sliding in the coexisting Charge Density Wave phase of strained TbTe3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34569)
*Antoine Gallo Frantz,Aleksander Sinchenko,Luc Ortega,Abdellali Hadj Azzem,Jose Emilio Lorenzo,Pierre Monceau,David Le Bolloch,Vincent Jacques*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Uniaxial tensile stress can switch the charge density wave (CDW) orientation in TbTe3 from the pristine c-axis CDW (CDWc) to an orthogonal a-axis CDW (CDWa), with a coexistence phase when the in-plane lattice parameters a and c are equal. By measuring current-voltage characteristics and differential resistances, the authors show that the strain-induced CDWa also exhibits non-linear sliding dynamics, proving its incommensurate nature, and that sliding occurs for both CDWc and CDWa in the coexistence phase with reduced threshold currents. The linearity of threshold fields is preserved across all deformation states, with finite threshold fields at Tc.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mechanical tensile stress has recently been shown to trigger unconventional phases in quantum materials, including an orientational transition of the CDW in TbTe3 linked to in-plane a/c lattice anisotropy. The dynamics of the strain-induced orthogonal CDW and of the coexistence phase remained unexplored, motivating a study of their sliding behavior.

Method: The authors probe the sliding dynamics of both CDWc and CDWa as a function of applied tensile stress—covering the pure CDWc, pure CDWa, and coexistence phases—by measuring current-voltage characteristics and associated differential resistances.

Result: The pristine CDWc displays non-linear dynamics (sliding), and the strain-induced CDWa phase shows the same characteristics, proving its incommensurate nature. Sliding also occurs for both CDWc and CDWa in the coexistence phase, with reduced threshold currents. The linearity of threshold fields is maintained for all deformation states, with finite threshold fields at Tc.

Conclusion: The strain-induced CDWa is incommensurate and exhibits sliding, and both orthogonal CDWs slide in the coexistence phase with reduced threshold currents, questioning the real-space microstructure of these coexisting orthogonal CDWs. The finite threshold fields at Tc are discussed in terms of commensurability and pinning potentials, opening new questions for the theoretical description of sliding CDWs in quasi-2D systems.

Abstract: In the last years, the application of mechanical tensile stress has been shown to trigger unconventional phases in quantum materials. Recently, an orientational transition of the charge density wave (CDW) of TbTe3 was reported under the application of moderate uniaxial tensile stress, associated to the in-plane a/c lattice anisotropy. This leads to the disappearance of the pristine c-oriented CDW (CDWc) and to the appearance of the orthogonal a-oriented one (CDWa). A coexistence phase with a superposition of CDWa and CDWc appears when a=c.
