Here, we probe the sliding dynamics of both CDWc and CDWa as a function of applied tensile stress, i.e. in the pure CDWc, pure CDWa and coexistence phases, by measuring current-voltage characteristics and associated differential resistances. If the pristine CDWc has been known to display non-linear dynamics and thus sliding for several years, we show here that the strain-induced CDWa phase also displays the same characteristics, proving its incommensurate nature. Moreover, we show that sliding also takes place both for CDWc and CDWa, with reduced threshold currents, in the coexistence phase, which questions the microstructure of these coexisting orthogonal CDWs in real space. Finally, the linearity of the threshold fields is kept for all deformation states, with finite threshold fields at Tc, which is discussed in terms of commensurability and pinning potentials. This work opens new questions for the theoretical description of sliding CDWs in quasi-2D systems.

</details>


### [142] [Quantum-Geometric Amplification of Nonperturbative Interaction Scales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34607)
*Stefano Bilamour,René Meyer,Johanna Erdmenger,Domenico Di Sante*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Weak interactions at a 2D quadratic band touching (QBT) open an exponentially small symmetry-breaking gap $m\sim e^{-A/V_1}$ that no finite order of perturbation theory can capture. The authors show that the singular quantum geometry of the gapped QBT converts this beyond-all-orders scale into an algebraically enhanced response: the quantum metric develops a momentum-space hot spot whose Brillouin-zone integral diverges logarithmically, growing algebraically as the gap is dynamically generated. Via the inverse-frequency Souza–Wilkens–Martin sum rule, this amplification appears in the negative-first longitudinal optical moment, despite the ordinary conductivity remaining $O(e^2/\hbar)$ with an exponentially small absorption threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonperturbative interaction-induced gaps at a quadratic band touching are exponentially small in the coupling, making them essentially invisible to finite-order perturbation theory and to conventional response measures. The paper asks whether this beyond-all-orders energy scale can nonetheless leave a parametrically large, observable signature through the quantum geometry of the band structure.

Method: Analytical treatment of a massive QBT, identifying a momentum-space hot spot in the quantum metric whose Brillouin-zone integral diverges logarithmically. Application of the inverse-frequency Souza–Wilkens–Martin optical sum rule to relate this geometric singularity to the negative-first longitudinal optical moment. Microscopic verification in an interacting kagome lattice model that develops a spontaneous quantum anomalous Hall mass at its QBT via loop-current order.

Result: The quantum metric integral diverges logarithmically and hence grows algebraically when the gap is dynamically generated by interactions. The same algebraic amplification appears in the negative-first longitudinal optical moment through the Souza–Wilkens–Martin sum rule, even though the ordinary optical conductivity remains $O(e^2/\hbar)$ and its absorption threshold is exponentially small. Results are established analytically for the massive QBT and confirmed microscopically in the interacting kagome model.

Conclusion: Quantum geometry acts as an asymptotic amplifier of nonperturbative interaction scales, converting an exponentially small beyond-all-orders gap into an algebraically enhanced optical response that is, in principle, observable.

Abstract: Weak interactions at a two-dimensional quadratic band touching (QBT) can generate an exponentially small symmetry-breaking gap, $m\sim e^{-A/V_1}$, that cannot be captured by every finite order of perturbation theory. We show that the singular quantum geometry of the gapped QBT converts this beyond-all-orders scale into an algebraically enhanced response. The quantum metric develops a momentum-space hot spot whose Brillouin-zone integral diverges logarithmically, and therefore grows algebraically when the gap is dynamically generated by interactions. Through the inverse-frequency Souza--Wilkens--Martin optical sum rule, the same amplification appears in the negative-first longitudinal optical moment, even though the ordinary optical conductivity remains $O(e^2/\hbar)$ and its absorption threshold is exponentially small. We establish these results analytically for a massive QBT and verify them microscopically in an interacting kagome lattice model known to develop a spontaneous quantum anomalous Hall mass at its QBT from loop currents order. Our results identify quantum geometry as an asymptotic amplifier of nonperturbative interaction scales.

</details>


### [143] [Thermal quasi-Devil's staircase in an anisotropic triangular-lattice Rydberg array](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29400)
*Jinghao Cao,Siyi Yang,Jingya Wang,Dong-Xu Liu,Zheng Yan*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The authors propose that a thermal quasi-Devil's staircase of winding-sector crossovers can be realized and observed in finite Rydberg-atom arrays, since thermal fluctuations overcome the topological barriers that block sector changes in purely quantum dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Devil's staircases—sequences of transitions between topological sectors as a control parameter varies—are predicted in frustrated magnets and lattice gauge theories but are hard to observe because the winding number labeling each sector is topologically protected; local quantum dynamics alone cannot move the system between steps. An experimentally accessible route is therefore needed.

Method: The anisotropic triangular-lattice Ising antiferromagnet is studied via a dimer mapping, a directed-string description, exact thermodynamics, and Monte Carlo simulations, with focus on the defect-free string manifold and the role of triangle-rule defects.

Result: In the defect-free string manifold, the commensurate–incommensurate onset follows from a simple energy–entropy balance, and the exact Ising critical relation reduces to the same condition when triangle-rule defects are rare. On finite lattices this onset appears as a quasi-Devil's staircase of winding-sector crossovers, resolved through winding distributions, structure factors, and directional correlations.

Conclusion: Thermal fluctuations overcome the topological barriers blocking sector changes under purely quantum dynamics, and the staircase occurs precisely at the finite sizes and tunable effective temperatures of current Rydberg platforms, making it directly observable. The finite-size steps are not phase transitions: in the thermodynamic limit only a single phase transition survives, with the system crossing over to a defect-dominated paramagnet.

Abstract: A Devil's staircase is a sequence of transitions between topological sectors as a control parameter is varied. Such staircases are predicted in frustrated magnets and lattice gauge theories, but observing them is hard: the winding number that labels each sector is topologically protected, so local quantum dynamics alone cannot move the system from one step to the next. Here we propose and analyze an experimentally feasible way to realize a thermal quasi-Devil's staircase in a finite Rydberg-atom array. We study the anisotropic triangular-lattice Ising antiferromagnet using a dimer mapping, a directed-string description, exact thermodynamics, and Monte Carlo simulations. In the defect-free string manifold, the commensurate--incommensurate onset follows from a simple energy--entropy balance, and the exact Ising critical relation reduces to the same condition when triangle-rule defects are rare. On a finite lattice, this onset appears as a quasi-Devil's staircase of winding-sector crossovers, which we resolve through winding distributions, structure factors, and directional correlations. The key point is that thermal fluctuations overcome the topological barriers that block sector changes under purely quantum dynamics, and the staircase exists precisely at the finite sizes and tunable effective temperatures of current Rydberg platforms. This makes the effect directly observable in existing experiments. Our comparison also shows that the finite-size steps are not phase transitions: in the thermodynamic limit only a single phase transition survives, and the system crosses over to a defect-dominated paramagnet.

</details>


### [144] [Chiral Weyl--Kondo semimetal and circular photogalvanic effect in a prototype Kondo lattice system](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35602)
*Yuan Fang,Kuan-Sen Lin,Mounica Mahankali,Arushi,Kevin Allen,Sanu Mishra,Emilia Morosan,Qimiao Si*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: A prototype Kondo lattice model with tetragonal symmetry is constructed to demonstrate symmetry-enforced Kramers chiral Weyl fermions and to predict sharp resonant peaks in the circular photogalvanic effect as a spectroscopic signature of Kondo-driven chiral Weyl nodes in chiral Weyl-Kondo semimetals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chiral Weyl-Kondo semimetals (cWKSM) host chiral Weyl quasiparticles near the Fermi energy arising from Kondo-driven reconstruction of strongly correlated states in chiral heavy fermion systems. Recent experiments in CeGaGe provide a concrete realization, motivating a materials-independent understanding of the essential physics beyond material-specific details.

Method: The authors construct a prototype Kondo lattice model retaining only the essential couplings that respect the associated tetragonal crystalline symmetries. Analytical and numerical calculations are performed to study the quasiparticle spectrum and the nonlinear optical response, specifically the circular photogalvanic effect.

Result: The simplified model robustly demonstrates symmetry-enforced Kramers Weyl fermions and related topological nodal states in the heavy quasiparticle spectrum. Both analytical and numerical calculations identify sharp peaks in the frequency domain of the circular photogalvanic effect as signatures of the Kondo-driven chiral Weyl nodes, with the spectral sharpness reflecting the resonant nature of the underlying strongly correlated electronic excitations.

Conclusion: The cWKSM platform offers a unique setting to spectroscopically identify topological fermions induced by strong electron correlations. The results provide new insights into strongly correlated gapless topological matter, using the circular photogalvanic effect as a probe of Kondo-driven chiral Weyl nodes.

Abstract: Chiral Weyl--Kondo semimetals (cWKSM) provide a setting in which chiral Weyl quasiparticles emerge in the immediate vicinity of the Fermi energy from a Kondo-driven reconstruction of the strongly correlated electronic states in chiral heavy fermion systems (K.-S. Lin et al., arXiv:2602.22185). A defining characteristic of this strongly correlated topological state is the Kramers chiral Weyl fermions in the low-energy quasiparticle states. Recently, experiments in CeGaGe have emerged as a concrete realization of the proposed effect (Arushi et al., preprint). Motivated by these findings, here we go beyond the materials-specific effects by constructing a prototype Kondo lattice model; it incorporates only the essential couplings that respect the associated tetragonal crystalline symmetries. This simplification allows us to robustly demonstrate the symmetry-enforced Kramers Weyl fermions and related topological nodal states in the spectrum of heavy quasiparticles. Furthermore, the simplification provides a tractable setting to determine the salient features in the system's nonlinear optical response, the circular photogalvanic effect, in chiral Weyl--Kondo semimetals. Both analytical and numerical calculations identify sharp peaks in the frequency domain as signatures of the Kondo-driven chiral Weyl nodes; the sharpness of the spectrum reflects the resonant nature of the underlying strongly correlated electronic excitations. Thus, cWKSM provides a unique setting to spectroscopically identify topological fermions that are induced by strong electron correlations. As such, our results are expected to bring about much needed new insights into the understanding of strongly correlated gapless topological matter.

</details>


### [145] [Perfect Born Sampling of Symmetric Thermal Tensor Network for Quantum Lattice Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35754)
*Jianxin Gao,Qiaoyi Li,Yuan Gao,Chuanshu Xu,Guoliang Wu,Su Yi,Bin-Bin Chen,Wei Li*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper introduces a "perfect Born sampling" approach for thermal tensor networks that samples directly from the purified density matrix via the Born rule while incorporating Abelian and non-Abelian symmetries through sampling of symmetry quantum numbers. Implemented as stoMPS and stoPEPS, it achieves both high sampling efficiency and full symmetry acceleration, enabling accurate simulations of large-scale quantum lattice models at ultralow temperatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate calculation of quantum lattice models at low temperatures is a major challenge in many-body physics. Stochastic sampling of tensor-network states is promising, but existing schemes face a fundamental dilemma: sampling efficiency and symmetry acceleration cannot be achieved simultaneously.

Method: A perfect Born sampling approach for thermal tensor networks is proposed. It performs importance sampling directly from the purified density matrix via the Born rule and incorporates Abelian and non-Abelian symmetries by sampling symmetry quantum numbers. The method is realized as stochastic matrix product states (stoMPS) and stochastic projected entangled pair states (stoPEPS).

Result: Using stoMPS, the square-lattice Hubbard model on cylinders up to width W=10 is accurately simulated, and the triangular-lattice Hubbard model is studied down to T/t = 1/64, revealing scalar chiral order at half filling and kinetic ferromagnetism upon electron doping. The method is extended to finite-temperature quantum dynamics, demonstrated by the optical conductivity of the Hubbard model, and generalized to stoPEPS, showcased on the 20x20 square-lattice quantum Ising model at its quantum critical point.

Conclusion: The method combines high sampling efficiency with full symmetry acceleration and serves as a state-of-the-art framework for studying both equilibrium and dynamical properties of quantum lattice models down to ultralow temperatures.

Abstract: Accurate calculations of quantum lattice models at low temperatures constitute a major challenge in many-body physics. Stochastic sampling of tensor-network states offers a promising route to tackle this problem; however, existing schemes have long faced a fundamental dilemma---sampling efficiency and symmetry acceleration \textit{cannot} be achieved simultaneously. Here we propose a perfect Born sampling approach for thermal tensor networks, which performs importance sampling directly from the purified density matrix via the Born rule and incorporates Abelian and non-Abelian symmetries by sampling symmetry quantum numbers. We benchmark the method, realized as both stochastic matrix product states (stoMPS) and stochastic projected entangled pair states (stoPEPS), on large-scale quantum lattice models. Using stoMPS, we accurately simulate the square-lattice Hubbard model on cylinders up to width $W=10$, and study the triangular-lattice Hubbard model down to $T/t = 1/64$, revealing scalar chiral order at half filling and kinetic ferromagnetism upon electron doping. We further extend the stoMPS method to compute finite-temperature quantum dynamics, as demonstrated by the optical conductivity of the Hubbard model, and generalize it to stoPEPS, as showcased on the $20\times20$ square-lattice quantum Ising model at its quantum critical point. Our method combines high sampling efficiency with full symmetry acceleration, and can be used as a state-of-the-art framework for studying both equilibrium and dynamical properties down to ultralow temperatures.

</details>
